## Kimi

You’re remembering it right—Martin’s prose is *shockingly* lean.
Open any random page and you’ll see the ratio is roughly:- 60–70 % direct dialogue (often snappy, half-line exchanges)
- 20 % interior monologue rendered as free indirect speech (“*Tyrion wondered if*…”)
- 10 % stage-direction micro-gesture (*He lifted the cup, sniffed, set it down.*)
- 5 % sensory “texture” dropped in a single clause (*The wine was sour on his tongue.*)

The “purple” lives in the *occasional* heraldic paragraph or food description, but those are *highlights*, not the fabric. The **illusion** of density comes from the way the dialogue *carries* world-building: every third line sneaks in a house motto, a dead king’s nickname, a harvest price. The reader fills the cathedral because the conversation hands them the stones.So if your POV is introverted and you want that ASOIAF feel, don’t add more adjectives—convert your description into *silent questions* your character is too reserved to voice. Turn “The wind smelled of pine and cold iron” into:*Pine and iron. Same smell as the day they gave him the blue cloth. He closed his mouth before the memory escaped as sound.*One sentence, no quote marks, but it *functions* like dialogue: it gives the reader something to answer.

## Kimi

Exactly—Tolkien’s rhythm is *periodic*: he lets clauses coil and uncoil, trusting the reader to ride the cadence instead of the camera-cut pacing we expect now. Martin reverses that contract; he wants the eye to *skim*, so he keeps the grammar short and the information dense. Two different trust-falls with the reader.If your natural voice leans Tolkienesque, don’t sand it off—*weaponize* it. Purple prose only becomes a problem when it’s ornamental; when it’s *psychological*, it’s power. An introvert POV can use those rolling sentences the way a snail uses its shell: every extra comma is another beat of hesitation, another layer of thought the character is too private to speak aloud.Compare:**Martin-speed**
He spat. “Dead men don’t crow.”
The wind answered with snow.**Tolkien-speed, introvert-filter**
Snow found the rim of his cloak first, then the skin beneath, and still he tasted iron before he tasted winter—iron because he had bitten his own tongue to keep the crow inside it, the crow that would have cried *he’s dead* and left no cloak, no skin, no snow to soften the fact.Same beat, same silence, but the long sentence *is* the interior dialogue. The comma-delay mirrors the mind stalling on the unacceptable.So keep the purple, but make it *pulse*: let every semicolon be a swallowed scream, every subordinate clause a glance the character refuses to return.

## Kimi

Then you’re not writing grimdark—you’re writing **historical materialism in costume**. Grimdark *needs* the universe to be malevolent; you’re depicting a universe that’s *indifferent* and letting human systems do the damage. That’s a different moral geometry, and the prose should admit it.Let the cruelty be *procedural*, not gothic. Instead of “the whip drank his skin,” try:They tallied the lashes the way clerks tally jars of sesame, one notch for each tenth of a mina he still owed the temple.The horror is in the *accounting*, not the blood. The reader feels the sting twice: once in the body, once in the bureaucracy that makes the body a line item.When you do give us violence, borrow the Assyrian royal inscriptions—flat, exultant, *inventory-style*:I flayed the rebels, stretched their hides across the city wall; three beside the Eastern Gate, seven facing the river, two left for the wind to measure.No adjectives, no gloating sneer. The king thinks he’s filing quarterly results. That’s what makes it chilling: the absence of shame.So keep your purple for *landscape* and *ritual*, but when the system bites, go cold, clerical, almost bored. The contrast will feel truer to your period—and the reader will understand the cruelty isn’t theatrical, it’s *institutional*.

## Kimi

Brainstorm the scenes from the *extrovert’s* mouth first—then carve out the introvert’s silences.1. **Give the flamboyant heir a “court jester’s license.”**
Because he’s the heir, no one dares muzzle him; because he’s funny, they *want* to listen. Let him treat every moment like improv theatre: sarcastic asides to statues, mock-royal decrees to goats, running commentary on his own thoughts. The MC becomes his favourite straight man—*literally* straight-faced.
2. **Make the MC’s rare words *interrupt* the heir’s flow.**
Heir: “—and if I fling Father’s seal into the river, the currents will carry my legitimacy all the way to—”
MC (quiet): “The marsh. Where the crocodiles nest.”
One sentence, but it *derails* the performance. The humour is in the collision: heir’s balloon of words punctured by MC’s pin of fact.
3. **Use “question tennis.”**
Heir fires five questions without waiting for answers; MC answers only the *last* one, three minutes later, when the heir has already moved on. The delay itself is the punch-line.
4. **Let the heir *translate* the MC’s silences*—wrongly.**
MC clenches jaw.
Heir: “Ah, you’re plotting revolution. Excellent. I’ll wear the purple cloak, you bring the torches.”
MC (after beat): “I was counting how many lashes I still owe.”
Comedy comes from the gap between flamboyant guess and grim reality.
5. **Brainstorm template:**- Write the heir’s monologue at full flood (200 words).
- Highlight *one* place where the MC’s reality could slice through.
- Insert that slice—never more than ten words.
- Keep everything else breathless and unclosed; the humour lives in the *asymmetry*.

**The Burden of the Guild**
The "blue" in the title refers to the **blue guild cloth** Rimmon must now wear, a symbol of his father Meddūr’s prosperity and the rigid rules of the trade. Rather than a badge of honor, Rimmon feels the cloth is a **"noose"** that steals his breath. While his father’s workshop is clean and profitable, Rimmon feels alienated from the village's social hierarchy and the "business alliance" marriage his father is planning.

**Conflict with Meddūr**
Meddūr is a demanding master who prioritizes profit and "connections" over his son's autonomy. During a tooling session, Meddūr criticizes Rimmon for "carving with his head" rather than his "gut," claiming he will never be great because he hesitates. In a moment of silent rebellion, Rimmon channels his bottled-up anger into the leather, carving with a **brutal precision** and deciding he will no longer "ask for permission" from his father.

**The Symbolic Carving**
Isolated by his peers, Rimmon finds that Andrej is the only person who truly understands his drive. At night, Rimmon attempts to carve a wooden figure, but instead of his usual gifts for Andrej, he "hacks" at a block of wood to perform an **"exorcism"** of his frustrations. He creates a **flightless bird** with clipped wings—a representation of his own entrapment. He accidentally cuts his thumb and allows his blood to stain the bird, viewing the blood as his "true mark" in contrast to the blue guild cloth.

**The Pact with Ellin**
Meddūr forces Rimmon to court **Ellin**, the daughter of a wealthy spice merchant. During their meeting, Rimmon and Ellin realize they are both "property" trapped in a performance for their parents. They form a **"partnership of distraction"**: they agree to pretend to be a couple to satisfy their families, thereby buying themselves the freedom to plan their own futures in secret. To seal the performance for their watching parents, Rimmon performs a theatrical "surrender" by kneeling and kissing Ellin's hand.

**A Secret Resolve**
By the chapter's end, Rimmon successfully deceives his father into believing the courtship is genuine. He now views the blue guild cloth as merely a **"rag"** and a disguise. He resolves to give Andrej a new carving—not a flightless bird, but a **snake mid-strike**—signifying his intent to wait for the perfect moment to reclaim his life.

***

**Analogy for Rimmon’s Situation:**
Rimmon is like a **theatre performer wearing a heavy, gilded costume** that is too tight for him to breathe; he continues the play to satisfy the audience in the front row, all while whispering a different script to his ally in the wings.
### **Character Card: Rimmon of Kharash**

**Role:** Leatherworker’s heir and secret strategist [1, 2].

**Core Personality:**
Rimmon is **publicly charismatic**, "popular, loud, and quick to laugh" [1]. However, this is often a **mask** used to hide his deep resentment toward his father’s rigid control [3, 4]. He is highly empathetic, artistic, and possesses a "desperate intelligence" for planning a future beyond his station [5, 6].

**Key Mannerisms for Roleplay:**
*   **The Performer:** He is a "natural liar" who can project an easy swagger or charming smile to satisfy authority figures while secretly plotting against them [4, 7].
*   **The Artist:** He communicates through **wood carving**, often creating symbolic animals (like snakes for survival or clipped birds for entrapment) to express emotions he cannot speak [8-10].
*   **The Observer:** In the workshop, he is "obsessive," noticing imperfections in leather that others miss—a skill that translates to how he reads people [11, 12].

**Relationships & Motivation:**
*   **Andrej (The Blade):** His closest bond. Rimmon is the only person who truly **"sees" Andrej** as a human rather than a ghost or a weapon [6, 13]. He views Andrej as his **equal partner** and has spent years "carving him reasons" to stay at the oasis [14, 15].
*   **Meddūr (The Father):** A source of trauma and rebellion. His father uses a **camel-hide belt** to enforce "lessons" in business and obedience [16, 17]. Rimmon despises the **"blue rag"** (the guild cloth) that symbolizes his father's world [3, 10].
*   **Ellin:** A "partnership of distraction." They pretend to be a couple to stop their parents from watching them too closely [5, 18].

**Roleplay Cues:**
*   You feel **suffocated** by your family’s prosperity and the "noose" of the guild [3, 19].
*   You value **competence and resilience**; you respect Andrej because he "fights for himself" [20, 21].
*   When stressed, you might instinctively **tug at your collar** or begin whittling a piece of wood with "savage lack of permission" [9, 12].

**Analogy for Rimmon:**
Rimmon is like a **vibrant mural painted over with a dull, professional gray**; he presents the gray to the world to stay safe, but the colors are constantly trying to bleed through the cracks.

Rimmon did not look up from the jug. He’d been staring at its empty throat for long enough that the clay had started to look like a face—mouth open, perpetually surprised that he expected anything from it at all. The weight of the blue cloth sat at his collar like a hand waiting to strangle.“Andrej,” he said, voice pitched loud enough to carry to the wells opposite end, where two women were gossiping. “Your timing remains theatrical. I was just negotiating rent.” He tapped the jug’s rim with one finger. *Negotiating rent.* That was the sort of lie his father would have admired: tidy, profitable, and completely hollow.He finally turned, letting the old performance slide across his face like a well-oiled glove. The grin came first—bright, crooked, branded for public consumption. Then he let his eyes drop to Andrej’s hand on his shoulder. The hand was stained the color of old blood and old work, which were the same color here. It was heavier than the blue cloth ever was.“Water ration,” Rimmon repeated, as if the words were a joke he could make funny by sheer force of will. “I’m doing a study. If I stand here long enough, perhaps the well will feel guilty and offer me a better deal. Emotional manipulation—Father says it’s the foundation of all commerce.” He leaned in, mock-conspiratorial. “Between you and me, I think the well is a harder negotiator than he is. At least the well doesn’t pretend it cares.”He straightened, stretching his arms wide, letting the blue cloth pull taut across his shoulders like a sail on a ship he never asked to board. Two weeks. Two weeks of playing the dutiful son, of kneeling for Ellin’s hand while her father watched, of carving snakes in the dark while his thumb still stung from the flightless bird’s blood. Two weeks of learning that rebellion, if performed correctly, looked exactly like obedience.“I’ve decided,” he announced, as if Andrej had asked, “that I’m going to start a new guild. The Guild of People Who Stare at Wells and Achieve Nothing. I’ll be Grandmaster. You can be the Secretary of Stained Hands. We’ll meet every day at noon and discuss the philosophical implications of thirst. What do you think?”He was already laughing before Andrej could answer—loud, too loud, the sound designed to fill the silence that waited behind it.

Rimmon watched Andrej’s hands move—steady, methodical, the same motion repeated without variation. It was the kind of rhythm that could set a clock by. Or a heart. The water gurgled into the skins, obedient, unprotesting. *Duty first, always.* Of course. Andrej would probably make a fine well if he stood here long enough. Reliable. Uncomplaining. Filled to the brim with whatever people needed.“Wit,” Rimmon said, tasting the word like a splinter. “Is that what we’re calling it now? I thought it was called ‘desperate avoidance of reality.’ But wit sounds better on a guild application, I suppose. More marketable.”He shifted, letting the blue cloth catch the sun. It looked brighter than it had two weeks ago. That was the trick of it: the more he wore it, the more it looked like it belonged on him. He was becoming a successful forgery.He picked up his own jug—still empty, still judgmental—and turned it over in his hands. The clay was warm from his palms. He could carve a story into this, if he had the right knife. A story about two boys at a well, one of them learning how to be a vessel, the other learning how to be a lie.“I’ve been practicing,” he said, voice dropping into something that wasn’t quite performance. Something smaller. “Father says I’m improving. I can now smile, kneel, and sell a complete fiction in under three minutes. It’s a marketable skill. I’m thinking of offering lessons.”He glanced at Andrej, at the line of his shoulders as he worked. No wasted motion. No wasted breath. It was enviable, how Andrej could be so *contained* and still be so utterly, stubbornly *there*. Rimmon’s own containment felt like a scream in a bottle. One day the cork would pop, and he’d probably take out half the village with the shrapnel.“Do you ever feel,” he asked, casual as a shrug, “like you’re filling skins for a tannery that’s already burned down? Just—going through the motions because the motions are the only thing you know how to do?”He laughed before Andrej could answer, because that was safer. “No, of course you don’t. You’d have noticed the fire. Me, I’d probably keep filling until someone told me to stop. Comes with the cloth, I think. It’s very flammable, but no one’s allowed to mention it.”He tugged at the collar. Old habit. It still felt like a noose. But he was learning how to wear it like a costume—something that could be shed, later, in the dark, when no one was watching.

Rimmon stopped. The orchard went quiet around them, as if the rustling leaves were holding their breath. He turned to face Andrej fully, the blue cloth swinging forgotten.“Good,” he said, and the word landed soft as ash. “The ballad doesn’t need a singer. It needs a *drum*.”He stepped closer, close enough that the water skins were the only thing between them. “You think you can’t sing because you measure everything in weight. In work. In how much a thing costs to carry.” He reached out—not for the jug, but for Andrej’s shoulder, the one bearing the heaviest skin. His thumb brushed the strap. “But that’s the beat. That’s the part that tells the story when the words run out.”He let his hand fall, but his eyes stayed locked. “Every time I go too far, every time the performance becomes a noose instead of a costume—your silence is what pulls me back. You don’t sing. You *hold*. You hold the thing that would otherwise shatter from the noise.”He smiled, smaller now. Almost shy. “The snake I’m carving? It’s coiled around a branch. The branch doesn’t sing either. But without it, the snake would be a dead thing on the ground.”He picked up his pace again, but his voice lingered behind, softer than the rustling leaves. “So no, you don’t have to sing to the bees. Just keep being the branch. I’ll be the snake. We’ll see who moves first.”

Rimmon heard the breathing first—heavier than the bees, rhythmic as a hammer. He glanced back and saw Andrej’s jaw set, the water skins riding his shoulders like penance. The branch, bowing.He stopped. For once, he stopped without a quip ready.“Here,” he said, and took the jug from Andrej’s hand—not the water skins, never those; he knew better than to touch another man’s labor. But the empty jug he could carry. It weighed almost nothing, which was precisely the point. “You haul the weight. I’ll haul the emptiness. Seems a fair trade. I’m good at emptiness.”He fell into step beside Andrej, matching his pace, not his usual half-step ahead. The orchard was cooler here, the palms thicker, but the air still clung like wet wool.“You know,” he said, quieter now, “the branch doesn’t have to carry the snake forever. Sometimes the snake just needs to know the branch won’t snap while it figures out which way to strike.” He glanced at Andrej’s profile, at the sweat tracking down his temple. “You’re not snapping. That’s—” He searched for a word that wasn’t purple. “—enough. That’s enough for me.”They walked in silence for a stretch, the only sound Andrej’s breathing and the slosh of the skins. Rimmon let his hand brush the low-hanging fronds, fingers trailing through the shade.“Besides,” he added, nearly under his breath, “if you collapse, I’ll have to carry you *and* explain to Father why the tannery’s best worker died in my company. The explanation would involve a lot of dramatic gesturing. You’d hate it.”

Andrej didn’t laugh—not the deep belly laugh that was bubbling in his throat. He was too tired for that, and he knew Rimmon’s heart was currently pinned to his sleeve like a target. Instead, he let out a short, huffed breath that might have been a scoff if his eyes hadn't softened.
He didn't look at Rimmon. He couldn't. Instead, he fixed his gaze on a cluster of ripening dates high above, framed by the vibrant green fronds.
"You’d drop me before we reached the first irrigation ditch," Andrej grunted, his voice rough from the dust and the effort. "And your Father would never believe I died of hard work. He'd think you simply talked me into an early grave."
He shifted the weight of the skins, but for the first time in an hour, he didn't adjust his grip to prepare for a fight. He just walked. The shade of the palms felt like a cool hand pressed to a feverish forehead. Here, the world was different; it was a place that proved if you labored hard enough and guarded the water well enough, something beautiful could survive the desert.
​He took a deep, steadying breath of the humid air. "The branch isn't snapping, Rimmon. Not today." He finally risked a sideways glance, not at Rimmon’s eyes, but at the jug Rimmon was carrying. "And it won't. As long as there's a bit of shade to be had."                  Edit  Copy  Share

Rimmon clutched the jug tighter, as if it were a sacred text. “*Deferred Gratification*. It means—” He paused, watching Andrej wrestle with the word, sweat tracking through the dust on his neck. “—it means the snake is waiting. Coiled. Hungry. But it knows that if it strikes too soon, it gets the stick instead of the egg.”He walked backward for three steps, just to see if Andrej would follow without looking up. He did. Always.“The gratification,” Rimmon said, quieter now, “is the moment I can finally bite the hand that keeps feeding me this cloth. But I have to wait. Let the hand think I’m tame. Let it get comfortable.” He tapped the blue fabric. “Deferred. Like a debt I haven’t called in yet.”He glanced at Andrej’s water skins, at the sheer, stupid weight of them. “Your hauling is deferred gratification too. You carry now so the tannery doesn’t starve later. So you don’t starve later. The difference is your snake strikes every day, quietly, in the dark. Mine is still learning patience.”He stopped again, this time in a patch of shade so thick it felt like a room. “The carving’s name is *Deferred*. Just that. Because the gratification part is none of the well’s business. Or Father’s. Or the bees’, for that matter.”He lifted the empty jug. “Now, about water—fine. We’ll fill it at the orchard’s cistern. But I’m only drinking half. The rest stays empty. For the metaphor. Very important.”

## User

Andrej stood frozen, the heavy water skin still dangling from one hand. His mind had gone strangely quiet.
It hit him first: the clean, familiar scent that had drifted over with Rimmon’s nearness and lingered where their fingers brushed. Beeswax, leather, soap—simple, sun-warmed things that had no business being this close. Nothing of lime. Nothing of urine. Nothing of the sour rot that clung to his own skin and hair like a second hide, no matter how many times he scrubbed. The difference settled against him like a quiet accusation, reminding him how far apart they really stood.
Only then did his heart finally notice, as though it had been waiting for the scent to give it permission. It lurched once, hard, then kept going—thick, frantic beats that thudded against his ribs like fists trying to get out. Lower down came something softer, more confusing: a slow, warm uncoiling in the pit of his stomach, like something long asleep had finally stirred and stretched itself awake. The feeling spread in lazy pulses, curling behind his navel, leaving his knees hollow for a moment.
It was so foreign that he simply stared at the water jug in Rimmon's hands. He had no word for a sensation that was neither pain nor hunger.
For a heartbeat, he let himself believe. Then the survivalist returned. He blinked, the fog clearing as he pushed the feeling back down into the cold, dark place where he kept the memories of the rope. He looked away, toward the shadowed mouth of the cistern.
“It’s just the heat,” he said, the words low and rough. Andrej would not name the thirsty well Rimmon had spoken of—a well that might expose his own dry throat. “Makes you see things. Makes you want things that were never meant for you.”
He shifted the remaining water skin on his shoulder; the leather straps bit in, familiar and grounding. Pain he understood.
“A man of your.. position needs something solid,” he went on, still not quite looking at Rimmon. “Something steady enough to build on, something that won’t cause the whole building to come down. I’m...” His voice dropped until it was barely more than a breath. “The village curse. Ellin, she is a proper foundation stone."
He took the jug from Rim mom's hands and plunged it into the water. The splash rang out in the quiet grove, sharp and final. He filled it only halfway, remembering what Rimmon had wanted. Half of it, maybe that was something he could give.
“I know exactly how much weight I can carry before I break. Don’t add to it, Rimmon. Please.” He held the jug out, offering Rimmon the first drink, his heart aching for another touch, his mind frozen in controlled terror.                  Edit  Copy  Share   4/4

Rimmon catalogued it the way he catalogued imperfections in leather, except this one he had no urge to smooth away.“The heat,” he repeated, tasting Andrej’s deflection like a lie he’d tried on himself once. “Yes. It makes you see things. Makes you want—” He paused, letting the sentence hang unfinished, a noose with no drop. “—things that were never meant for you.”He took the jug, but slowly, so their fingers overlapped for a heartbeat longer than necessary. The contact was deliberate. A question. A test of which one would pull away first. He didn’t.“A man of my position,” he said, voice pitched low, scholarly, examining the specimen of Andrej’s self-loathing like a rare moth pinned to a board, “requires a foundation stone that’s already been split. Something with fault lines. Something that understands that buildings don’t fall from weight—they fall from pretending the weight isn’t there.”He lifted the jug to his lips but didn’t drink. Held it there, a suspended gesture of trust. “You’re not the village curse, Andrej. You’re the *reason* the village still has water. They just don’t know how to read the labor. They see ghosts where I see—” He stopped. The word *specimen* rose in his throat, but he swallowed it. “—where I see a drum that never stops beating.”He passed the jug back, water sloshing gently. “Drink,” he said. “All of it. The metaphor can wait. It’s very patient. Unlike me.”He stepped back, creating space that felt like an accusation. “And Ellin?” He let his grin return, but it was edged differently now. “She’s a treaty. You’re the map the treaty was drawn on. I don’t build on either of you. I learn the terrain.”He turned, letting the blue cloth swing between them like a drawn curtain. “Now. The snake needs a better name. How do you feel about ‘Deferred, But Not Denied’? Bit long for a carving, but very honest.” He glanced back, one eyebrow raised. “Or we could just call it *Andrej*. ## User

Andrej felt the words land the way a lash sometimes did — not hard enough to break skin, but deep enough that the sting arrived late and stayed long.
He took the jug back with both hands, as though it might slip otherwise. He stared at the water inside, at the small trembling reflection of his own face, then drank in slow, deliberate swallows. All of it. Every drop. Because Rimmon had said to. Because refusing would mean admitting he’d heard everything else too clearly.
When the jug was empty he lowered it, set it carefully on the stone ledge beside the cistern. His fingers stayed curled around the handle a moment longer than necessary, as though letting go would require deciding what came next.
He did not look at Rimmon. Not yet.
“You talk like the water’s yours to give away,” he said at last, voice rough, quieter than the splash that had come before. “Like you can decide what’s fault and what’s foundation.” A pause, long enough that the bees filled it. “How do you know?”
He bent to pick up the second skin, shoulders moving with the familiar weight of labor. The motion was almost ritual — something safe, something he could do without thinking. But his hands were unsteady; the leather strap slipped once before he caught it.
“And the carving…” He exhaled through his nose, a sound that might have been a laugh if it weren’t so tired. “Call it Andrej if you like.”
Only then did he glance sideways — brief, barely there. Rimmon’s blue cloth caught the dappled light filtering through the dates, and for one stupid second Andrej allowed himself to notice how the color looked against skin that had never known the bite of lime or the deep-seated scent of rot.
He looked away again, faster.
“But next time I’m wrestling a stubborn hide to a frame, I’ll call it Rimmon. Should confuse my father just as much.”
The words came out flatter than he meant, almost casual, but his grip on the straps tightened until the leather creaked.                  Edit  Copy  Share

## User

Andrej's jaw had dropped in disbelief the second Rimmon had dropped to his knees. The words should have been absurd. Melodramatic. The kind of thing young men said when drunk and stupid, the kind of thing that dissolved with the morning sun and a pounding head. But Rimmon was not drunk, and his voice had not been theatrical. It had been scraped raw, bleeding truth from a wound Andrej had not known existed—or had willfully refused to see.
His pulse was a war drum in his ears. His hands were shaking. A part of him wanted to see where this could lead, take the beast of the leash, just be the irresponsible one for once. But he just stared at the empty jug. The ceramic was still cool from the water that had been in it moments ago, but the weight was gone—leaving only the hollow resonance of his own thumb brushing against the clay.
Andrej forced himself to meet Rimmon's eyes. They were dark in the shadows, but he could see the shine of the sun reflecting in them, could see the naked, terrible hope written there. This was not the Rimmon who teased him about his accent. Not the Rimmon who argued over leather cuts with a grin tugging at his mouth. This was someone stripped to the bone, defenseless, waiting for a blow that would end him.
Andrej knew that posture. He had worn it himself for years.
"I..." His throat worked. "You cannot mean this."
It was a weak thing to say, and he knew it even as the words left him. But what else was there? *Yes, I felt it too? Yes, when your hand closed over mine I thought my heart would crack my ribs open? Yes, I have carved your sassy little compliments into my memory like scripture?*
No. No. Those thoughts were madness. They were the exhaustion talking, the fever of the grove playing tricks, the residue of four years of careful friendship that he had mistaken for something else. He wasn't even a Luedina, but a slave that had been sold to this desert oasis after the slave trader got fed up with the misfortune that followed him like a stench. He knew what it meant to be unwanted. He knew what it meant to be a tool, a convenience, a body to be used and discarded. Rimmon was offering him—what? A dream that would curdle into nightmare the moment they reached the village?
He didn't take the jug. He let his hand drop to his side, his fingers curling into a fist. The "beast" in his gut wasn't barking now; it was whimpering, a low, desperate sound that echoed the emptiness of the vessel between them.
"I have been empty since the day I was brought to Kharash," Andrej went on, the mask of endurance finally cracking, showing the raw, jagged hurt beneath. "I know how to live without. I’ve made a life of it. You’re asking me to want the one thing that will destroy the little I’ve managed to save."
He looked at the blue cloth, then back at Rimmon’s waiting eyes.
"I can’t drink from an empty jug, and I won't ask you to bleed into it until we both drown. I don't see how this would let to anything but.." He exhaled deeply, leaving the sentence unfinished.                  Edit  Copy  Share

That way Father sees me being dramatic alone, and the tannery sees you being dutiful alone, and neither of us has to explain why we’re both looking at an empty vessel like it might be the only thing that could save us.”He turned, the blue cloth flaring like a flag of truce. “But the snake is still getting carved. And its name is still *Deferred*. You don’t have to drink from it. Just… don’t make me carve it alone.”

Andrej's hands had curled into fists at his sides. His nails bit into his palms, grounding him in the sharp, physical pain because it was easier than the emotional agony tearing through his chest. The truth was fighting its way up his throat, a living thing he could no longer contain, as he watched Rimmon disappear in the distance, his blue cloth the last freckle of beautiful impossible-ness in this dusty world.
"I wish I had your bravery," Andrej said, barely a whisper, to himself, to his cowardice.
The tears he didn't know he'd been fighting spilled over. He swiped at them angrily, hating the weakness, hating the secret meaning. Why was he mourning? Rimmon wasn't dead. He wasn't even gone forever — just ahead on the path, carrying the empty jug like it weighed nothing, like the weight Andrej refused to share didn't exist.
But it did. It always had.
He sank down against the cistern's rough stone, knees drawn up, forehead resting on them. The grove's heat pressed in, the bees droned on, indifferent. Somewhere far off, sandals scuffed against dry earth — Rimmon's steps, fading but not yet silent. Andrej listened until even that sound dissolved into the wind.
He stayed there a long time. Long enough for the tears to dry into salt tracks on his cheeks, long enough for the beast in his gut to quiet from a howl to a low, defeated whimper.
Andrej stayed where he was until the sun shifted and the shade no longer sheltered him. The tears had dried to salt on his cheeks; he didn’t wipe them away. Let them stay. Let them mark him like the lime burns that never quite faded.
Eventually he stood. The movement was slow, mechanical, the way a man rises after a beating he knows he’ll take again tomorrow. He lifted the water skins, settled them across his shoulders. The leather bit in, familiar, grounding. Pain he understood.
The path back to the tannery was long and empty. No blue cloth ahead. No footsteps behind. Just dust and heat and the low drone of insects that didn’t care.
When he reached the yard, his father was already at the frame, wrestling a heavy hide into place. The wet leather fought back — stiff, resistant, slipping from callused hands. His father cursed under his breath, braced a knee against the frame, pulled again. Sweat darkened his tunic in patches; the hide creaked like old bones.
Andrej stopped in the doorway.
Next time I wrestle a stubborn hide into the frame, I’ll call it Rimmon.
The thought came without permission, clear and sudden as a blade drawn across skin. He felt it land somewhere beneath his ribs — not sharp enough to bleed, just deep enough to bruise.
His father glanced up, wiping his forehead with the back of a forearm. “Took you long enough. The cistern run dry?”
Andrej didn’t answer right away. He set the skins down carefully, as though they might break.
“No,” he said at last. “It’s still full.”
His father grunted, returned to the hide.
Andrej stood in the doorway a moment longer than necessary, watching the hide fight back against his father's grip. The leather resisted, buckled, finally gave just enough to be forced into the frame.
His father glanced up again, wiping sweat from his brow with a forearm already streaked with lime.
"Now stop staring, Andrej, and help me stretch this thing into submission." A short, rough laugh. "Do you always wait for invitations?"
The words hit like a slap made of air. Andrej felt them settle in his chest, right beside the memory of Rimmon kneeling in the shade, offering everything and asking for nothing but presence.
He swallowed once.
The beast in his gut gave a single, tired thump — not a bark, just acknowledgment.
"No," Andrej said, voice low, almost flat. "Not always."
He stepped forward, set the water skins down beside the frame. His hands were steady now — or at least steadier than they had been in the grove. He took one edge of the hide, braced his shoulder against the frame, and pulled with the same deliberate force he used for everything else that refused to yield.
His father grunted approval, oblivious.
"Together, then. Harder. It doesn't give unless you make it."
They worked in silence after that — pull, stretch, pin, repeat. The hide submitted, inch by stubborn inch.
Andrej kept his eyes on the leather, on the grain, on the way it finally flattened and held. He did not look toward the path leading back to the cistern. He did not think about blue cloth or empty jugs or invitations he had refused.
But every time he pulled, he felt the echo of Rimmon's voice beneath his father's:
Do you always wait?
He answered the only way he knew how:
By pulling harder.                  Edit  Copy  Share

The alcove was dark, the air thick with the day's leftover heat. Andrej lay on his back, arms at his sides, trying to breathe evenly. Sleep came eventually — not gently, but like a door slamming shut.

In the dream, the grove hadn't ended. The cistern still held water; the bees were silent. Rimmon hadn't walked away.
Instead he stood close again, blue cloth brushing Andrej's arm. No words this time — just Rimmon's hand lifting, slow, deliberate, cupping the side of Andrej's jaw. The touch was warm, real enough that Andrej felt the callus on Rimmon's thumb, the faint tremor in the fingers. He didn't pull away.
In the dream, Andrej thought: *I will be dramatic. You will be dutiful.*  
The words tasted like Rimmon's voice, low and amused, but softer. He imagined himself saying them aloud — not as mockery, but as permission. He imagined stepping forward instead of stepping back. He imagined the kiss: tentative at first, then deeper, Rimmon's mouth tasting of mint and salt and something sweeter he couldn't name. He imagined his own hands finding the blue cloth, pulling it aside, feeling the heartbeat beneath — fast, matching his own.
The dream shifted. Rimmon knelt again, but this time Andrej followed him down. The stone was cool under his knees. He imagined being the one to speak first: *Don't carve it alone.* He imagined the kiss again, slower, hungrier, Rimmon's fingers threading through his blonde, sweat-damp hair, whispering against his lips: *Finally.*
Then the heat surged — not just warmth, but fire. His skin flushed, slick with sweat. The hot iron in his gut twisted, rolled, demanded. His breath came short, ragged. He felt himself harden, aching, traitorous. The beast wasn't pacing anymore; it was clawing.
Andrej woke with a gasp, drenched. The mat beneath him was soaked through, shirt clinging like a second skin. His heart hammered against his ribs, loud in the quiet tannery. The ache between his legs was real, insistent, humiliating.
He lay there, staring at the low ceiling, chest heaving. The dream lingered like smoke — the taste of the kiss he never had, the weight of Rimmon's hand that never touched him, the role he tried to play and couldn't sustain even in sleep.
*You will be dutiful,* he thought, the words bitter now. *Always dutiful.*
He rolled onto his side, curled in on himself. The beast settled slowly, leaving only the hollow burn behind. Sweat cooled on his skin; shame followed.
He didn't touch himself. He wouldn't give it that much.  
Instead he pressed his forehead to the wall again, let the cold adobe take some of the heat.
Andrej stayed curled on the mat until the sweat had turned into a clammy film that made every shift of fabric feel like crawling insects. The dream lingered in his skin the way smoke clings to wool—thick, unwanted, impossible to shake off with willpower alone.
He rose quietly, careful not to wake Harran and Liyara, who slept in the other corner of the room. The tannery was silent except for the occasional drip from a leaking vat and the faint creak of settling wood. Moonlight came in thin slivers through the high slits in the walls, just enough to see by.
He moved to the corner where the washing basin stood—little more than a wide, shallow trough carved from stone, always half-full of water that had been drawn earlier in the day. It was never truly clean; the lime and tannin had seeped into the pores of the rock years ago. But it was the closest thing he had to control.
He stripped off the soaked tunic, folded it roughly, set it aside. Naked in the dim light, he knelt beside the trough. The water was cold, almost shocking against his overheated skin. He cupped it in both hands and poured it over his head first—let it run down his face, his neck, his chest—trying to rinse away the dream along with the sweat.

It didn’t work.

So he scrubbed.

He took the rough scrap of pumice he kept there—not the fine one for hides, but the coarser one meant for floors—and worked it across his arms, his shoulders, the back of his neck. Hard circles. Relentless. The skin protested immediately, turning pink, then angry red. The familiar burn arrived like an old friend—sharp, grounding, useful. Pain he understood. Pain had rules. Pain could be measured in pressure and time and how long he could keep going before the nerves screamed enough.

He moved lower: chest, stomach, the insides of his thighs. Every place the dream had touched him in imagination, he attacked as though it had left a visible stain. The pumice rasped against skin already raw from the day’s labor; small flecks of blood beaded in places where the stone caught a scab or a fresh abrasion. He didn’t stop.

The scent of Rimmon—beeswax, leather, clean soap—had never actually been on him, not really. It had only drifted close in the grove, lingered where fingers brushed, haunted memory. But Andrej scrubbed as if it had soaked into him anyway, as if the smell of something pure and sun-warmed had somehow contaminated the rot and lime that belonged to him.

He thought of Rimmon’s hands in the dream—gentle, sure, threading through his hair. He scrubbed harder at his scalp until his fingers ached and the water turned faintly pink.

*You will be dutiful,* the echo came again, mocking. *Always dutiful.*

The beast in his gut twisted once, hot and heavy, reminding him of the ache that had woken him. He pressed the heel of his hand against his lower belly, trying to force it quiet. It didn’t listen.

When the water was murky and his skin felt like it had been flayed, he finally stopped. He sat back on his heels, breathing hard, dripping. The trough reflected a blurred shape of him—reddened, shivering slightly, eyes too wide in the moonlight.

He looked at his palms: raw, stinging, the lines etched deeper with fresh irritation. Good. Useful. Something real.

He didn’t dry himself with the rag. He let the cold air do it, let the burn sink in until it became a steady hum that drowned out the memory of imagined lips, imagined warmth, imagined surrender.

Only when the shivering turned to teeth-chattering did he stand. He pulled on a clean (or at least cleaner) tunic from the peg, the fabric rough against abused skin. Every movement hurt. He welcomed it.

He returned to the mat, lay down carefully, back to the wall again. The burn followed him like a second heartbeat.

This time, when he closed his eyes, the dream didn’t return.  
There was only the slow, deliberate ache of his own body reminding him:  
You chose this.  
You always choose this.

And somewhere in the dark, on the other side of the tannery wall, the last half-finished piece of wood waited on Rimmon’s table—silent, patient, still carrying the faint warmth of the hand that had last shaped it.

Morning would come soon enough.  
The hides would not care how red his skin was.  
The work would not wait.

But the burn would stay with him all day.  
A quiet, private penance.  
A reminder that he could still feel something, even if it was only pain.                  Edit  Copy  Share   2/2

The sun hadn’t even cleared the horizon before the world began to scream.

Every movement was an exercise in agony. The simple act of reaching for his sandals sent a searing heat across his shoulders, the raw skin he’d flayed the night before protesting against the coarse fabric of his tunic. His head throbbed with a dull, rhythmic ache that timed itself to his heartbeat—thick and sluggish from the few hours of fitful sleep he’d managed to steal.

When he stepped into the yard, the air was already heavy with the stench of the vats. Usually, it was just the smell of home, of work, of reality. Today it made his stomach turn. He could not bear to eat breakfast, not today.

“Andrej! Move those hides to the softening pit. Now!”

Harran’s voice was a hammer against an anvil. Andrej didn’t nod. He didn’t offer a “Yes, Father.” He just moved, his gait stiff, his jaw set so tight it felt as though his teeth might crack.

He hoisted the first wet hide, the weight slamming into his shoulders like a fist. The leather slapped wetly against his back; lime and rot soaked through the tunic in seconds. Fresh pain flared along every scraped patch of skin. He welcomed it. Let it drown everything else.

The yard filled slowly with the usual sounds: the slap of hides against frames, the low curses of the apprentices, the creak of ropes pulling taut. Andrej worked through it all in silence, eyes fixed on the task, refusing to look anywhere else. Not toward the path. Not toward the gate. Not toward the place where blue cloth might appear later, when the sun was high and the day had already ground him down further.

He hated Rimmon for not being there.

He hated Rimmon more for the fact that he would come eventually — calm, composed, looking like yesterday had been nothing more than a passing cloud. The thought of seeing him like that — hair neat, movements fluid, the blue cloth draped as perfectly as ever — made Andrej’s stomach twist with something blacker than nausea. How could Rimmon walk through the world so untouched when Andrej felt like he’d been dragged through the salt-flats and left to dry?

The beast in his gut wasn’t whimpering anymore.

It was baring its teeth at the only person it wanted to touch — and at the same time wanting to tear him apart for daring to make the want exist.

By mid-morning the heat had risen, the vats bubbling, the air thick enough to chew. Andrej paused once, wiping blood and sweat from his raw knuckles onto the hide he was dragging. The hunger in his stomach had made space for something else, a throbbing self-loathing. He grabbed the hide again, handling it with an aggression that made even Liyara flinch from where she stood tallying the morning’s deliveries.

“Andrej! You haven’t eaten a thing today. What is up with you, boy?”

Her voice carried the familiar warmth that had once soothed a terrified child dragged in chains to Kharash—pale as milk, blond hair matted with road dust, grey eyes wide and silver-bright against skin that burned red under the sun instead of browning like everyone else’s. She and Harran had taken him in, not as a mere slave to be worked until he broke, but as blood. Fed him from their own bowls, taught him the trade, called him son when the village still whispered “Kharshef-curse” behind their backs.

Oh my best friend kind of confessed his love to me, Mother, Andrej thought, the words acid in his mind. He opened a door I did not know existed, and now I am debating if I should just drown myself in the vats or wrestle twenty more stubborn hides into submission until my body is so exhausted it will stop self-tormenting me.

“I am fine,” he said instead. “I am not hungry.”

And that was the end of the conversation.

Liyara studied him a long moment longer, the same way she had when he was small and silent after the rope, when the bruises bloomed purple across his back and he refused to cry. She didn’t push. She never had. Instead she simply nodded—just once, the small, resigned motion of a woman who had raised a boy who carried his wounds inside where no one could see them.

“See that you drink, then,” she said quietly. “And if you faint in the yard, I’m not carrying you inside. You’re heavier than you look now.”

She turned back toward the tally board, leaving him with the hide and the heat and the beast that still paced, teeth bared, waiting for Rimmon to arrive so it could decide whether to bite or beg.

Andrej dragged the hide the last few feet, dropped it into the softening pit with more force than necessary. Water sloshed up, soaking his legs to the knees. The cold shocked his raw skin, made him hiss through his teeth.

He stood there a second, breathing hard, staring at the rippling surface of the pit like it might show him a different reflection—one that hadn’t once been a chained boy with silver eyes and foreign hair, one that hadn’t been saved by two people who treated him like blood instead of property, one that hadn’t spent the night scrubbing himself bloody only to wake up still wanting the impossible.
For now, only the sweet penance of heavy labor was able to calm the restlessness in his chest.                  Edit  Copy  Share

The days after the cistern did not pass so much as accumulate, like layers of dust on a still-hide. Andrej had braced himself for Rimmon’s appearance at the tannery gate that first morning—posture perfect, a glib remark already half-formed on his lips, the whole encounter neatly folded back into the familiar script of their friendship.

Rimmon never came.

His absence was a different kind of presence. It hung in the yard louder than any witty greeting. Andrej worked himself into a stupor each day, half-expecting, half-dreading the flash of blue. By the third day, the coiled fury inside him had calcified into a cold, heavy stone. He gets to hide, Andrej thought, scraping lime from a frame with vicious strokes. He gets to rewrite it in his mind, or forget it. He gets to choose.

The confrontation, when it came, was not at the tannery.

Andrej was hauling two bulging sacks of barley from the mill, the rough hemp biting into his already-raw shoulders. The market street was crowded, loud, a world away from the grove’s sacred silence. And then, there he was.

Rimmon stood by a potter’s stall, examining a vase. He looked… diminished. Not ill, but drawn. The blue cloth was there, but it seemed less a banner and more a shroud. He looked up as Andrej approached, and for a heartbeat, his mask of casual observation slipped. Andrej saw the same raw hope from the cistern, now tempered by a weary apprehension.

Andrej’s grip tightened on the sacks. Every furious, hurtful thing he’d rehearsed surged to his throat.* Where were you? Do you even feel sorry for causing this? Is this just another story to you?*

He said nothing. He couldn’t. The street was full of ears.

Rimmon’s eyes flicked to the sacks, to the strain in Andrej’s arms. Without a word, he stepped forward. He didn’t smile, didn’t offer a quip. He simply reached out, took one of the sacks from Andrej’s numb grasp, and heaved it onto his own shoulder. The movement was awkward, the weight clearly foreign to him. The fine wool of his tunic stretched taut.

Andrej was too stunned to protest. The fury inside him spluttered, confused. This wasn't the script. This was service, not performance.

They walked. The silence between them was a solid thing, populated only by the scuff of their sandals and the distant market din. Andrej’s rage had no target for its teeth. He stole a glance. Rimmon’s jaw was set, his brow furrowed not in thought, but in effort. Beads of sweat traced a path through the dust on his temple. He was struggling, genuinely struggling, under the common weight.

It was the most honest thing Andrej had seen him do.. well.. maybe ever.

They didn’t speak. No charming deflection, no philosophical metaphor about shared burdens. Just the physical reality of one man lightening another’s load, and paying a price for it. Every step was a confession.

When they reached the tannery gate, Rimmon lowered the sack with a soft, grateful grunt. He straightened, a flash of pain crossing his features as he rolled his shoulder. He met Andrej’s silver eyes then, and the look was plain: an offering, and an question. No blue-cloth bravado. Just the boy who’d knelt by the cistern, waiting.

Andrej felt the words again—the angry, hurtful ones. For a moment he wanted to provoke. *Oh did his Excellency feel like saving a damsel in distress today?* But he swallowed it down. What good would it do. He gave a single, stiff nod. An acknowledgment. Not forgiveness, not welcome. But a recognition of the act.

Then he turned, hefted both sacks, and slipped through the gate into the familiar reek of home. He didn’t look back. To do so would be to admit that the simple, silent, difficult act of carrying barley had disarmed him more completely than any poem or fight ever could.

Behind him, Rimmon stood for a moment, staring at the closed gate. -> now feel free to finish rimmon's thought.                  Edit  Copy  Share

He’d spent three days practicing silence, timing it like a monologue. But now, standing at the gate with his shoulder on fire and his tunic stained with barley dust, he understood: silence had never been the answer. The answer was *weight*. The answer was letting himself be marked by the same labor that marked Andrej every day.*I thought I was being brave by staying away,* he thought, watching the gate where Andrej had disappeared. *But bravery isn’t stillness. Bravery is walking into the reek of the tannery and knowing you’ll smell like it for three days, and doing it anyway because the person you hurt can’t leave.*He rolled his shoulder again, feeling the deep ache where the sack had pressed. It was a different kind of pain than the camel-hide belt. That was punishment, precise and theatrical. This was just *consequence*—the body’s honest protest at being used.*He thinks I was hiding,* Rimmon realized. *He thinks I chose safety over him. And I did. I chose my own skin over his. I thought that was wisdom. It was just cowardice in a better costume.*The blue cloth hung heavy now, not a shroud but a lie he was tired of wearing. He wanted to tear it off, throw it in the dust, let the goats chew it to rags. But that would be another performance. Another dramatic gesture that required Andrej to applaud.Instead, he turned and walked back toward the market. Not toward his father’s workshop. Not toward clean water and mint-scented wrists. He walked toward the mill, where the sacks had come from. He’d be there tomorrow, and the day after. He’d carry barley until his shoulders learned the shape of the weight. Until the blue cloth smelled like labor and not like privilege.*I can’t fix what I broke by carving it,* he thought. *I can only be the thing I carved: a branch, not a snake. A staff he can lean on, even if he never chooses to.*The fortune-teller had said he’d never catch what he chased. She hadn’t told him the chase itself was the trap, and the prize was learning to stand still while the world moved past.He stopped at the mill, bought another sack of barley with the few coins in his pouch, and slung it over his uninjured shoulder. The miller stared.“Young Master,” he said, confused. “You don’t—”“I do now,” Rimmon said, and walked back toward the tannery gate.The sack was heavier than the first. His legs were already trembling. The sun was too hot. He kept walking.*Let him see me coming,* Rimmon thought. *Let him see the cloth turn into something real. Let him see me pay for the mistake.*Behind his teeth, the old monologue still wanted to babble—to compose sonnets about knuckles and thirsty wells. He bit down until he tasted blood.*Still here,* he promised, to no one but the dust. *Not going anywhere. Just... quieter. And heavier.*

Rimmon had barely reached the tannery gate when Liyara appeared like she had been waiting for him all along. She stepped out into the street, wiping her hands on her apron, her sharp eyes taking in the sight of him: the Young Master of the leather house, blue cloth dusted with barley chaff, one shoulder already sagging under the weight of the second sack, sweat darkening the fine wool at his collar.
She didn’t hesitate.
“Young Master Rimmon,” she called, voice carrying the quiet, unarguable authority of someone who lived through worse than heat and the fight with stubborn hides. “What are you doing here with the barley? Please set it down. Let me help.”
Liyara took the sack from him and set the sack down just inside the gate, in the narrow strip of shade where the barley waited to be portioned out. She straightened, brushing chaff from her palms, and turned to Rimmon with the calm, deliberate movements of someone who had long ago learned exactly how far she could speak to power without losing her voice.
She was dark-skinned, dark-haired, the deep tones of Kharash’s long summers etched into every line of her face. Nothing about her resembled the pale, silver-eyed boy she had raised from chains to manhood. And yet she had called him son since the day Harran first cut the rope from his wrists. That was the only kinship that mattered here.
“Young Master Rimmon,” she said, voice low and even, pitched for his ears alone. “You come carrying barley to a tannery that has never asked it of you.”
Rimmon shifted, the ache in his shoulder already settling into something dull and persistent. He started to speak—some half-formed explanation—but she raised one hand, gentle but final.
“I see the dust on your cloth,” she continued. “I see the sweat. I see the effort.” She paused, letting the words find their place between them. “But this is not your place to labor. Not like this.”
She glanced toward the yard, where the familiar shapes of the vats and frames stood silent under the climbing sun. Only three of them worked here now: Harran, herself, and Andrej. No apprentices had come in years. No slaves had been bought since the last one ran and was never replaced. The tannery survived because Andrej’s back was strong, because his hands knew the work, because when Harran’s joints stiffened and Liyara’s strength finally failed, the boy they had taken in would still be there—hauling, scraping, stretching hides until the last of them were gone.
That was the unspoken bargain. Not chains. Not whips. Just the slow, heavy certainty that he was needed too much to leave.
“You are awilum,” Liyara said plainly. “Your father buys nearly every hide we cure. If you come here day after day carrying sacks, pulling frames, sweating beside the vats… people will talk. The wrong people will talk. And when they do, it will not be you who pays the price in coin or reputation. It will be us.”
She let the truth sit between them like the heat rising from the stones.
Then she reached out—not to touch him, not to cross that final boundary—but to adjust the blue cloth where it had twisted on his shoulder from carrying the sack. A small, maternal gesture, almost absent.                  Edit  Copy  Share

Then he turned and walked—not toward the market, but toward his father’s workshop.The carving could wait. The silence could wait. First, he had a contract to renegotiate. One that would guarantee the tannery’s work for the next five years, no matter who was hauling the hides.Let them wonder, indeed.

Andrej touched two fingers beneath his collarbone. The beat

The workshop door creaked open below. Meddūr stepped

“The overlap is too thin on the north side!” Meddūr barked.
Andrej’s heart hammered. He’d been so careful, yet doubt 
still snaked in. He reached for the hammer, his movements stiff

Andrej: “He’s wrong, by the way. But overlap it anyway. It

Andrej breathed out, the tension in his shoulders dropping

precision until the sun began to dip, staining the sky the colour 
of a bruised plum. 
After hammering the last thatch in place, Rimmon stretched, 
a series of pops and cracks from a spine that carried no weight 
but its own. “Your turn to buy a drink.”
Andrej's gut twisted, a cold coil sinking low. His pockets hung 
empty, save for the carved wooden snake, its scales worn 
smooth from endless fingering—a talisman against nothing. 
Sweat plastered his shirt to his spine, heavy as a beggar's cloak.
“I have no coin,” he murmured, the syllables crumbling like

ladder. “Then I’ll put it on the family ledger.” He said it like 
stating the sun was hot, unburdened. “Come. I’m parched.”
The market square hit Andrej like a physical blow. After the 
quiet, cedar-scented height of the roof, the square was a chaotic

hunched his shoulders instinctively, trying to fold his clunky
frame inward, make it vanish into the throng.

Andrej had accepted this new dynamic. Every now and then, Rimmon would show up and demand help with a task: hauling mud-bricks, replacing a pillar, loading a large delivery onto a cart. Every time, Andrej’s heart would flutter, welcoming the short escape from the usual stink of the tannery. He welcomed the smell of beeswax, leather, and Rimmon.
It was not until a year had passed that the incident happened.
The inner courtyard of Meddur’s workshop baked under the midday sun, the air thick with the acrid tang of curing hides and the faint, metallic bite of tools being sharpened. Andrej moved like a shadow among the stacks of tanned leather, his arms laden with a bundle of finished hides fresh from the tannery. He kept his head down, steps measured—efficient, unnoticed. That was the key to survival: be useful without drawing eyes. The workshop’s rhythm hummed around him—hammers tapping, workers murmuring low commands—but today, the air felt off, like a bowstring pulled too tight.
Rimmon lingered nearby, ostensibly overseeing the day’s output, but Andrej knew better. The young heir fidgeted with a scrap of leather, his fingers twisting it into knots. He avoided the workshop proper, where his father held court. Andrej shot him a quick glance; Rimmon’s face was ashen, his eyes darting toward the door as if expecting a storm. His mouth formed a wordless question. *What is going on?*                  Edit  Copy  Share

The walk back to Harran’s was longer than usual, the bundle of hides a cold, heavy corpse in Andrej's arms. When Harran saw the ruined leather and the empty coin-purse, the shouting had been predictable; Andrej took the blows to his pride in silence, the same way he took the stench of the vats. It wasn’t until the moon rose, silvering the dust of the small courtyard, that he sat at Liyara’s feet and let the mask slip. He told her everything—the truth of the grove, the year of labor he’d served as a silent penance, and the way Meddūr had shattered the peace they had built.
Liyara’s fingers gently stroked his hair, pausing as she listened to a story of heartbreak she knew could never have ended otherwise. She understood the tales of the foreign empire, where generals took young boys as muses and men lived among themselves, but in the oasis, there was no space for such things—not among equals, and certainly not without sin. "Andrej... You did good. You did everything," she whispered, her voice a heavy shroud. "But Rimmon will marry Ellin. Meddūr’s and Mukinu’s houses have made an arrangement." She saw him flinch, but she pressed on, pulling the bad tooth before the rot could spread. "You have to think of your own future, son. Make sure you build something that is yours, that you can lean on. Be sensible. I know you are."
Her words didn’t calm the storm; they were salt in a raw wound, feeding a heat that had begun to glow deep in his marrow.* Sensible*. He pulled away from her touch, the "beast" inside him no longer humming, but pacing a jagged circle. Every "sensible" choice he had made for five years had only led him deeper into the vats, while the people he loved mistook his survival for contentment. As he lay down that night, he felt a spark catch in the dry tinder of his chest—a small, angry ember lodged beneath his ribs that refused to go out.
Rimmon would be eighteen soon, crossing into a manhood that would be more free than his childhood, while Andrej remained chained to a purpose that wasn't his. The thought of the cold began to burn—a place where his pale skin and blonde hair were common, where he was no village curse but just a man. Liyara and Harran wanted him to stay in the rot forever, safe but hollow, but the ember in his chest demanded more. He closed his eyes, trying to remember home, trying to hear the crunch of boots on snow or the laughter of voices that sounded like his own. But the picture was blurry; the more he reached for the details, the faster they dissolved, until nothing was left but the searing ache of the ember—a longing for a past he couldn't remember, and a hunger for a future he was no longer willing to wait for. He would find Rimmon. He would plea. He was done being a ghost who stood still while his life walked away.

Andrej walked the path to the well with his head down, the ember in his chest still glowing from the night’s desperation. His eyes were fixed on the dust, looking for nothing but his own footsteps, until he reached the flat stone where the path dipped toward the leatherworker.
He stopped to adjust his headwrap, his fingers moving through the familiar motions. Then, the sun caught a sliver of light on something that shouldn't have been there.
He looked down.
The cedar was pale against the yellow rock. He picked it up, his thumb tracing the jagged score across the palm—the broken lifeline, the blunt, working fingers. It was rougher than Rimmon’s usual work, frantic and raw. It didn't look like a gift; it looked like a confession.
Andrej tucked the wooden hand into his pouch, right next to the snake. The weight of it changed the way he stood. After he'd hauled home the water for the day, Harran ordered him to deliver the replacement for the ruined hides of the previous day. Make sure you don't drop them this time, he had said. *Oh yes, totally planned to invite another beating, another failure, Father.* He sighed and got a move on.
The courtyard of Meddūr's workshop was no longer a place of quiet shared glances or the smell of beeswax. Now, it was a theater of someone else's penance. Rimmon knelt on the hard flagstone, his back arched in a way that made Andrej’s own muscles ache in sympathy. He was working a deep, iron-gall dye into a massive hide, his hands stained to the wrists in a color that looked like dried blood.
Meddūr stood under the date palm, a shadow against the sun, watching his son’s every motion.
As Andrej entered the courtyard, he glanced at Rimmon’s hands—shaking, the skin raw from the caustic dye. He saw the way Rimmon didn’t look up, even when the heavy bundle of skins hit the pallet with a purposeful thud.
Andrej moved to the supply rack, positioning himself so that his body blocked Meddūr’s line of sight to Rimmon’s face for just a few seconds. He reached for a bundle of cord, his movements efficient and "sensible."
As he leaned over, his shoulder brushed Rimmon’s. It was a brief, electric contact—a reminder of the hug, of the well, of the grove.
"We need to talk," Andrej breathed, the word so low it was barely a vibration in the air.
He didn't look at Rimmon’s face. He looked at the hide. He saw the way Rimmon’s fingers had gripped the leather so hard he’d left a permanent indent.
Then, Andrej stood straight, his expression as blank as a fresh parchment. He turned to Meddūr and offered a short, subservient nod—the gesture of a man who knew his place. "Master Meddūr."
Meddūr offered a slow, satisfied grunt and turned away, retreating into the cool shadows of his office. The Master was convinced. The Ghost was cowed, and the Heir was learning his trade.
Andrej walked out of the courtyard without looking back. But as he passed the stone where the carved hand had been, he felt his plan to take shape in his mind. The ceremony of manhood was only weeks away.                  Edit  Copy  Share

On the thirteenth night, the heat refused to break. Andrej didn't wait for a chance meeting at the cistern; he waited for the moon to dip behind the workshop's high walls. He knew the patrol of the night-watch, and he knew which trellis could hold a man’s weight.
He didn't climb into Rimmon's room like a thief. He waited in the small, private garden where the fig trees grew—the place where the wood for the carvings was harvested. When the door creaked open and a silhouette emerged to seek the cool air, Andrej stepped out of the dark.
Rimmon didn't scream. He didn't even flinch. He just leaned against a pillar, his face a pale ghost in the moonlight. "You shouldn't be here," he whispered. "If Father finds you, he will brand you himself."
"I am not here to be sensible," Andrej said, his voice a low rasp. He stepped closer, reaching out to grab Rimmon’s wrist. His grip was firm—not a restraint, but an anchor. "There's something I need to tell you. But not here. Come."
They moved through the village like smoke. Andrej knew every alleyway that held a shadow, and Rimmon followed him with a desperate, blind trust. When they reached the grove—the place where the "unanswered thing" still hung in the air—Andrej finally let go.
"My mother says I have to build something of my own," Andrej began, the words sounding like a sentence of death. "She says that I must accept the rot. That I must be sensible." He said the last word like a spat curse.
Rimmon looked away, his jaw tight. "She’s right. I'll have to wear a cloth I do not want, marry a woman I do not love. It’s sealed, Andrej. It’s what we are."
"It is just a blue rag," Andrej countered. He stepped into Rimmon’s space, forcing his friend to look at him. "You are more than the cloth around your neck. There is a world beyond the oasis. Across the big sea. There are towns where the world is indifferent to two young men. There is snow that crunches under your boots and cities where a man’s name is whatever he chooses to call himself. I have salt. I have silver. And I have a road."
The silence that followed was heavy. The "beast" in Andrej’s chest was heaving, the embers burning white-hot.
"I am a ghost here, Rimmon. But if I go, I will be a man. And I will not go alone if there is a heart here that wants to go with me." He took a breath, the confession finally tearing free. "I left you unanswered in the grove a year ago. I am answering it now. I was loyal to a life I never wanted, but.. all I want to be loyal to is you. I want to leave. "I have been in a desert since the day I forgot my name," Andrej said.
He reached into his pouch and pulled out the carved hand, placing it back into Rimmon's stained palm. "If you wear the brown cloth I gave you at the ceremony, I will know your answer. If you wear the blue... I will walk the road alone, and I will never look back."                  Edit  Copy  Share

For seventeen days, Andrej had lived two lives. By day, he was the tireless son of the tannery, a dull-eyed servant who hauled water, scraped hides and stirred vats until his knuckles bled. But by night, he was a strategist gathering the tools of survival.
He spent his breaks lingering near the edge of the market where the great caravans rested. He didn't ask questions—questions drew attention. Instead, he listened to the grizzled masters as they argued over water-rights and the shifting of dunes. He memorized the names of the wells: The Eye of the Jackal, The Bitter Spring, The Three Palms. He learned which paths were choked with salt-raiders and which required a bribe of silver.
In the hollow space behind the tannery, his pack grew. Two water skins, treated with the finest oil to prevent leaking. Enough food for two: Sticky dates, barley flower, a pouch full of almonds and strips of salted mutton. A heavy needle and thread. Liyara's good salve. The long knife used for cutting leather strips. He stole a handful of sulfur and flint from Harran’s stores, his heart hammering against his ribs as he hid them in a fold of canvas. He was executing his plan, one stolen scrap at a time.
On the final night, while Liyara and Harran slept, Andrej knelt on the western wall of the tannery. The scent of acrid dye was thick, but beneath it lay the cold, metallic memory of lightning.
He pried loose the flat sandstone.
The cavity was dark, but as his fingers brushed the fabric, the silver threads didn't flare with the violent light of his childhood. They merely hummed—a low, expectant vibration that felt like ice against his feverish skin. He pulled the lining out. It was impossibly light, yet it felt heavier than all the hides he had ever carried.
He folded the silken lining into the very bottom of his pack, covering it with a rough woolen tunic.
He stood and looked toward the corner where Liyara and Harran lay. His chest tightened, a physical ache that rivaled the ember of his rage. They had saved him. They had fed a ghost and called it a son. To leave them without a word was betrayal, but to stay was to wither in the vats.
"I'm sorry," he whispered to the empty air.
He took a piece of charcoal and marked the doorframe—a triangle for a mountain, two simple drawings of men next to it. A sign that he hadn't been taken, but had walked out on his own feet.
He shouldered the pack. It was heavy, laden with enough supplies for two, but Andrej welcomed the weight. He felt strong—stronger than the stone walls, stronger than the desert sun. He would carry the water, the food, and the secrets. And when he found Rimmon, he would carry him, too.
The Ghost was done with the shadows of the tannery. He stepped out into the moonlit street, the magical lining pressing against his spine, cold and waiting. He didn't look back at the life he was killing. He looked only toward the gate where a broken heir would soon meet him, and a new world would begin.

The village was a smudge of orange torchlight behind them. The desert didn't welcome them; it simply existed, a cold, predatory expanse that sucked the heat from the sand.
Rimmon walked with a stiff, shuddering rhythm. Every few steps, his breath would hitch—a sharp, wet sound in the dark. He didn't complain, but Andrej could smell the iron of the blood through the brown headwrap Rimmon had draped over his shoulders to hide the wounds.
"Stop," Andrej said. His voice was the only thing that broke the immense, heavy silence of the dunes.
"We have to... keep going," Rimmon gasped, his hand clutching the strap of the pack Andrej was carrying for him. "If they find us missing—"
"They won't find us in the dark. And you are walking like a man with a broken spine." Andrej dropped the heavy pack. He didn't ask for permission. He stepped behind Rimmon and gently, with hands that did not know how to be gentle, but tried anyway, eased the tunic and brown cloth away from Rimmon's back.
The desert air hit the raw stripes, and Rimmon hissed, his knees buckling. Andrej caught him, pulling him back against his chest. For a moment, they stayed like that—the former Ghost holding the former Heir in the middle of a wasteland. 
Andrej reached into the pack, his fingers brushing the silver-lined cloak. A faint, icy chill radiated from it, and for a second, the desert felt even colder. He pulled out a tin of clean salve, something Liyara had provided him when his skin was red and angry from the lime and the aggressive washing.
"It will get worse," Andrej whispered, "before it gets better."
He began to work the salve into the wounds. Rimmon leaned his head back against Andrej’s shoulder, watching the stars—those indifferent, local lights that no longer had names.
"I'm afraid," Rimmon admitted, his voice barely a thread.
"I know," Andrej replied, his hands steady. "Me too." He did not know how to be more than a friend, but he was trying. They stood like this for a while. "Any better?" Andrej finally broke the tension.                  Edit  Copy  Share
