The embers of the fire glowed like dying stars scattered across the sand, their light pulsing faintly against the encroaching dawn. The air hung heavy with the sharp tang of smoke and the dry, mineral bite of the desert night—cold enough to seep through Andrej's tunic and prickle his skin like invisible thorns. He hadn't slept, not really. Every time his eyelids drooped, the ghosts of the past months clawed him back: the tannery's acrid stench, the shouts in the courtyard, the moment they'd both chosen to flee into this vast, unforgiving emptiness. For... love. The word still twisted in his gut, unfamiliar and sharp, like swallowing a shard of glass. He shook his head, the motion stirring the fine grains of sand that clung to his hair, whispering against his scalp. The desert's silence judged him louder than any voice, an endless hush broken only by the occasional sigh of wind over the dunes.
Rimmon stirred then, a low, ragged whimper slipping from his lips before he could clamp them shut— the sound raw, like tearing cloth. His eyelids fluttered open, hazy at first, pupils dilating in the dim light until they locked onto Andrej, who sat with knees pulled tight to his chest, the carved wooden hand gripped in his fist like a talisman. Its smooth curves dug into his palm, a reminder of something solid in this shifting world.
“You stayed,” Rimmon murmured, his voice gravel-rough, scraped by thirst and the echoes of last night's fevered cries.
Andrej's throat tightened, a flush of warmth blooming unbidden in his chest. “I said I would.” The words came out steadier than he felt, but inside, his heart stumbled in a clumsy rhythm.
Rimmon pushed up onto one elbow, his face twisting in a grimace as the motion tugged at the still raw stripes across his back. A hiss escaped through his teeth, sharp and involuntary. The brown headwrap had slipped askew during the night, exposing a tangle of sweat-damp black curls. Without thinking— or maybe thinking too much, the hesitation a beat too long— Andrej reached over. His fingers brushed Rimmon's temple as he straightened the fabric, the skin there warm and slightly sticky with dried sweat. Salt lingered on his fingertips, a faint, earthy taste when he absently touched them to his lips later. The contact stretched, a second too long, Rimmon's pulse thudding faintly under Andrej's touch like a distant drum. Neither pulled away, but neither leaned in; it was a dance on the edge of a cliff, toes curling over the drop, hearts pounding with the what-if.
Andrej's mind raced: *What am I doing? This isn't... I shouldn't...* But the warmth spread, coiling low in his belly, awkward and insistent, like fumbling for a key in the dark.
“We should keep moving,” he said finally, his voice thicker than intended, breaking the spell. He dropped his hand, the absence of touch leaving his skin tingling. “The sun will be brutal soon.”
Rimmon nodded, his dark eyes flickering away, a faint flush creeping up his neck. He reached for the waterskin, his movements deliberate, careful not to jar his wounds. A shallow sip, throat working visibly, then he passed it over. Their fingers brushed— not accidental this time, the slide slow, deliberate, knuckles grazing in a way that sent a spark up Andrej's arm. Still, they avoided each other's gaze, eyes fixed on the sand, the horizon, anything but the pull between them. Andrej's breath caught, a clumsy hitch; he wanted to grasp, to hold, but fear held him back, circling like a vulture overhead.
The water was laced with that faint metallic tang— Rimmon's blood, from the split lip or the seeping back, he couldn't tell. It didn't repel him; if anything, it grounded him, a bitter reminder of the cost they'd both paid. He handed it back without a word, their fingers parting with reluctance, the air between them thick with unspoken things.
They packed in silence, the only sounds the soft rustle of fabric and the crunch of sand underfoot. Andrej folded the silver-lined cloak with reverent care, its metallic sheen catching the dawn light like a promise of shade later. He tucked it deep into the pack, the weight settling against his back like an old friend. The carved hand went into his pouch this time, nestling beside the snake.
Andrej shouldered the pack, the straps biting into his shoulders with familiar pressure. Rimmon reached out, fingers hovering near his shoulder as if to share the burden, but Andrej shook his head, a quick, firm motion. “Not yet. When the scabs close.”
Rimmon's mouth opened, protest forming on his lips— chapped and pale— then closed again. A small surrender, his eyes dropping, but Andrej caught the flicker of frustration, the way his jaw tightened.
They started walking. The wind picked up, whipping grains against their faces— tiny stings like bee pricks, leaving Andrej's cheeks raw and tingling. He set a steady pace, sandals sinking slightly into the soft give of the sand, muscles burning with the effort. His eyes scanned the horizon, searching for the low ridge the caravan masters had described: a jagged line against the sky, marking the salt flats' edge. The air warmed gradually, the chill retreating as the sun peeked over the dunes, painting everything in harsh, golden hues.
Rimmon walked beside him, not quite matching strides— a half-step behind, then catching up, their arms brushing occasionally in awkward sync. Every so often, he'd stumble, foot catching on a hidden dip, and Andrej would be there: arm slipping around his waist, firm but tentative, the heat of Rimmon's body seeping through his tunic. Steadying him without a word, but inside, Andrej's thoughts churned: *He's hurting more than he lets on. I should say something— but what? Don't make it worse.* The touches were brief, electric, gone before they could linger, leaving Andrej's skin buzzing with the ghost of contact.
After an hour— or was it longer? Time blurred in the endless march— Rimmon broke the silence. “Do you remember the first time we met?”
Andrej nodded, the memory flashing: the tannery yard thick with the reek of hides and chemicals, the ball sailing over the wall like a taunt. “You tossed that ball into the yard. Insulted me, then looked shocked when I fought back. Another split lip back then.” His voice carried a hint of amusement, but underneath, a warmth bloomed— clumsy, unpracticed.
“I didn't know what I was getting into.” Rimmon's tone was soft, laced with a wry edge, his breath coming in shallow puffs from the exertion.
“I didn’t either.” Andrej's mind wandered: That boy at the gate, all fire and privilege. Now here, broken but unbroken. *For me?*
They walked another dozen paces, the sand shifting underfoot with a soft hiss, the sun climbing higher, its rays prickling like hot needles on exposed skin.
“I’m still learning,” Rimmon said quietly, his voice almost lost in the wind.
Andrej glanced over, catching the vulnerability in his eyes— dark pools reflecting the desert's vastness. “So am I.” *Learning you, learning this— whatever it is. Afraid to name it, afraid to lose it.*
The sun ascended relentlessly, the heat swelling like a living thing, pressing down on their shoulders, drawing sweat that trickled down Andrej's back. Rimmon's steps faltered more, each one a careful placement, his breath ragged. Andrej slowed without a word, matching the pace, the silence between them comfortable yet charged— a circling, always circling.
At midday, they halted in the scant shade of a lone acacia, its twisted branches casting jagged patterns on the sand like skeletal fingers. The air hummed with the faint buzz of insects, the tree's leaves rustling faintly overhead. Andrej spread the silver cloak on the ground, its fabric cool and slick under his palms, a brief respite from the baking earth. Rimmon lowered himself face-down with a muffled groan, the sound vibrating through Andrej's chest like an echo.
As Rimmon's tunic lifted inch by inch, the sight hit Andrej like a blow: the fresh welts, barely scabbed over, jagged and angry red against the patchwork of old scars— silver lines crisscrossing his shoulder blades, some thick as twisted rope, others fine as spider silk. In the firelight last night, they'd been shadows; now, in the harsh noon glare, they were a map of endurance, each mark a story of defiance. Meddūr’s belt, Andrej knew— punishment for stolen hours at the tannery gate, for excuses woven to linger with him, for that final defense. Guilt surged, hot and acrid in his throat, like bile rising after a bad meal. *My fault. All this, because of me.*
His hand moved before his brain caught up, fingertips hovering, then brushing the nearest welt— raised and surprisingly cool, textured like braided leather under his touch. He traced another, light as a breath, the skin quivering faintly beneath. Rimmon went utterly still, his breath hitching, the tremor rippling through the scars into Andrej's fingers like a shared pulse.
“These… all from your father?” Andrej's voice was a whisper, barely cutting the heavy air.
A single, tight nod, Rimmon's curls shifting against the cloak.
“You never said it was this many.” *Why hide it? To protect me? The fool.*
Rimmon's shoulders rose and fell in a shallow breath, the motion sending another shiver through Andrej's hand. He pressed his palm flat between the shoulder blades— warm skin over old pain, the steady thrum of a heartbeat beneath, like a bird trapped in a cage. *You should have told me.* The thought ached, ancient and wordless, twisting deeper than guilt. He wanted to say: *I'm sorry. I cost you this. The salt flats stretched before them like a vast, blinding sheet of cracked porcelain, the ground a mosaic of hexagonal plates that heaved and split under the sun's merciless glare. Each step crunched with a brittle snap, the white crust giving way slightly, powdering their feet in fine, stinging dust that tasted of ancient sea and mineral bitterness. The air shimmered, heat rising in visible waves that bent the horizon into liquid distortion—mirages of phantom water danced just out of reach, teasing the eye, promising relief that vanished when approached.
Here the land offered no mercy, no shade, no wind to cool the sweat that poured down their faces and backs in salty rivers. The light was so fierce it bleached color from everything; skin turned ashen, eyes watered behind narrowed lids, the world reduced to glare and shadow.
Rimmon managed the first hours with grim determination, leaning hard on the acacia staff, its end digging shallow furrows in the salt. His breath came in short, ragged gasps; the staff trembled in his grip. Andrej walked next to him, scanning for the faintest change in the blinding white—a shift in texture, a darker line that might signal the far edge—but his glances to Rimmon grew more frequent, more worried.
By midday the heat pressed like a physical weight, squeezing the air from their lungs. Rimmon stumbled, once, twice, the staff slipping from numb fingers. Andrej caught him before he fell fully. He helped him back on his feet, and they pressed on. Hours blurred. The sun began its slow descent, but the flats seemed endless, mocking their progress. Rimmon's steps turned to shuffles, then drags. His face was gray beneath the dust, lips cracked and bloodless.
Andrej stopped at last, shoulders heaving.
“You can't keep going like this.”
Rimmon tried to straighten, pride flaring weakly. “I can—”
“No.” Andrej's voice was rough, final. He knelt, turning his back. “Climb on.”
Rimmon hesitated, the awkwardness sharp between them. He draped himself over Andrej's back, arms looping around broad shoulders, legs wrapping as best they could. Andrej's hands hooked under Rimmon's thighs, lifting him with a low grunt.
The weight settled, warm and heavy, Rimmon's chest pressed to Andrej's spine, breath hot against his neck. Every step jolted them both—Andrej's muscles straining, Rimmon's body shifting slightly with each movement, the friction of cloth and skin a constant, clumsy intimacy. Neither spoke. There was only the crunch of salt, the rhythm of Andrej's sandals, the shared heat between them like a second sun.
The flats seemed to stretch forever, but eventually the white began to thin, giving way to darker earth, scattered stones, the faint promise of scrub. Andrej's legs burned, sweat stung his eyes, but he did not stop. Not until the crust finally ended and the ground turned to hard-packed sand again, the ridge they'd left far behind, the first hints of coastal scrub appearing on the horizon.
He lowered Rimmon carefully against a low boulder, both of them collapsing in a heap. Rimmon's head lolled against Andrej's shoulder for a moment, too exhausted to pull away. Their breaths synced in the sudden quiet, chests rising and falling together.                  Edit  Copy  Share

Andrej woke to the low, wet snort of a camel and the sudden press of a stranger’s shadow across his face—eyes snapping open to an unfamiliar bearded face hovering close, firelight carving deep lines into weathered skin.
Andrej's knife was half-drawn before his mind fully caught up, the blade catching firelight in a quick silver flash. His body had uncoiled like a spring, one arm shielding Rimmon, who stirred groggily against his side. Heart hammering, breath sharp—*How did I sleep through this? The beasts, the footsteps...*—but exhaustion had dragged him under deeper than any watch could prevent.
The merchants did not flinch. They had built a fire a careful distance away—far enough to respect the strangers' space, close enough that its warmth reached like an open hand. The camels, massive and patient, knelt in the sand with heavy sighs, their breath clouding white in the night chill, the soft clink of their bells fading into the quiet. The oldest merchant, a wiry man with a face creased like old leather, had spoken first, palms raised: "We thought you were dead." His companions—two younger men and a boy barely grown—had nodded solemnly, then busied themselves with the bronze pot, pouring steaming mint tea into chipped clay cups that smelled of green leaves and faint sweetness.
The oldest gestured again to the fire, to the tea. "Drink. Warm yourselves. We called out—shook the blanket. Nothing. The desert takes the unwary." His voice was calm, the accent thick with the rolling consonants of the coastal tribes—proper Luedina desert dwellers, Andrej guessed, from the woolen cloaks dyed deep indigo.
Rimmon's hand found Andrej's wrist, fingers tightening—a silent wait, listen. The tension in Andrej's shoulders eased a fraction. No weapons visible. No chains, no ropes. Just tea, and the promise of warmth.
So they accepted, and Andrej sheathed the knife slowly, deliberately. Rimmon managed a nod, too weary for more. The merchants passed the cups; the tea was hot, sweet with honey and mint, cutting through the dust in their throats like a blade through silk. Andrej drank deeply, feeling the heat spread down his chest, loosening the knots of fear. Rimmon sipped more carefully next to him.
"May the Gods bless you," Andrej said at last, voice rough. "We... owe you."
The oldest merchant smiled faintly, teeth flashing in the firelight. "The desert owes no debts. It only asks you survive." He glanced at Rimmon's stained tunic, the way he held himself stiff against the pain. "You came across the flats? On foot?"
Andrej nodded once. No point in lying; the salt still powdered their feet, white as bone.
The younger men exchanged looks. One chuckled low. "No camel, open sandals... no offense, but it looks you crawled out of a grave. How?"
"Stubbornness," Rimmon murmured, surprising them all. His voice was faint but steady. "And him." He tilted his head toward Andrej.
The merchants laughed—quiet, appreciative. Andrej had his features covered, only his pale eyes betrayed his origin. He tried to keep it that way. The oldest poured more tea. "Stubbornness carries far. But the flats are cruel even to the stubborn."
They sat in companionable quiet for a time, the fire popping softly, the camels chewing their cud with rhythmic, wet sounds. Then the oldest spoke again, eyes on the horizon where the first gray of false dawn touched the sky. "You're heading south? To the sea?"
Andrej hesitated, then nodded. "The next city. Somewhere with ships."
"Kar-Nim," the merchant said without surprise. "The old harbor. Walls of stone, smells of tar and fish and freedom—if you can pay for it." He paused, gaze sharpening. "We live in dire times. The Emperor of Nova Prima has declared war on the Eisenbund. The forges run day and night. Ships come in heavy with refugees and slaves—people fleeing the endless hunger for bloodshed. They leave just as heavy, loaded with weapons, grain, mercenaries. The docks are chaos. The Awilum control and tax everything that breathes, but the Luedina captains will still take you—if you have the coin."
Rimmon shifted, wincing. "Refugees... how many?"
"Too many," one of the younger men muttered. "Whole families sleeping in the alleys. The war eats men like bread."
Andrej felt the weight settle heavier—their own flight suddenly small against that tide. But Kar-Nim still called: crowds to hide in, ships to cross the Big Sea.
"Where are you bound?" the oldest asked, polite curiosity, no demand.
Andrej met his eyes. He wasn't sure, he felt like his homeland was somewhere far south. Eisenbund sounded familiar. "South. Further south."
The merchant nodded slowly. "Look for Mergos. The world burns hot these days, the free cities are a safe haven in this storm." He reached into a satchel at his belt, drew out a small clay flask sealed with wax. "For the pain," he said, offering it to Rimmon. "Lotus liquor—strong, made from the fruit of the jujube tree. Sweet at first, then it dulls the edges. Not too much, or you'll forget your own name." He grinned, a flash of mischief. "The old empire wrote of us—Lotus-eaters, they called our kin. Said we lived on nothing but the fruit and its wine. Maybe they were half-right."
Rimmon took the flask, fingers brushing the merchant's in thanks. The seal cracked under his thumbnail; a faint, heady scent rose—sweet date-like fruit undercut by something sharper, fermented and wild. He took a small sip, eyes closing as the warmth bloomed in his chest, easing the fire in his back.
Andrej watched, tension still coiled but loosening. The merchants shared dates, a bit of flatbread, more tea. They told stories of broken lose camels, of how the younger merchant got lost in a sandstorm, and how they once made double the profit because they happened to be the only ones trading pomegranate at the time.
Dawn crept closer. The camels stirred, ready to move. The oldest merchant rose. "Come, travel with us to the coast road. It is safer than alone. We trade fruit inland, return with wool and leather."
Andrej glanced at Rimmon. A small nod—trust, fragile but growing.
"Yes," Andrej said. "We'd be grateful."                  Edit  Copy  Share

The Ghost moved like a man who had done this before, who knew how to make himself small and necessary at the same time.Rimmon’s head felt heavy, his thoughts moving through honey. Rimmon felt the contact like a brand, even through the haze.“Later.” His voice sounded wrong, too soft, too honest. He never pushed. That was his particular cruelty: he gave Rimmon space to be weak, and then watched him hate himself for taking it.The fire crackled. The camels chewed. Not a clasp. Not a promise. Just… contact. Skin to skin, warm palm pressing down on cold knuckles as if to say: *I am here. You are not falling.*The day ended not with a thought, but with a sensation: the taste of lotus and blood, the smell of camel and campfire, the weight of a hand that didn’t demand anything, and the bone-deep understanding that he was no longer the boy who wore blue cloth and carved snakes to survive.He was just Rimmon. And for the first time in his life, that felt like enough.

Dawn came slow and gray, the sky the color of wet slate, carrying the first faint promise of the sea on the wind. The merchants moved with practiced efficiency—rolling blankets, securing loads on the camels—while the low clatter of wooden crates and the soft jingle of bells filled the quiet. Andrej had spoken to the oldest while Rimmon slept, his voice low and steady as he traded a third of their meager coin. In the end, the merchant had nodded, gesturing to the low cart trailing behind the lead camel: a simple wooden frame piled with bundles of leather and folded wool, just wide enough for one man to lie flat.
“He can ride,” the old man had said simply.
Now the camp was nearly broken. Andrej knelt beside Rimmon, who hadn’t stirred since the fire died to embers. The boy’s breathing was deep and slow, the lotus liquor and bone-deep exhaustion holding him under like a tide. Andrej touched his shoulder—gentle, then firmer. Nothing. He tried again, murmuring his name. Rimmon only sighed, curling tighter into the blanket, his face slack and peaceful in a way that felt almost too intimate to witness.
The merchants exchanged glances, amused but patient. The oldest waved a hand. “Let him sleep. The cart has space enough.”
Andrej hesitated only a moment. Then he slid one arm under Rimmon’s shoulders, the other beneath his knees, and lifted him with the same careful strength he’d used across the flats. Rimmon was limp, heavy with sleep, head lolling against Andrej’s chest. The scent of lotus and sweat and faint mint salve rose from him, mingled now with the smoke of last night’s fire. Andrej carried him the few steps to the cart, laid him down on the wool blanket the merchants had spread across the leather goods—thick, scratchy, but warm from the morning sun already touching the fabric.
He tucked the edges around Rimmon’s body, careful of the wounds, smoothing the blanket over his chest like a mother might. Rimmon didn’t wake. Only his fingers twitched once, seeking something in sleep, then stilled.
The caravan started forward. Andrej walked beside the cart, one hand resting lightly on the wooden side, eyes flicking between the path ahead and the sleeping figure behind him. The camels plodded with their swaying, unhurried gait, bells chiming softly. After a time the younger merchants began to sing again—low, rhythmic, the same droning melody from the night before, until a chorus of voices filled the air. The song rolled over the dunes like water, ancient and wordless in parts, rising and falling with the cadence of their steps.
The land began to change. The sand grew coarser, flecked with broken shells. Scrub appeared—thorny acacias and salt-tolerant grasses. Then the air shifted: sharper, cooler, carrying the unmistakable tang of brine. Sea birds cried overhead—sharp, wheeling calls that cut through the morning haze. Far off, the low thunder of waves breaking on rock answered them.
They crested a final low rise and there it was: a proper road, paved in worn sandstone blocks, running straight along the coast like a scar on the land. The oldest merchant reined in his lead camel. The caravan halted.
“This is where we part,” he said, voice carrying over the wind. “Left for Kar-Nim. Right for Karzem. May the sun bless you both—and the sea be kind.”
Andrej turned to Rimmon. The boy still slept, face turned toward the sea now, dawn light painting faint gold along his cheekbones, the headwrap loosened so that dark curls spilled free. Andrej climbed onto the cart’s edge, knelt beside him.
“Rimmon,” he said softly, taking Rimmon's hand in his. “We’re here. The coast.”
No response.
He leaned closer, voice lower, intimate, brushing a thumb along the curve of his jaw. “Time to wake. The city’s waiting.”
Rimmon’s lashes fluttered. A slow breath. Then his eyes opened—hazy at first, unfocused, the pupils wide from liquor and deep sleep. He blinked up at Andrej, confusion giving way to recognition, then to something softer, unguarded.
“The sea?” Rimmon murmured, voice thick and cracked from sleep and liquor, the words barely rising above the distant crash of waves.
Andrej nodded, eyes fixed on the faint white line where water met sky, the first sails catching light like distant promises. “Close enough to taste.”
The phrase slipped out easy, but inside Andrej it landed heavier than he’d intended. *Close enough to taste.* Gods, yes—the salt wind already coated his tongue, sharp and clean, carrying the mineral bite of the Big Sea, the faint rot of seaweed, the promise of open water. But that wasn’t all he tasted in this moment. Rimmon’s nearness filled his senses: the lingering sweetness of lotus on his breath, the warm, lived-in scent of skin and sweat and salve that clung to him even after the flats, the faint herbal ghost of mint from the merchants’ tea. It was all tangled together now, salt and sweetness and something darker, earthier—the smell of a man who’d chosen to survive, who’d let himself be carried, who’d pressed close in the night without shame.
Andrej’s pulse kicked, low and steady, a rhythm he couldn’t quite name. He wanted to lean in, bury his face in the crook of Rimmon’s neck the way he’d done with the headscarf hours ago, inhale until the world narrowed to that single point of contact. He wanted to taste the salt at Rimmon’s temple again, the way he had when he’d straightened the wrap back in the dunes—slow, deliberate, pretending it was only practicality. He wanted more than the sea’s promise. He wanted the heat of Rimmon’s mouth, the give of his lips, the way his breath might hitch if Andrej finally stopped circling and simply took.
But he didn’t move. Instead he swallowed, let the want settle deep in his gut, kept the beast on a short leash.

Andrej exhaled through his nose, a quiet sound that might have been a laugh or a surrender. *Close enough to taste. Soon, maybe.* Then he pulled his hand back from Rimmon's jaw and dropped off the ledge of the cart. "Come, we're almost there."                  Edit  Copy  Share

## User

Andrej squeezed once. “Then let it rest.”
For now, he thought, the words silent even in his own head. Until we’re not limping anymore. Until the beast doesn’t have to choose between devouring you and tearing me apart first.
They walked into Kar-Nim together, two ghosts with names no one knew, holding the broken pieces of each other and calling them whole.
The streets of Kar-Nim swallowed them like a living tide—narrow, twisting alleys of salt-crusted stone and whitewashed mud-brick, roofed in places by sagging awnings of dyed wool that filtered the midday sun into dappled patterns of indigo and gold. The city sprawled vast along the curve of the bay, a chaotic mosaic of old Nova Prima arches crumbling beside newer Awilum towers, their basalt reliefs of winged lions staring sternly down at the throng below. The air was thick with the reek of fish guts, hot tar from the shipyards, roasting cumin and coriander from street vendors, and the constant, pounding brine of Big Sea crashing against the quays. Voices overlapped in a dozen tongues—Luedina cadences rolling like waves, Awilum commands sharp as whips, the quick barter of refugees and merchants haggling over amphorae of oil and grain.
Kar-Nim was no quiet refuge; it was a beast in its own right, massive and indifferent, fueled by the war's endless hunger. Ships crowded the harbors—broad-beamed Luedina traders with indigo sails furled, Nova Prima galleys low in the water from fresh cargoes of weapons and salted meat, privateers slipping in under cover of dusk. The forges rang day and night, hammers striking like distant thunder, while the wounded limped off transports, bandaged and hollow-eyed, and slaves were herded toward the markets in chains that clinked with every step. The city profited from the chaos without fully joining it—taxing every hull, feeding every army, sheltering spies and deserters alike in its labyrinthine quarters.
Andrej fought his way through the city, trying to read the signs, trying to understand the pattern. Ultimately, they arrived in the healers' district, a warren of low buildings near the old commercial harbor where the scent of bitter herbs cut through the fish-stink. Rimmon walked beside him now, steps steadier but still careful, one hand occasionally brushing Andrej's arm for balance or something more.
They found a modest infirmary tucked behind a row of bonded warehouses, its sign a faded carving of the healing goddess, her staff raised over a coiled serpent. The healer was an older Luedina woman, skin dark from years under the coastal sun, hands callused from grinding salves. 
She examined Rimmon's back and cleaned his wounds with spirit that made Rimmon hiss, then reapplied a thick paste of honey and crushed leaves that smelled sharp and green. "They are healing well, but you need rest," she said, voice low. "No lifting. No fighting. Avoid any physical labor."
Andrej paid from their dwindling coin, then asked the questions burning in him: "The other side of the sea—where is it safe? Which cities should we avoid?"

The healer wiped her hands on a rag, eyes flicking to the door. "Avoid the south shore if you value your skin. Micholeum is the Empire’s heart now—full of fanatics and legions who’d chain a man for looking at them sideways. And Salmacis is worse; it’s a meat grinder for the war. Troops going out, broken boys coming back. You’ll be pressed into service before you can find an inn."

She paused, appraising Andrej’s silver eyes with a sharp, knowing look. "Mergos is your best bet. It’s a free city, a den of merchants and spies where the great roads meet. It has its own rules. If you’re nameless, you can vanish there. Many head that way."

Andrej absorbed it, his mind turning like a ship’s rudder. Mergos sounded like a place where two ghosts could breathe—crowded enough to hide, strategic enough to offer a way out. Not safe, exactly, but possible.
"And... the Eisenbund?"
She studied him again—his bulky frame, the broad shoulders, the rose-undertone of his skin so unlike the olive of the desert people. "Do not go there. You will find nothing but death and sorrow."
*Death and sorrow... story of my life.*
They left the healer with a small jar of salve and directions to a cheap caravanserai near the docks. Rimmon needed rest; Andrej needed coin. The inn was a narrow three-story building of weathered timber, rooms small but clean, with a shared courtyard where sailors drank barley beer and sang in slurred Luedina. Andrej bartered their labor: he would haul cargo from the quays at dawn, and Rimmon could help in the kitchen once his back allowed. It would give them enough to eat, and enough time to save for passage.                  Edit  Copy  Share

Let me craft a scene that shows their first days in Kar-Nim, the rhythm they establish, and how they begin to build a shared life while dealing with the physical and emotional aftermath of their escape.   Rimmon’s first week in Kar-Nim was a lesson in smallness. He woke before dawn—not by choice, but because the kitchen fires started at first light, and the cook was a woman who believed in greeting sunrise with a wooden spoon and a stream of inventive curses. His back was still a map of scabs and fire, so his duties were limited: peeling vegetables, grinding spices, boiling water for the endless tea the sailors demanded. The work was mindless, repetitive, and blessedly anonymous. No one called him Young Master. No one asked him to perform.Andrej left earlier, his bulk an asset on the quays. He hauled amphorae of fish oil and bales of wool, returning at dusk with his tunic soaked in sweat and brine, his hands raw from rope burns. They shared a room on the third floor—a space barely wider than the bed, with a window that looked out on the courtyard where drunks sang themselves into oblivion. It was enough.On the fourth night, Rimmon returned from the kitchen to find Andrej already in the room, sitting on the floor because the bed was too small for both of them to sit comfortably. He had a wooden bowl in his lap, a bundle of rags, and the small knife from the healer.“Take your tunic off,” Andrej said, not looking up.Rimmon obeyed without question, the habit of obedience still strong. Andrej gestured for him to turn. The wounds were healing, but slowly—the humidity and constant motion kept breaking the scabs. Andrej cleaned them again, his touch as methodical as it had been in the desert, but slower now, as if he were memorizing the terrain.“You’re getting better at this,” Rimmon murmured, wincing as the salve stung.“I’ve had practice.” Andrej’s voice was flat, but there was something underneath—satisfaction, maybe. Pride.Rimmon looked over his shoulder, catching Andrej’s eye. “Practice on me, you mean.”A flicker of a smile. “Someone has to keep you from falling apart.”“I’m not falling apart. I’m just… reassembling.”Andrej’s thumb brushed a particularly tender spot, making Rimmon hiss. “Could have fooled me.”The teasing was gentle, careful, like handling cracked pottery. It was the first time they’d joked since the courtyard. The first time the space between them felt breathable.That night, they lay side by side on the narrow bed, not touching but close enough to share warmth. The courtyard singers were at it again, a slurred ballad about a sailor’s lost love. Rimmon listened to the words, then to Andrej’s breathing, slow and even beside him.“I never asked,” he said to the ceiling. “What will you do? Once we’re in Mergos.”Andrej was quiet long enough that Rimmon thought he’d fallen asleep. Then: “I’ll find work. There’s always work for a man who can lift things.” He paused. “And maybe I’ll learn to read.”The word landed like a stone in still water. Rimmon turned his head on the thin pillow. “You want to?”“I want to know what contracts say before I sign them. I want to read maps. I want…” He stopped. “I want to read the things you carve.”Rimmon’s chest tightened. He’d never carved words—only shapes, symbols, things that spoke without language. But the idea of Andrej reading them, translating them into sound…“I’ll teach you,” he said. “If you want.”Andrej didn’t answer. But his hand found Rimmon’s in the dark, fingers tangling together on the rough blanket.The courtyard singer hit a high, mournful note. The room smelled of salt and old wood and the faint, honeyed ghost of lotus. Rimmon closed his eyes, letting the chaos of Kar-Nim fade into the background, until all that remained was the anchor of Andrej’s hand and the promise of a word he couldn’t yet read.The day ended not with a plan, but with a question left open—like a door that had never been closed, waiting for someone to finally walk through.

The smell of frying onions and scorched flatbread pulled Andrej toward the kitchen before the sun had even cleared the harbor walls. He found Rimmon hunched over a blackened griddle, his movements careful but efficient, the steam curling around his dark hair.
"You’re supposed to be resting," Andrej grunted, though he couldn't hide the way his eyes softened at the sight of the boy upright.
Rimmon didn't look up, but a small, tired smirk touched his lips. "And you're supposed to be an 'ox,' according to the innkeeper. The kitchen needed a hand, and I have two. Besides, the cook gives better scraps to the help." He slid a folded piece of bread filled with salty cheese toward the edge of the table. "Eat. Before you try to lift a whole ship by yourself."
Andrej took the bread, his thumb brushing Rimmon’s as he snatched it. "I’ll be back by dusk. Stay off your feet when the rush starts."
"Go," Rimmon laughed softly, shooing him with a wooden spoon. "The sea isn't going to move itself."
Andrej chewed as he walked, the salt and grease fueling him through a grueling morning of hauling crates until his muscles screamed and his skin was a map of brine and sweat.
The dockhands had claimed Andrej the moment the last amphora was stacked, their voices rough with the day’s salt and triumph “The ox needs a proper breaking in!” one bellowed, slapping a broad palm against Andrej’s back hard enough to make the big man grunt. They dragged him to a low table behind the warehouses, crates for seats, a big clay jug of Nova Prima red waiting like a challenge—dark as spilled blood, heavy with blackberry, clove, and the faint smoky bite of imperial oak.
Andrej tried to wave them off. “I’ve got a room to get back to.”
"A room? What are you, a chair?"
They laughed, poured anyway, shoved one of the cups into his hand. “Come on, Ox. One round. It brings luck.”
Then another. Then a third. The wine went down smooth at first, warm and velvet, loosening the knots in his shoulders, the ache in his arms. By the fifth cup the world tilted pleasantly, harbor lights smearing into gold streaks, laughter blurring into a distant roar.
The man beside him—slender, his beard carefully trimmed, with a sailor’s crooked grin and a scar that hooked under his left eye—leaned in too close, breath hot with wine.
“Those eyes of yours,” he said, voice low and amused, tilting his head as if studying a rare cargo. “Silver like moonlight on water. Never seen the like down here. Makes a man wonder what you’re hiding under that headscarf.” His gaze flicked to the undyed cloth wrapped around Andrej’s head, then lower, deliberate, lingering on the sweat-damp line of Andrej’s throat, the open collar of his tunic. “Bet there’s more than dust and scars underneath.”
The words landed like a stone in still water.
Andrej froze, cup halfway to his lips. The wine suddenly tasted sharper, bitter. His pulse kicked hard against his ribs—not anger, not exactly. Something hotter, more confused.
A man. Flirting. With him.
The thought hit him sideways, clumsy and bright. In the tannery yards, in the villages, in every place he’d ever been, desire had always been spoken of in women’s names—soft curves, laughter behind veils, the expected dance. No one had ever looked at him like this, open and hungry and unafraid. Well.. no one except—He pushed the thought down.
The world narrowed to the heat in his cheeks, the low chuckle of the dockhand, the way the man’s knee had shifted closer under the table.
Andrej’s mouth went dry. He set the cup down carefully, too carefully, fingers trembling just enough to slosh wine over the rim.
“Don’t,” he said, voice rougher than he intended.
The sailor raised both hands, grin widening but eyes sharp. “Easy, ox. Just paying a compliment. You’re a sight, that’s all.”
He leaned back, but his gaze did not waver. It bore into Andrej like a hot iron, lingering there, until he could no longer take it. Andrej stood abruptly, chair scraping loud against stone. The table tilted in his vision.
“Enough for tonight,” he muttered, already turning away. The others laughed, good-natured, poured another round without him. They began to sing, about what, he could not hear. He stumbled up the narrow stairs, feet heavy, head swimming with wine and something far more dangerous.
The room was dim when he shouldered the door open, lit only by the single oil lamp Rimmon had left burning low on the sill. Rimmon was already in bed, curled on his side, asleep. Andrej stood in the doorway a moment, swaying, the wine humming in his veins like a second heartbeat, the sailor’s words still ringing in his ears.
He kicked the door shut behind him, louder than intended. Rimmon didn’t move.
Andrej peeled off his sweat-soaked tunic, letting it drop to the floor with a wet slap. The air was cool against his flushed skin, raising gooseflesh along his broad shoulders, the heavy muscles of his chest and arms still gleaming faintly from the day’s labor and the wine’s heat. He crossed to the bed in two unsteady steps, lowered himself beside Rimmon with a groan of old wood and tired bones.
The mattress dipped sharply. Rimmon shifted, just enough to press their shoulders together—accidental, necessary, inevitable.
Andrej stared at the ceiling, chest rising and falling too fast. The sailor’s gaze burned behind his eyes, making everything sharper, more real. A man could want another man. It wasn’t myth or sin whispered in corners. It was here, possible, and now Andrej felt it in his own blood—hot, undeniable, terrifying. He lowered his gaze to Rimmon's silhouette, trying to make out his features.
Words rose unbidden, thick on his tongue.
“Shouldn’t look at you like that…” he mumbled, his voice thick and slurring at the edges. “But gods, Rimmon… every time you move… makes me want to—”
The sentence hung unfinished, severed by the sudden, violent tilt of the room. As Andrej closed his eyes, the solid floor seemed to drop away, leaving the bed adrift on a dark, heaving swell. It wasn't the gentle sway of the merchant’s cart; it was the sickening roll of a ship in a storm. The ceiling spun clockwise while his stomach lurched to the left, a dizzying centrifugal force that pulled him deeper into the mattress.
He tried to catch his breath, but the air felt like warm silk, heavy and impossible to swallow. The shadows behind his eyelids weren't black—they were a pulsing, bruised purple that throbbed in time with the wine-drum in his ears. He reached out a hand to steady the world, his fingers brushing the coarse wool of the blanket, but even that felt miles away, as if his nerves were firing through layers of wet clay.
Then, the spin reached a crescendo and snapped.
The vertigo didn't stop so much as it was consumed. A Great Blackness—not a sleep, but a void—rose up from the floorboards to meet him. It was a heavy, sensory-deadening tide that extinguished the smell of the lamp oil and the warmth of Rimmon’s shoulder in a single, silent crash.
Time didn't just pass; it ceased to exist.
Andrej didn't drift. He fell. One moment he was a man on fire, haunted by a sailor's gaze and a boy's silhouette; the next, he was nothing at all, his consciousness snuffed out like a wick dipped in oil. The snoring began then—a low, rhythmic rumble that signaled he was no longer in the room, but lost somewhere in the featureless deep.                  Edit  Copy  Share

He tucked the carving back into his pouch. “Okay.”“Okay?”“Okay.” He stood, brushing sand from his trousers. “But if I’m going to be whatever we are, I need to learn how to walk without limping first.”He walked back to the fire, leaving Andrej alone on the bluff with the sea wind and the beast that had finally stopped pacing. Two ghosts walked into a city where names were currency, carrying broken pieces and calling them whole.Tomorrow, they would see if the city believed them.

Andrej stayed where he was, alone on the bluff's edge, the sea wind tugging insistently at his tunic, cold fingers slipping under the fabric to trace the sweat-damp lines of his ribs. Below, the waves broke white against black rocks in a steady, indifferent rhythm—like a heartbeat that didn't care whether it was wanted or not.

*If he is going to be whatever they were.*

He turned the words over in his mind, slow and deliberate, weighing them like stones before skipping them across water. *Partners? Yes. Obviously*. They'd carried each other across salt flats and through nights that should have killed them both. *Friends?* The word felt too small, too safe, like a child's hand-me-down tunic. He nodded to himself anyway, because it was easier than admitting the next thought.

*Lovers?*

The word landed in his gut like a live coal. His breath hitched—sharp, audible even over the wind. Heat bloomed low and sudden, throbbing behind his navel, spreading outward in slow, inexorable waves until it reached his throat and tightened there. He had never even kissed someone. Not really. Not the way people did in the stories the adults used to whisper about, half-laughing, half-awed, when they thought no one else was listening. Sometimes, late at night back in the tannery compound, Andrej had heard Liyara and Harran—the rhythmic thuds of bodies meeting, the low animal sounds muffled but unmistakable, rising and falling like the sea itself. He'd lain awake, staring at the ceiling beams, skin prickling with something he couldn't name, something that felt dangerous and holy at the same time.

Now the name was Rimmon.

Now the want had a face, a voice, a scent—mint salve and salt and the faint green bitterness of crushed herbs. Now the want had hands that trembled when they touched him, eyes that looked at him like he might be something worth keeping.

Andrej closed his eyes and let himself fall back flat onto the grass, arms spread wide on either side, fingers digging into the tough turf as if he could root himself there, anchor himself against the pull. The grass was cool and damp against his palms; he pulled, hard, tearing small clumps free, the sharp green smell rising sudden and sharp. His chest rose and fell too fast, tunic riding up to expose a strip of skin to the wind—gooseflesh chasing across his abdomen, tightening everything lower still.

The conversation in the shared room replayed in his head, soft and deliberate, Rimmon's voice low. *You said I make you want to—*. Taunting. Demanding.

And devastating.

The image flashed behind Andrej's eyelids: Rimmon beneath him, dark curls spilling across a pillow, lips parted on a gasp that might be Andrej's name. The thought sent a fresh spike of heat through him—sharp enough to make him bite the inside of his cheek, tasting copper.

He was terrified.

He was aching.

And the worst part—the sweetest, most horrifying part—was that Rimmon knew. He'd said it on purpose, left it hanging between them like bait, then walked away with that small, knowing hitch in his step, as if to remind Andrej exactly what was waiting when the healing was done.

Andrej opened his eyes to the stars, chest heaving, fingers still clenched in the grass. The beast in his gut wasn't pacing anymore. It was crouched low, muscles bunched, eyes fixed on the firelight where Rimmon sat.

Tomorrow they would reach Mergos.

Tomorrow the city would swallow them, give them new names or none at all.

Tomorrow Andrej would have to decide whether to keep being the sensible one… or finally step forward and take what Rimmon was so calmly, ruthlessly offering.

For now, though, he lay there spread-eagled under the indifferent sky, body thrumming with want and fear in equal measure, the wind teasing every inch of exposed skin like a promise he wasn't sure he could survive.

It took him what felt like an eternity to get back on his feet. He moved like a man wading through deep water, staggering into the tent where the air was still and smelled of the cedar-smoke of the dying fire. He did not dare to undress, to even look toward the dark shape of the bedroll. Every movement felt like a betrayal of the silence. He simply collapsed onto his blankets, fully clothed, and closed his eyes, faking a sleep so desperate it eventually—mercifully—claimed him.                  Edit  Copy  Share

I thought maybe you were trying to drink it through your skin.”Andrej yanked the tunic the rest of the way off, let it fall to the floor. “I was thinking.”“About what?”“Nothing.”“Liar.” Rimmon stood, the carved hand still clicking in his palm. He stepped closer, close enough that Andrej could smell the kitchen’s onions on him, the mint tea, the faint, stubborn scent of the salve. “You were thinking about the sailor. Pretending you’re still the ghost who has to earn his place by being useful.” He stopped, just short of touching. “You don’t have to be useful to me, Andrej. You just have to be *here*.”Andrej’s hands curled into fists at his sides. “I am here.”“No.” Rimmon lifted the carved hand, pressed it to Andrej’s chest, right over the heart hammering beneath. “You’re *there*. Behind a wall. And I’m tired of throwing stones at it.”He dropped the carving. It fell to the floor with a soft thud. Then he did something Andrej had never seen him do: he stripped off his own tunic. No flourish. No performance. Just the quick, efficient motion of a man shedding armor. You gave me the brown cloth. You gave me the knife. You gave me the road. Now I’m giving you this: the ugliest, most honest piece I have.”He took Andrej’s hand—not gently, but with the same grip Andrej had used on the ladder in the salt flats, the same grip that said *I will not let you fall*. He placed it on his own bare chest, over a heart that was beating as fast as Andrej’s.“I’m not asking you to carve me into something beautiful,” Rimmon said. “I’m asking you to hold the broken piece and not flinch.”Andrej’s fingers splayed against the skin, feeling the heat, the hammering pulse, the faint tremor of absolute terror wearing a brave face. The tent was too small for the silence Andrej brought back from the bluff, the canvas walls closing in like a fist around his chest. He ducked under the flap an hour after Rimmon had left him there—long enough that the fire outside had burned down to embers and the night air had grown sharp with the scent of cooling earth. His sandals scraped against the packed dirt floor, the sound unnaturally loud. He winced, still tasting the faint copper on his tongue where he’d bitten his cheek.

Rimmon was already there, sitting on the edge of the narrow bedroll. He was turning the carved hand over in his fingers—*click, click, click*—the wood on wood sounding like a clock counting down. The oil lamp flickered low on a makeshift crate, highlighting the sharp line of Rimmon's jaw, the patchy beard that had started growing there, and the faint sheen of sweat at his temples.

Andrej paused, shoulders hunched. He avoided Rimmon's eyes, focusing instead on untying his sandals, the leather straps sticky with the day's dust.

“So,” Rimmon said, not looking up, his voice light but edged like a blade, “did the sea answer you? Or are you still out there, trying to stare the water into submission?”

Andrej froze, his tunic half-pulled over his head, the fabric muffling the world—dark, damp, smelling of salt. “What?”

“I left you brooding an hour ago.” Rimmon finally lifted his eyes, and they were surgical, dissecting Andrej with a glance. “You came back looking like you’ve been wrestling with a ghost. Or yourself.”

Andrej yanked the tunic the rest of the way off, the rough wool scraping his ears. The cool air hit his bare chest like a slap, raising fresh gooseflesh across his broad shoulders, his muscles still taut from clenching in the grass. *He’s toying with me*, Andrej thought, heart pounding. *He knows.*

“I was thinking,” Andrej muttered, his voice rough as gravel.

“About what?”

“Nothing.”

Fear clawed at him, sharp and familiar: *What if I touch him and it breaks everything? What if I don't and it breaks me?*

“You’re doing it again,” Rimmon said, his voice dropping to a murmur that vibrated through Andrej's chest. “Pretending the thing you want is a burden you can’t carry. Pretending you’re still the ghost who has to earn his place by being useful.” He stopped just short of touching, his breath warm against Andrej's collarbone. “You don’t have to be useful to me, Andrej. You just have to be here.”

Andrej’s hands curled into fists at his sides, nails biting into palms, the sting grounding him against the wave of heat building low in his gut. “I am here,” he ground out, but it sounded weak even to him.

“No.” Rimmon lifted the carved hand, pressed it to Andrej’s chest, right over the heart hammering beneath like a trapped animal. The wood was warm from Rimmon's grip, and the contact sent a jolt through him—prickling, intimate. “You’re there. Behind a wall. And I’m tired of throwing stones at it.”

Rimmon let the carved hand drop.

It struck the dirt with a soft thud and stilled. Then Rimmon reached for the hem of his tunic. Andrej watched, helpless, as Rimmon pulled the wool over his head in one efficient motion—no hesitation, just the quiet shedding of armor.
The Gods were testing him tonight. Or Rimmon. Or both of them.

Rimmon stood bare-chested in the unsteady lamp-flicker. Andrej’s lungs forgot how to work. He couldn’t tear his eyes away.
The sight was merciless. Rimmon’s front bore no deep welts—Meddūr had saved the worst for the back—but the body beneath was still a brutal testament: lean muscle earned from privilege turned to survival, faint ridges of ribs still visible under skin stretched tight from weeks of scant meals, the shadowed hollow above his collarbone rising and falling too fast. A thin trail of dark hair arrowed downward from his navel, disappearing beneath the waistband of his loincloth. Sweat from the long day’s march still clung in the dips of his sternum, catching the flame in tiny, treacherous glints.
Andrej’s gaze traced every inch—greedy, reverent, starving—like a man who’d walked through desert for months and only now realized water had been standing in front of him the whole time. His mouth went dry. His hands flexed at his sides, fingers curling then uncurling, nails biting into palms. Heat pooled low and insistent, making his thighs tense, his breath come shorter. He could smell Rimmon now—cooling sweat, mint tea, the stubborn green of salve—and it was too much. Too close. Too real.
*Move*, some part of him screamed. *Touch him. Take him.*
But he stayed rooted, trembling, while the beast inside clawed at the bars.
Rimmon didn’t flinch under the scrutiny. He simply stood there, arms loose at his sides, chin lifted just enough to meet Andrej’s stare without blinking. The lamplight carved sharp angles across his face—high cheekbones, stubborn jaw, the faint violet bruise of sleepless nights under his eyes—and for once there was no mask, no careful poetry. Just Rimmon. Bare. Honest. Terrified and defiant in equal measure.
The air between them thickened, turned viscous, heavy with cedar smoke from the dying fire outside, the cooling musk of sweat, the faint green bitterness of salve that still clung to Rimmon’s skin like a second, fragile layer.

“You want to know what a man is?” Rimmon asked, and his voice was raw now, stripped of all its poetry, the words hanging heavy in the tent's stale air. “A man is someone who can be broken and still chooses which piece to hand to someone else. He took Andrej’s hand—not gently, but with the same firm grip they had used on the ladder back at the oasis. He placed it on his own bare chest. *Gods, he's real*, Andrej thought, the steady thrum of Rimmon's pulse finally breaking through his restraint.

“I’m not asking you to carve me into something beautiful,” Rimmon said. “I’m asking you to hold the broken piece and not flinch.”

Andrej’s fingers splayed against the skin, feeling the heat, the hammering pulse, the faint tremor of absolute terror wearing a brave face. This wasn’t seduction. This was surrender—Rimmon offering himself as a specimen of what it meant to be ruined and still want. And inside Andrej, the beast uncoiled further, claws scraping against the walls he'd built, demanding release. *I could crush him*, the thought flashed, terrifying and intoxicating. *I could take him apart right here, feel him shatter under my hands.* It was torture. The words were a spark to dry tinder. The confession rose in his throat, jagged and burning, so close he could taste it—bitter, coppery, like blood from a bitten tongue. His lips parted. His breath came in shallow, ragged pants that filled the tent. One word. Just one filthy, honest word and everything would shatter.
But the scars on Rimmon's back flashed behind his eyes—fresh, red-edged, still healing. Andrej had carried him across salt flats because he couldn't bear to see him fall again. If he spoke the truth now, if he let the beast loose while Rimmon was still limping, what would stop him from breaking him completely? The thought twisted like a knife.

He choked it back.

“I want to—” he started, but he could not finish. Rimmon didn’t move, didn’t blink. He just waited, the most brutal thing he could do, his pulse racing under Andrej's palm like an invitation.

Andrej’s other hand came up, almost against his will, and cupped Rimmon’s jaw—the skin warm and smooth, the new stubbles of his beard rough under his thumb, the line of bone firm yet fragile. Rimmon’s eyes fluttered shut for a heartbeat, the mask slipping completely. He looked young. He looked terrified. He looked like a boy who had just handed a loaded knife to the one person who could kill him with it.

*No*, Andrej thought, the word a silent roar. *Not yet. Not like this.*

“—I want to stop being the branch,” Andrej finished, voice rough as gravel. “And see if I can be the man who holds it.”

It was a deflection, but it was all he could give. Relief washed over Rimmon’s face.

“Took you long enough,” he whispered, and leaned in.

The kiss wasn't gentle. It was a collision—lips meeting hard, tasting of mint and salt. Andrej angled his head, deepening it instinctively, his hands sliding to Rimmon's waist to pull him closer. It was the end of being sensible.

Rimmon pulled back first, just enough to breathe, his hands on Andrej’s shoulders, fingers digging in like anchors. “Now,” he said, voice husky, eyes dark with the same fire Andrej felt, “let’s see if you can carry me without me having to pretend I’m not bleeding.”

He let himself go limp—just for a second, just to test it, his body sagging against Andrej's with a trust that made his heart stutter. Andrej caught him, arms wrapping around his back, careful of the wounds but firm, the heat of Rimmon's skin searing through him, the faint tremor of shared fear vibrating between them. They stood there, swaying like a tree in wind, the room too small for what they’d just admitted.

Rimmon’s breath was hot against Andrej’s neck. “I’m going to be a terrible lover,” he murmured, lips brushing skin in a way that sent shivers racing down Andrej's spine. “I’ll probably quote poetry at the wrong moment.”

“I’ll probably break something,” Andrej countered, his voice low, his hand on Rimmon's back drawing mindless patterns. “Or you.”                  Edit  Copy  Share

He could feel Andrej’s hardness through the thin cloth, a fact that made his own breath stutter. He wanted to be suave, wanted to say something devastatingly clever, but what came out was: “You’re, uh, very… present.”Andrej’s laugh was sharp, startled. “That’s one word for it.”Rimmon’s face burned. *Idiot*, he thought. *You had one chance to be sexy and you said ‘present’.* But Andrej’s hands were on his waist now, thumbs tracing the bone there with a reverence that felt like prayer, and the embarrassment dissolved into something hotter, more urgent.“Rimmon,” Andrej said, and the name sounded different now—like a key turning in a lock. “If we do this, I need you to tell me when it’s too much. I need you to say stop. I won’t know otherwise. I’ll just—” He stopped, swallowed. “I’ll just keep going until I break you.”The honesty was so raw it hurt. Rimmon leaned down, pressed his forehead to Andrej’s, and whispered, “Then I’ll just have to break first. So you learn the sound.”He kissed him again, slower this time, teaching the shape of his mouth the way he’d taught Andrej to read maps: methodically, deliberately, with occasional detours into territory that made them both gasp. His hands moved to Andrej’s shoulders, feeling the massive muscles there, the sheer, stupid *strength* that had carried him across the flats, and thought: *This is mine. This terrifying, useful, impossible man is mine.*And under him, Andrej finally stopped being sensible. He let his hands roam—up Rimmon’s spine, careful of the welts but ruthless everywhere else, mapping the dips and hollows he’d spent a year pretending not to notice. When he reached Rimmon’s hair, he tangled his fingers in it, pulled just hard enough to make Rimmon’s breath catch, and whispered, “You’re not a performance anymore.”“No,” Rimmon agreed, biting Andrej’s lower lip. “I’m a mess. Your mess.”They moved together, clumsy and desperate, knocking over the lamp, spilling oil across the dirt floor, filling the tent with the smell of cedar and sweat and something sweeter—mint and honey and the lingering ghost of lotus. It wasn’t graceful. It wasn’t perfect. It was two boys who had survived the desert by learning to carry weight, now learning how to set it down.When it was over—when they lay panting in the dark, limbs tangled, the blanket kicked aside—they didn’t speak. Rimmon rested his head on Andrej’s chest, listening to the heart that had finally stopped racing, and thought: *I broke first. Just like I said I would. And he learned the sound.*The beast in his gut was quiet now. Not pacing. Not biting. Just... there. A part of him, curled up next to the part of Andrej that had finally let go.Tomorrow, they would walk into Mergos. Tomorrow, they would be ghosts with new names. But tonight, they were just Rimmon and Andrej, terrible lovers in a tent that smelled of spilled oil and honesty.And for the first time, that felt like enough.

Andrej woke first, as he always did.
The tent was dim, the canvas walls glowing faintly gold where the dawn pressed through. The tent still smelled of the spilled oil and something more musky, manly, raw. He lay still, listening to Rimmon’s even breathing against his chest. Dark curls tickled his collarbone, and Rimmon’s arm lay possessively across his ribs, as if afraid to let go even in sleep.
With practiced patience, Andrej eased himself out from under the weight. The boy stirred once, a soft, protesting murmur, then settled with his face half-buried in the blanket. Andrej paused, looking down at him: all naked and glorious, his lips still swollen from kisses that had started desperate and ended reverent.
*We did that*, Andrej thought, a quiet wonder curling in his chest. *I did that and he’s still here.*
He dressed quietly—sandals, tunic, the undyed headwrap tied with steady fingers. Every rustle of fabric felt like a potential betrayal of the silence. He stepped outside into the cool air, where the bluff’s edge stood sharp against the horizon. Below, Mergos glowed like a spilled jewel box. Towers of pale stone caught the rose-gold light, and bridges arced over streets like silver threads. The city breathed with an old Elven grace; Renaissance curves and delicate filigree decorated the gates, while gardens spilled over the terracotta and lapis rooftops in impossible greens. Beautiful. Different.
The camp was stirring. A few traders moved near the dying embers, murmuring in low Luedina cadences. No one looked up. No knowing smirks; no raised brows. Relief flooded through Andrej like cool water. They didn’t know, or they didn’t care. Either way, he could breathe.
He moved to the small cookfire the traders had left banked overnight. The routine was grounding: strike the flint, feed the flame, wait for the bronze kettle to shiver. He found flatbread, a wedge of salty cheese, and a few dates. Simple, practical things Rimmon could eat without having to move too fast.
When the tea was ready—steaming and fragrant with mint—Andrej carried the cups back like offerings. Inside, Rimmon was awake, propped on one elbow. His hair was a wild tangle, his eyes heavy-lidded and soft in the filtered light. He looked at Andrej like he was seeing him for the first time.
“You’re up early,” Rimmon said, his voice rough.
“Someone has to be.” Andrej set the food down, sitting on the edge of the bedroll until their knees brushed. “Tea. Food. You need both.”
Rimmon’s mouth quirked. “Ever the sensible one.”
“Ever the terrible lover,” Andrej countered, the words slipping out before he could catch them.
Rimmon laughed—soft, startled—and reached for the tea. His fingers lingered against Andrej’s as he took the cup. “I did say that, didn't I? And you proved me right.” He sipped, eyes never leaving Andrej’s face. “But you’re also… very present.”
Andrej felt heat crawl up his neck, but he didn’t look away. “That’s one word for it.”
They drank in silence as the camp woke around them: the grunt of camels and the distant crash of waves. Mergos waited, its Elven spires catching the sun as if they’d spent centuries waiting for someone to notice.
Rimmon set the cup down. “We’re going to be terrible at this, you know.”
“I know.”
“But we’re going to do it anyway.”
Andrej nodded, slow and sure. “Anyway.”
Rimmon leaned forward, pressing a brief, open-mouthed kiss to Andrej’s lips—tasting of mint and promise—then whispered against his skin: “Then let’s go see what a city built by elves and fools thinks of two boys who finally stopped running.”
Andrej stood and offered his hand. Rimmon took it, letting Andrej pull him up with careful strength. For once, as he found his footing, he didn't favor his injury.
They stepped out into the dawn together, ready to conquer the day.                  Edit  Copy  Share

“So, what do you want to do?”
They had barely enough coin left for either a washhouse or a decent dinner—not both, and certainly not for a room at an inn. Yet Andrej felt as though he could offer Rimmon the entire world.
“We could head to the harbor and look for work,” he said, voice low and steady. “If we’re lucky, we’ll find something that includes lodging again. Or…”
His eyes drifted over the city streets, drinking in the endless possibilities. Every kind of artisan was here: elven masters of magical enchantment with their quiet, glowing stalls; food-sellers whose braziers released sharp, sweet, bitter fragrances all at once; dancers and musicians weaving through the crowd; grooms tending to sleek horses and gilded chariots.
Then Andrej spotted something that made his gaze sharpen.
A ring—an arena of packed sand circled by low stone walls. Men wrestled inside it, bare except for loincloths, bodies gleaming with sweat under the midday sun. Muscles flexed and strained; grunts and laughter carried over the crowd. Spectators leaned on the barriers, tossing copper coins, cheering, placing wagers.
Andrej pointed.
“There,” he said, the single word carrying more weight than he intended.
Rimmon followed his gaze. His brows lifted slightly, a slow, knowing smile curving his mouth.
“Interesting choice, ox,” he murmured, stepping closer until his shoulder brushed Andrej’s. “Looking for work… or looking for an excuse to watch half-naked men roll around in the dirt?”
Andrej’s ears went hot, but he didn’t look away from the ring.
“Work,” he said firmly. “They pay winners. And losers, sometimes, if they put on a good show. We need coin. And—” He swallowed, glancing sideways at Rimmon. “I’m strong enough. You could watch from the edge. Rest your back.”
Rimmon’s smile widened, sharp and delighted.
“Oh, I’ll watch,” he promised, voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. “But don’t think I won’t enjoy every second of it. You, all oiled and glistening, pinning some poor fool to the sand… I might even cheer. Loudly.”
Andrej felt the familiar heat crawl up his neck again—this time mixed with something darker, hungrier. He turned fully toward Rimmon, voice low enough that only Rimmon could hear.
“Keep talking like that,” he warned, “and I won’t make it to the ring. I’ll drag you into the nearest alley and remind you what happens when you push.”
Rimmon’s eyes darkened, pupils blown wide. He leaned in, lips brushing the shell of Andrej’s ear.
“Promises, promises,” he breathed. “But first—coin. Then dinner. Then a washhouse. Then… we see how long you can keep being sensible.”
He pulled back just enough to meet Andrej’s gaze, small and vicious and hopeful.
“So. Arena first?”
Andrej exhaled through his nose, half laugh, half surrender.
“Arena first.”
He took Rimmon’s hand—not gently, but firmly—and led him toward the ring, the city’s pulse beating around them like a second heartbeat.                  Edit  Copy  Share

Andrej understood the rules the moment the arena master barked them out: simple endurance, no weapons, no tricks beyond the body’s own cunning. Victory came by knockout, submission, or forcing the opponent beyond the low stone ring. Skill mattered, but stubbornness mattered more. And in stubbornness, Andrej was a master.

When he stripped to the loincloth, he did it slowly, deliberately, keeping his silver eyes locked on Rimmon the entire time. Rimmon leaned against a pillar at the edge of the crowd, arms crossed, mouth curved in that small, vicious smile that said *I see you, and I like what I see*. Andrej felt the weight of that gaze like a hand on his skin—hot, unblinking, possessive.

The arena master tossed him a small clay bottle of oil. Andrej caught it without looking away from Rimmon. He poured a generous amount into his palm, then spread it methodically across his arms, his shoulders, his chest. The oil gleamed under the midday sun, turning his pale skin darker than usual, highlighting every ridge of muscle earned from years of hauling hides, lifting crates, carrying Rimmon across salt flats. The crowd murmured—some appreciative, some envious, a few openly hungry. Andrej ignored them all. He was oiling himself for one audience only.

*Imperial people are definitely kinky,* he thought, half-amused, half-aroused by the ritual of it. The oil smelled faintly of olives and myrrh, slick and warm against his skin. He worked it into his thighs last, slow strokes that made Rimmon’s smile sharpen, his eyes darken.

His first opponent was named Claudius—perhaps four years older than Andrej, shorter, leaner, built for speed and agility rather than endurance. Imperial coloring, quick feet, the kind of man who danced around opponents until they tired. Andrej planted his feet in the center of the ring and simply refused to move.

Claudius circled, feinted, lunged—trying grips, trying sweeps, trying to unbalance him. Andrej let the man exhaust himself against an immovable object. Every shove, every twist, every attempt to hook a leg met the same quiet, unyielding resistance. Sweat poured down Claudius’s face, his breaths coming shorter, his movements slowing.

When the other man finally faltered—just a fraction, just enough—Andrej moved.

He stepped forward once, twice, closed the distance in a heartbeat. His hands found Claudius’s hips the same way he’d once wrestled a stubborn hide—or, more recently, a flirtatious Rimmon. Firm. Certain. Inevitable. With a single, skillful twist of his torso and a powerful shove, he sent Claudius stumbling backward, feet skidding across the sand, until he crossed the ring’s invisible line and landed hard on his backside outside the stones.

The crowd erupted.

Cheers, curses, coins changing hands, voices shouting in half a dozen tongues:  
“Look at that guy—what a beast of a man!”  
“Again! Put him up again!”  
“Who’s next?”

Andrej stood in the center of the ring, chest heaving, oil and sweat gleaming on his skin, silver eyes lifting to find Rimmon at the barrier.  
Rimmon wasn’t clapping. He wasn’t shouting.  
He was simply watching, lips parted, eyes dark and bright at once, like a man who’d just witnessed something sacred and profane all at once.

The arena master tossed another small purse of copper at Andrej’s feet—winnings from the first bout—then jerked his head toward the next challenger, preparing on the sidelines.

Andrej bent, scooped up the purse, and walked to the edge where Rimmon waited.

He moved up close, as close as he dared in this public space.

“You wanted to see me oiled and glistening,” he murmured. “Enjoying the view?”

Rimmon’s breath hitched. His hand came up, fingers trailing once down Andrej’s slick chest—slow, deliberate, claiming.  

“Immensely,” he whispered back, voice rough. “But don’t get too cocky, ox. You still have to win enough for dinner. And a washhouse. And maybe—*maybe*—a room tonight.”

Andrej’s hand caught Rimmon’s wrist, his thumb searching for his pulse.  

“Then keep watching,” he said, his thumb caressing the sensitive skin. “Because I’m not stopping until we have everything we need.”

He finally released Rimmon, stepped back into the ring, and faced the next challenger.                  Edit  Copy  Share
