Andrej’s name was announced again, the voice of the arena master booming over the rising murmur of the crowd. Two more bouts. Two more chances. The purse from the first win already weighed heavy at his belt—copper coins clinking with every step—but it wasn’t enough. Not yet.
The second opponent was a wiry youth named Lysander, barely older than Andrej himself, quick as a lizard, all elbows and knees. Lysander danced around him, feinting left, right, trying to hook a leg or slip behind. Andrej planted his feet in the center of the sand, oil-slick skin gleaming under the sun, and waited. Lysander tired faster than Claudius had. When the opening came—a fraction too long between lunges—Andrej stepped in, caught him around the waist in a bear hug that lifted him clean off the ground, and simply walked him backward until Lysander’s heels crossed the line. The crowd roared louder this time. Coins rained into the sand. Someone shouted “Silver-Eyes! Silver-Eyes!” like it was already a name.
The third opponent was bigger—taller than Andrej, but less broad shoulders, a man named Drakon who moved like he’d been born fighting. Drakon tried power first: bull-rushes, shoulder checks, trying to drive Andrej straight out. Andrej absorbed each impact, feet sliding only inches before he reset, refusing to give ground. When Drakon finally overcommitted—lunging too far forward—Andrej sidestepped, hooked an arm around Drakon’s neck, and used the man’s own momentum to spin him out of the ring in a controlled, almost elegant throw.
Then the grand finale: “Pontodoros! A Veteran of the Gymnasium and the Sea alike!” Andrej’s pulse pounded in his ears, his pouch already heavy from coins paid with blood and sweat. Not enough. Never enough. The crowd’s murmur swelled, a living thing, hungry for blood or spectacle—it didn’t matter which.
Pontodoros stepped into the ring. Older, broader, a mountain of muscle and silver-threaded beard. He moved like a man who knew the sand beneath his feet, the weight of his own body, the exact moment to strike. No flourish. No taunt. Just the slow, deliberate roll of his shoulders, the crack of his knuckles—pop, pop—like a promise.
Andrej wiped sweat from his brow, salt stinging his eyes. The sun beat down, turning the oil on his skin to fire. He planted his feet, knees bent, hands loose. The crowd’s roar faded to a distant howl, the world narrowing to the space between them. Pontodoros’s smile was small, almost kind. “Amusement,” the arena master had called it.
Andrej’s stomach twisted. This was no amusement.
They circled. Pontodoros’s breath came even, steady, while Andrej’s lungs burned. The older man’s stance was deceptive—feet shoulder-width, weight balanced, arms loose, as if he had all the time in the world. Andrej feinted left, testing. Pontodoros didn’t bite. Just watched, dark eyes unreadable, tracking Andrej like a predator sizing up prey.
Andrej lunged first, fingers aiming for Pontodoros’s wrist. Their skin met—oil-slick, hot—and for a heartbeat, Andrej’s pulse stuttered. Pontodoros’s forearm was corded with muscle, the hair rough against his palm. Then Pontodoros sidestepped, fluid as water, and Andrej’s momentum carried him forward—too far. A hand clamped around his waist, fingers digging into the dip of his spine, lifting him an inch off the sand. The crowd gasped. Andrej twisted, but Pontodoros’s grip was iron, his body a wall of heat and sweat.
Andrej landed hard, sand spraying. Pain flared in his ribs, but beneath it, something else—a thrill, sharp and unwanted. Pontodoros loomed over him, not gloating, just breathing, slow and controlled. His gaze flicked down to Andrej’s mouth, then back to his eyes. 
“Again,” he murmured, voice rough as gravel.
Andrej refused to chase. He planted his feet, knees bent, and waited. Pontodoros came at him like a storm, shoulder first, driving him back. Their bodies collided—chest to chest, oil and sweat sealing them together. Andrej gritted his teeth, the scent of Pontodoros filling his nose: salt, leather, and something darker, muskier. He pushed back, muscles straining, but Pontodoros was relentless, his breath hot against Andrej’s ear.
“You’re strong,” Pontodoros growled, “but you waste it.” 
His hand slid up Andrej’s back, fingers pressing into the tension there, almost a caress. Andrej’s skin burned where he touched. He surged forward, trying to hook a leg, but Pontodoros anticipated it. With a twist of his hips, he sent Andrej flying. The sand rushed up to meet him, the impact knocking the breath from his lungs.
Andrej rolled, gasping. Pontodoros stood over him, legs braced, watching him with something like approval—or hunger. The crowd’s roar was a distant thing. All Andrej could hear was his own heartbeat, the rasp of Pontodoros’s breathing.
Andrej lunged again—desperate, reckless. Pontodoros caught him mid-motion, arms locking around his ribs in a crushing grip. Andrej’s vision blurred, the arena tilting. He was lifted, twisted—airborne—then dropped. The impact sent a crack through his bones. Sand filled his mouth, gritty and bitter.
The crowd’s roar was a thunderclap. Pontodoros’s hands were under his arms now, hauling him up. Andrej’s heels crossed the line, and the transition from soft sand to the sun-baked stone of the perimeter bit into his soles like a jagged blade.
Silence. Then Pontodoros’s voice, low and rough: “Good fight, boy.”
Pontodoros offered a hand. Andrej hesitated, then took it. The older man’s grip was firm, calloused, pulling him up with surprising gentleness. Their faces were inches apart, Pontodoros’s dark eyes searching his. “You’ve got heart. And strength,” he said, voice low. His thumb brushed Andrej’s wrist, just once. “Come find me in the baths if you want to learn how to use both.”
The crowd’s cheers were a storm, but Andrej barely heard them. His ribs ached, his tongue throbbed where he’d bitten it. The taste of copper filled his mouth. Pontodoros turned away, but not before Andrej caught the flicker of a smile, the promise in his eyes.
Andrej stood, legs unsteady, the weight of the purses suddenly meaningless. The heat of Pontodoros’s hand still lingered on his skin.
An attendant grunted beside him, poking at a cut on his shoulder. The cloth was cool but the water was stinking of vinegar. “No concussion. You’ll bruise, but nothing’s broken. Go wash. The basins are that way.”
Andrej nodded, his chest heaving. Every breath felt like drawing air through a wet wool blanket. He looked for Rimmon.
Rimmon was already there, his face pale against the vibrant colors of the crowd. When he reached Andrej, he didn’t touch him at first; he hovered, his hands twitching as if he were trying to map the damage without making it worse.
“You’re bleeding,” Rimmon said, his voice tight with a familiar, protective anger. 
“It’s nothing.” Andrej wanted to laugh. Of course I’m bleeding. But all he managed was a shaky smile. The skin of his cheek felt tight and hot, already beginning to swell. “I won three. Lost the last. But look, we’ve got coin now.” He pressed the heavy leather pouch into Rimmon’s hands.
Rimmon ignored the money. His fingers settled on Andrej’s jaw, surprisingly warm, turning his face toward the light. “You’re an idiot,” he whispered. The thumb that brushed his bruised cheek was so gentle it made the ache in Andrej’s ribs feel twice as heavy.
The walk to the basins was a blur of heavy limbs and the smell of drying blood. The water in the cold tanks was brutal. When Andrej ducked his head under, the shock silenced the ringing in his ears, replacing it with a sharp, crystalline clarity. He scrubbed at the oil and sand, watching pink-tinged water swirl into the drains. 
Rimmon hovered nearby, arms crossed, his usual warmth replaced by a tight, worried silence. Andrej caught his eye, tried to smile, but Rimmon only scowled. “You let him throw you,” he muttered. “You could’ve won.”
Andrej opened his mouth to argue, but a shadow fell over them.
Pontodoros stood there, a towel slung over one shoulder, his oiled skin gleaming. “The cold water is for the poor,” he said, “or those who prefer punishment to pleasure. Today, you’ve earned more.” He looked at Rimmon, a slow, assessing sweep of his eyes, then back to Andrej. “Come, Ironborn. Enjoy the hot steam.”
Andrej’s breath hitched at the word. It was tugging at something deep inside him, something that had been buried under sand for a long time.
Rimmon’s jaw tightened. Andrej hesitated, aware of the social line only he was being asked to cross. The cold basins were for everyone; the hot rooms were for patrons, veterans, men of status. Pontodoros was offering him a place among them.
“I—” Andrej glanced at Rimmon, who looked away, lips pressed thin.
Pontodoros’s expression didn’t change. “If you prefer the cold, no offense taken.” He turned, as if to leave, then paused. “But if you want to learn how to fight more like other Ironborn…” He let the words hang, then disappeared behind a steam-wreathed curtain. He knows, Andrej thought. He knows I don’t know what an Ironborn is, and he noticed it makes my blood boil.
“We don’t need his charity,” Rimmon snapped.
Andrej looked at the steam-wreathed curtain where Pontodoros had disappeared. He could feel the cold water leaching the last of his strength. “It’s not charity. It’s an invitation.”
“To what?” Rimmon’s voice was sharp.
“To an answer,” Andrej said quietly. Forgive me, he thought, and stepped toward the heat.
Inside, the air was a thick, humid embrace that smelled of eucalyptus and wood-smoke. It was a world of soft edges. The floor was smooth mosaic, slick under his bare feet. He saw the guard in the eagle-embossed chestplate, then the two men in the corner pool, lounging in a way that made Andrej’s heart skip. They were open. Unafraid.
Pontodoros gestured to a sunken bath where the water rolled in gentle ripples. “Sit. Then we’ll talk.” 
He didn’t wait for an answer, stripping off his loincloth with effortless confidence and stepping into the water. Andrej followed suit, though with less grace, the heat seeping into his bones the moment he sank beneath the surface.
Guilt caught up to him, a bitter tang on his tongue. Rimmon wasn’t here. Andrej would have to come up with a fitting atonement. But first, this. 
Pontodoros leaned back against the bath’s edge, watching Andrej through half-lidded eyes. “You fight like a man who’s used to losing,” he said. “But you don’t have to.”
Andrej wiped a hand over his face, clearing the sweat. “I usually don’t fight, so it is rare for me the lose.” 
“Is that so?” Pontodoros’s smile was knowing. “Why did you let me throw you?”
Andrej’s chest tightened. He glanced toward the curtains, the courtyard beyond, where the cold basins sat like a rebuke. “I didn’t let you.”
Pontodoros chuckled. “You are a horrible liar. So…” He reached for a clay jug of watered wine, pouring two cups, handing one to Andrej. “You seemed quite confused when I addressed you as Ironborn. How come?”
Andrej looked down at his arms. In the golden light of the steam room, the fine blond hairs shimmered. He saw the contrast of his pale shoulders against the darker tan of his forearms—the mark of a life spent in the sun, but born of a different soil. “I was raised in the desert,” he admitted. 
Pontodoros moved closer, his hand landing on Andrej’s shoulder. The heat of the man’s palm matched the water. “The desert? With the Luedina? Oh, you poor thing. No wonder they let a gem like you go to waste.” He took a sip of wine, his eyes fixed on Andrej’s. “Eat with me at sunset. I own a taverna: The Prosperous Voyage. If you don’t come, there’ll be no debt between us.” 
Andrej’s fingers tightened around his cup, he broke the intimate moment, now looking back toward the courtyard, where Rimmon—furious, hurt—was waiting outside. He felt the weight of Pontodoros’s hand on his shoulder, the promise in his voice.
Pontodoros followed his gaze. “The other one,” he said. “What is he to you?”
Andrej’s throat closed. The words my home. My everything lodged in his throat. He cleared it, forcing himself to meet Pontodoros’s eyes again. “My partner,” he said, the word heavy, final. “Meaning I’d like you to extend your invitation to us both.”
A moment of silence stretched, thick with the smell of eucalyptus. Pontodoros studied him, then gave a slow, respectful nod. He settled back into a more conversational, easy grace. “I see. Well, then I am happy to have him as well.”
Rimmon was drying himself with sharp, angry motions by the cold basins when Andrej found him. His back was to Andrej, shoulders rigid.
“Pontodoros invited us to dinner,” Andrej said.

The cold water in the basin was a curse and a benediction—sharp enough to silence the fire in his back, cruel enough to remind him he was still bleeding. Rimmon scrubbed at his skin with a rough cloth, each stroke punishing the flesh that had failed him. His reflection in the water was a stranger: pale beneath the dust, eyes too dark, mouth a flat line where a smile should be.*He left.* The thought was a splinter under his nail, impossible to ignore. *He walked away with Pontodoros and didn’t look back.*Rimmon had watched them go—the older man’s hand on Andrej’s shoulder, the easy familiarity, the offer of hot steam and belonging. He’d seen the way Pontodoros had looked at Andrej, like a man appraising a weapon he wanted to wield. And he’d seen the way Andrej had hesitated, just for a heartbeat, before following.That heartbeat had been enough.The pain in his back was nothing compared to the cold knot in his gut. *I’m not enough,* he thought, the words acid. *I’m a mess he has to carry, and Pontodoros is offering him a chance to set the burden down.*He twisted the cloth in his hands, wringing it so hard his knuckles went white. Water dripped onto the stone, dark as tears. He wanted to carve something—wanted the comfort of wood giving way to the shape of his fury. But he had no knife, no block, no way to bleed the feeling out except through his skin.*You’re a fool,* he told his reflection. *You handed him everything—your heart, your name, your broken pieces—and he still walked away.*The sound of footsteps made him go rigid. He didn’t turn. Didn’t want to see Andrej’s face, soft with steam and secrets he hadn’t been invited to share.“Pontodoros invited us to dinner,” Andrej said behind him, voice careful as a man stepping over broken glass.*Us.* The word landed like a blow. Rimmon’s hands stilled. He stared at the water, at the ripples his own breathing made on the surface.“Did he?” he asked, and his voice came out flat, stripped of all its poetry. “How generous.”He turned, slowly, letting Andrej see the full force of his anger—his naked chest still pink from the cold, his shoulders tight with pain, his eyes burning with the betrayal he was trying not to name.Andrej stood there, water beading on his skin, the silver lining of his heritage stark against the sun. He looked… uncertain. Like a boy who had just realized the game had real stakes.“He invited you,” Rimmon corrected, each word precise as a cut. “I was an afterthought.”Andrej’s jaw tightened. “I told him you were my partner. That the invitation was for us both. Or it was nothing.”The silence stretched. Rimmon wanted to stay angry—wanted to wrap the fury around himself like armor, wanted to make Andrej pay for that moment of hesitation. But the truth was there, naked in Andrej’s eyes: he had chosen. He had *chosen* the burden.Rimmon’s shoulders sagged. The anger drained out of him, leaving only exhaustion and a humiliating, desperate relief.“You shouldn’t have had to ask,” he whispered, the words cracking. “You shouldn’t have to fight for me to be included.”Andrej stepped closer, close enough that Rimmon could smell the steam on his skin, the faint scent of eucalyptus, the musk of a man who had just been touched by someone who wasn’t him.“Maybe not,” Andrej said, his voice low and raw. “But I will. Every time. Until you stop expecting to be left behind.”He reached out—not to touch, but to offer. His palm was open, empty, waiting.Rimmon looked at the hand, at the calluses and the rope burns and the faint tremor that said *I’m scared too.* Then he looked at Andrej’s face, stripped of all its stoicism, and saw the truth: this wasn’t about Pontodoros. This was about two boys learning that love wasn’t a performance—it was a choice you made every time you came back.Rimmon took the hand, but slowly, letting his fingers slide against Andrej’s palm in a touch that was half-caress, half-surrender.“Then let’s go to dinner,” he said, and his voice was still rough, but it was his again—not the heir’s, not the ghost’s, just Rimmon’s. “And remind Pontodoros that the Ironborn doesn’t travel alone.”He let Andrej pull him close, let their foreheads rest together for a moment, breathing the same air.“I’m still angry,” he whispered.“I know,” Andrej murmured back. “You can be angry and still hold my hand.”And they stood there, in the cold basins of a city that didn’t know their names, holding each other’s broken pieces and calling them whole.                  3/3

Andrej looked down at the pile of clothing next to their feet. They were surrounded by the marble of an ancient empire, invited to join in, and they looked like they had been dragged through the guts of the world. Their tunics were salt-rimed, stained with the gray dust of the arena and the yellow clays of the road, their sandals were coming apart, Andrej's headscarf was no longer the color of undyed linen, but yellowed.
“Maybe it is time to spend some of our new coin,” Andrej said, his voice regaining its strength.
Rimmon’s eyes flicked to the heavy pouch at Andrej's belt. A spark of his usual light returned, replacing the cold shadows of the basin. “We look like beggars at a king’s feast. If we walk into The Prosperous Voyage like this, they’ll seat us in the kitchen with the goats.”
They dressed quickly, pulling on their tattered linens. The fabric felt abrasive against Andrej’s newly washed skin, a reminder of the life they were trying to outrun.
The market district of Mergos was a riot of color that made the desert look monochromatic. Here, the “Nova Prima” style was on full display—long, flowing tunics of fine wool draped with structured, almost Elven-inspired vests that featured high collars and intricate silver thread.
They found a shop tucked between a spice merchant and a jeweler. The sign was a simple bolt of violet silk hanging over the door. Inside, the air was cool and smelled of pressed lavender.
“By Kypris,” a bright, female voice lilted from the back, “look what the sun dragged in.” 
A woman stepped out from behind a rack of deep-green mantles. She was draped in layers of sheer, shimmering gauze, her hair piled high in an architectural feat of pins and pearls. She didn’t look like a shopkeeper; she looked like a predator bred for silk. Her eyes swept over them, lingering on Andrej’s silver eyes before settling, with a glint of curiosity, on Rimmon’s golden skin and wild, dark curls.
“Two sun-scorched gods have walked into my humble shop,” she purred, gliding toward them. Her hands, tipped with stained henna, didn't touch, but they hovered close enough for Andrej to feel the heat of her. “Or is it one god and his guardian?”
Internally, Andrej’s blood simmered. He hated the way she categorized them, relegated him to the shadow of the 'god.' But he kept his face a mask of granite, his posture loose and deceptively bored, even as his pulse thrummed against his collar.
Rimmon drew himself up with a dramatic inhalation, his vanity—always his most reliable defense—sparking to life. He made sure to loom impressively over the woman. “We need something for dinner with a man of status,” he declared, his voice regaining its melodic, sing-song lilt.
“A man of status,” she repeated, her gaze traveling down the line of Rimmon’s throat. “Well, you have come to the right hands, let me see.” 
She assessed Andrej first, her professional eye measuring the width of his shoulders. With a faint tch, she dismissed the finer linens on display and vanished into the back, returning with a tunic of thick, creamy wool that smelled of lanolin and a bundle of thick, brown cloth. “For the Guardian something practical. And for this one...”
She reached out, her fingers inches from Rimmon’s hair. “Is it true what the sailors say? That the desert-born are lions in the dark?” She flashed a sharp, white smile. “I’ve always wanted to see a desert lion up close. See if the mane is as soft as it looks.”
Rimmon saw the minute tightening of Andrej’s jaw—the tiny, delicious fracture in his “cool” exterior. He leaned into the woman’s space, almost brushing her hennaed fingers with his temple. He didn't look at her; he looked at Andrej, his eyes dancing with a wicked, taunting light.
“It’s softer,” Rimmon whispered, his gaze locked on his friend. “But the claws are real. Aren't they, Andrej?”
Andrej felt the hook sink in and twist. He wanted to haul Rimmon back by the scruff of his neck, away from her scent and her appraisal. Instead, he simply shed his old tunic and put on the new garment, his expression as cold as a mountain stream, while his mind screamed at the reckless, preening fool beside him.
The woman laughed, a sound like silver coins hitting a tray. For Rimmon, she pulled a smaller tunic from the shelf. “The same foundation. But for the God...” Her fingers trailed along a shelf before landing on a folded bolt of fabric the color of fresh blood. She shook it out with a snap, and the room seemed to brighten. It was fine-combed wool, lighter than Andrej's, meant for show, not snow. “The himation,” she said, the word a purr. “In white, you are a citizen. In this?” She let the lush fabric pool at Rimmon's feet. “You are a poem. And every poem... requires a certain deliberate carelessness in its composition.” She gestured to a marble bench behind a freestanding screen painted with herons. “Your travel dust will offend the linen.” She Pointed at Rimmon. “Come with me.”
The new tunic sat snug across Andrej’s chest, but he enjoyed wearing a fresh, clean garment for once. He was trying to remember the last time he bought himself clothing, but his thoughts got interrupted by the soft rustle of Rimmon's garments behind the screen, followed by the woman's low, appreciative hum. It wasn't a sound for him. He stared at the wall, listening to the intimate silence of the new cloth being lifted, the whisper as it settled over another man's skin, the barely-there gasp of a pin finding its hold.
Andrej let out a grunt and pulled the umber Paenula—a hooded, bell-shaped cloak—over his shoulders, fastening the singular button with trembling hands. I guess this is what I deserve for the hot baths, he thought to himself. 
When they both emerged from behind the screen, Rimmon looked... respectable. The crimson wool was arranged around his hips, over his left shoulder, and finally flowed over his forearm in the classic, stately curve. With a provocative glance towards Andrej, she hooked a single finger into the fabric near Rimmon's chest and gave it the slightest, most deliberate tug. The elegant line broke; a fold loosened over his shoulder, exposing a bit more of the white tunic beneath, creating a glimpse of skin and a sense of dynamic, tempting disorder. “There,” she whispered, stepping back. “Now it is alive. It has a story. It says, I do not need to try... but you may try for me.” 
Rimmon examined the crimson drape over his forearm, then lifted his gaze to Andrej. A slow, cat-like smile spread across his face. ”Hm. It seems some garments are made for standing guard,” he said, his voice a honeyed murmur. “And others are made for being... unwrapped.”
He’s enjoying this, Andrej realized, the jealousy curdling into something hotter, sharper. He wants to see if I’ll snap. Andrej forced a slow, even breath through his nose and crossed his arms. The scent of lavender was suddenly cloying. He gave Rimmon a single, flat look that said, I see your game. And you will lose. The muscle in his jaw, however, had gone rock-hard.
“Good,” she said, clapping her hands once, deciding the flirtation had served its purpose. ”Because dinner with a man of status is not about cloth. It’s about stories. And your feet are telling the wrong one.”
She quickly assessed their feet, then swept to a cabinet and produced two pairs of sandals. One pair was quickly shoved into Andrej’s hands, but when she handed the second pair to Rimmon, her fingers trailed over his palm, a deliberate, lingering contact.
They took the sandals, the new leather fine and supple compared to the cracked, salt-bitten soles of their old ones. Andrej sat on a low stool, his movements jerky as he laced the thin straps up his calves, his focus entirely on the floor to avoid looking at the vendor’s hands on Rimmon. Beside him, Rimmon didn't sit; he balanced with the easy grace of a dancer, sliding his feet into the cradles of leather and tightening the buckles with a flourish. They stood together—the transition from dusty outcasts to men of the city complete from the ground up.
“City-sandals,” the woman said. “And remember, a Himation is an art form. It requires... constant attention to keep the drape just so.” She leaned in, her voice dropping to a sultry murmur intended for only Rimmon to hear. “If you require any assistance in adjusting your Himation in the future, Lion... you know exactly where to find me.”
Andrej let out a breath that could have been a laugh. He let his hands fall to his sides. They were open, loose. A fighter’s calm. Inside, he was mapping the fastest way to put himself between Rimmon and this woman, between Rimmon and the door, between Rimmon and his own damned, provocative smile.
Then, louder, the vendor tapped Rimmon’s new sandals with one fingernail. ”And you—before you dine—you will visit the barber three doors down.”
Rimmon’s brows arched. He tossed his hair back, a full, theatrical gesture. ”My hair is perfect.”
“A lion is a noble beast,” she said sweetly, not looking at Rimmon, but at Andrej, as if they were two parents discussing an unruly child, ”but a wild mane belongs in the waste, not at a table of politics.” She now addressed Andrej directly: ”Please take him.”
“And if I don’t?” Andrej asked, his own voice dangerously low.
Her smile sharpened. She finally looked at Rimmon, her appraisal open, shameless. ”Then I’ll keep him.” She gestured around the shop at the headless mannequins draped in finery. ”I have displays. I have very strong pins. I have all the time in the world to admire a living sculpture.”
Rimmon’s grin turned truly wicked. He played with the crimson tail over his shoulder with a flamboyant flourish, looking at Andrej with a silent dare. 
“Careful,” Rimmon told the woman, his voice a playful chime. “Sculptures stand still. I am very well able to move.”
“Then move to the barber,” she laughed, shooing them toward the light of the street. “Before I decide to pin you to the wall myself. But first”
They stood together, the transformation nearly complete, but the discarded remnants of their journey remained on the floor. Andrej reached down and gathered his leather belt and buckled it over the new tunica.
Rimmon watched him, then reached into the heap of their old travel-dusted clothing. He plucked out his own worn pouch and the old brown headscarf—the one Andrej had made for him. With a look that was uncharacteristically solemn, he stepped close to Andrej and tucked both items into the travel pack over Andrej’s shoulder. He patted the leather, a silent surrender of his burdens, entrusting his few memories to Andrej’s keeping. Their eyes met for a brief, quiet second, something softer passing between them—a silent vow that no matter the finery, they were still the men who had bled on the road together.
Andrej’s hand closed around Rimmon’s upper arm—not roughly, but with an undeniable, guiding force. It was the grip of a guardian, a handler, a man at the very end of his patience. He steered the living sculpture, the poem in red wool, out into the sun-drenched chaos of the market, the vendor’s silver-coin laughter and the soft clink of freshly counted payment chasing them out the door.

Andrej felt the wine burn down his throat, felt Rimmon's hand in his—still trembling slightly, still raw from the archway—and tried to hold onto the certainty he'd carved into that moment. *I want you. To hell with respectable.* He'd meant it. Gods, he'd meant it with every fiber of his body.

But sitting across from Pontodoros now, watching the veteran's easy command of the space, the way servers nodded to him without being called, the way even the rowdy sailors kept their voices down near his table—Andrej felt something shift. Not doubt. Not yet. Just... awareness.

This man had answers. About the Ironborn. About why Andrej could take hits that should have shattered bone and walk away with bruises. About where he came from.

*And he wants to give them to me.*

"Eat," Pontodoros said, leaning back in his chair with the casual grace of a man who'd never had to fight for a place to sit. He gestured to the platters before them—grilled fish still steaming, bread warm enough to release its scent when broken. "You both look half-starved."

Andrej realized how true that was. They'd eaten nothing since a hurried breakfast of flatbread and dates before entering the city. His stomach growled in response, and he reached for the fish without ceremony, tearing into it with his hands. Beside him, Rimmon took bread and dipped it in oil, his movements more delicate but no less hungry.

Pontodoros watched them eat for a moment, then poured more wine into their cups. "You've made your position clear, Silver-Eyes. A package deal. I respect that." His eyes flicked to Rimmon, assessing. "Loyalty is rare. Fierce loyalty even more so."

Andrej felt Rimmon tense beside him. Felt the way his partner's fingers twitched, reaching for his cup instead of Andrej's hand.

"But tell me," Pontodoros continued, his voice taking on a pedagogical tone—like a teacher posing a question he already knew the answer to. "In the arena today, when you fought those three boys before me—what were you thinking about?"

Andrej swallowed his mouthful of fish, the taste of it suddenly too vivid on his tongue. "Winning."

"No." Pontodoros shook his head, a small smile playing at his lips. "You were thinking about coin, yes. About what you could buy with it. About *him.*" A nod toward Rimmon. "But also—and I saw this clearly—you were thinking about impressing him. Every throw, every pin, you'd glance to the edge of the arena to see if he was watching. To see if he saw you win."

The words landed like stones in still water. Because they were *true.* Andrej felt heat creep up his neck—not shame, but the uncomfortable recognition of being seen. He *had* been showing off. Between each fight, still breathing hard, he'd looked for Rimmon. Wanted to see that sharp, hungry look in his eyes. Wanted Rimmon to see what his body could do, what it had done to Rimmon just last night in the dark—

Andrej took a long drink of wine to cover the memory.

"That's not a weakness," Pontodoros said, reading Andrej's face with unnerving accuracy. "It's youth. It's passion. But it's also a distraction. You fight like a man performing for an audience. And men who perform..." He paused, leaning forward. "They make mistakes."

Andrej's jaw tightened. "I won."

"You won the first three," Pontodoros agreed. "But when you fought me? You hesitated." His voice dropped, became almost intimate. "I felt it—the moment your grip should have tightened, but didn't. The moment you could have slipped my hold, but chose to fall instead. Why?"

Andrej's mouth went dry. Because he *had* hesitated. In that split second when Pontodoros had closed the distance, when the veteran's breath had been hot against his neck, when their bodies had been pressed chest-to-chest in the grapple—something had flickered through Andrej.
It had cost him the match.

"You outmatched me," Andrej said aloud, his voice rough. "Taking the fall meant I could walk away and still get your respect."

Pontodoros's smile widened, but it wasn't mocking. It was knowing. "Perhaps. Or perhaps, Silver-Eyes, you were distracted by something you didn't expect to feel. The body has its own language, doesn't it? Its own... curiosities."

Andrej heard Rimmon take in a sharp breath.

Pontodoros took a leisurely bite of fish, chewed, swallowed. Let the moment stretch. "Strategy. Survival. Distraction. The same forces that got you across the desert alive—and made you surrender in my arms." He paused, let the double meaning hang in the air like smoke. "But, what if you didn't have to survive anymore? What if you could *thrive*?"

Andrej felt Rimmon's hand tighten in his—a warning, or a plea, he couldn't tell.

Pontodoros leaned forward. "I told you in the baths that I could help you understand what you are. I've fought other Ironborn, Andrej. You could become unstoppable. But that requires space. It requires focus. It requires a man who can commit fully to the path."

"I am committed," Andrej said, and his hand squeezed Rimmon's hard enough to hurt. "We both are."

"To what?" Pontodoros asked, and the question hung in the air like a blade. "To each other? That's beautiful. That's poetry." His eyes flicked to Rimmon. "But poetry doesn't solve hunger, doesn't quench thirst, doesn't build a future. It doesn't answer the question of who you are and where you come from."

Andrej felt something cold settle in his chest. Because the archway had been about defiance. About choosing Rimmon over respectability. But Pontodoros wasn't asking him to choose respectability—he was asking him to choose *knowledge.* To choose *purpose.*
And that was so much harder to refuse.

Pontodoros watched Andrej with the patience of a man who had won this argument a hundred times before. "So, Rimmon," he said, turning his attention to the poet. "Andrej tells me you have a gift for words. That you see the world as a series of stanzas. Tell me, how does this tavern scan? Is it a tragedy or a comedy?"

Andrej felt Rimmon's silence like a drawn bow. He pressed his thigh against Rimmon's under the table—an anchor, a reminder: *I'm here. We're here. Together.*
But even as he did it, he wondered if Pontodoros could see the performance in the gesture.

Rimmon leaned forward, deliberately allowing the messy, ruined drape of his crimson himation—the one Andrej had bunched and mussed in the archway—to slide further off his shoulder, exposing his dark, bronze skin.

"It's a farce," Rimmon said, his voice smooth as the wine. "Because you're pretending to be a patron, and he's pretending to be a guest, and I'm pretending I don't know that you've been looking at his throat for the last ten minutes."

Andrej's grip on Rimmon's hand tightened to a painful crush. *Gods, Rimmon. You are right, but also, we are both his guests.*

Pontodoros froze, his cup halfway to his lips. The tavern around them seemed to hold its breath. The veteran's eyes darkened, but not with anger—with something colder. More analytical.

Like Rimmon had just proven something.

But Rimmon reached out and took Andrej's cup. He didn't drink from it. Instead, he dipped a finger into the dark liquid and slowly, with the agonizing deliberation of a man who knew exactly who was watching, traced a line of red wine down the side of his own neck, ending where the red cloth met his skin.

"There," Rimmon whispered. "Now the 'poetry' has a bit of blood in it. Is that scandalous enough for your table, Pontodoros? Or should I continue on with the chest?"

*This is what you wanted,* he told himself. *This is what you chose in the archway. Chaos. Defiance. Him.*

But sitting here, watching Pontodoros's cold assessment, watching the servers exchange knowing glances, watching the respectable patrons lean away from their table like they were contagious—Andrej felt something crack.

He reached up, his hand blunt and heavy, and settled it on the back of Rimmon's neck, his thumb grazing the wine-wet skin. The contact felt like a claim—like planting a flag in contested ground.

But even as he made it, Andrej wondered if he was claiming Rimmon, or just proving to Pontodoros that he was part of the play.

Pontodoros didn't flinch at the provocation. He set his cup down with a soft click that seemed to silence their corner of the tavern. His eyes, however, didn't hold hunger for Rimmon's display. They held something colder—satisfaction. Like Rimmon had just confirmed a hypothesis.

"Bold," Pontodoros conceded, his voice a low rumble meant only for their table. "A dramatic flourish. It reminds me of the boys who used to paint their faces before battle—thinking ferocity was a color you could wear."

He leaned forward, his gaze pinning Andrej, deliberately excluding Rimmon from the weight of his attention.

Andrej felt the shift like a door closing. The veteran wasn't interested in Rimmon's performance. He was interested in Andrej's *tolerance* for it.

"But you, Andrej. You don't wear your strength. You are it. It is in your bones. I saw it in the sand today—four fights, and you won three of them before you even met me. You have the makings of a true—" He used a word Andrej didn't know, something with hard consonants that sounded like armor clanking, "*hoplites*—a man who stands in the line. But you are fighting with one arm tied behind your back."

Pontodoros's eyes flicked to Andrej's hand on Rimmon's neck, then back to his face.

"Because you are using it to hold him steady."

The words cut deeper than any throw in the arena. Because they were *true.* How many times had Andrej caught Rimmon before he fell? How many times had he stepped between Rimmon and consequence? How many times had he translated Rimmon's sharp tongue into something the world could swallow?

*Every time,* Andrej realized. *Since the oasis. Since we fled. Since he chose me and I chose to carry him.*

"He doesn't need holding," Andrej said, but his voice lacked conviction.

Pontodoros smiled—not unkindly. "No? Then why is your hand on his neck right now?"

Andrej's hand tensed on Rimmon's neck. He felt Rimmon go rigid beside him—felt the accusation land like a blow.

*Because I was trying to protect him. Because we are in this together. Because—*

"Because," Pontodoros continued, his voice taking on a pedagogical tone, "you know that the world doesn't reward chaos. It rewards control. And you've been trying to control him since the moment you arrived, because you understand—perhaps better than he does—that wildness without direction is just noise."

The veteran shifted then, his tone becoming intimate, confessional. "In my youth, I was *erastes* to a young warrior from Thracia."

Andrej tried to parse the word. *Erastes.* It sounded important. Formal. Like a title.

"A mentor," Pontodoros clarified, reading Andrej's confusion. "A guide. An older lover who shapes the younger. I saw the raw ore in him, as I see it in you. I polished it. I armed it. I taught him how to channel that glorious, brute strength into strategy, into presence. When he walked into a room, men fell silent not because they feared his fists, but because they respected his *arete*—his excellence. I gave him that."
Andrej felt the pull of the offer like a tide. *He could teach me. He could tell about the Ironborn. He could turn this brute strength into something more than just survival.*
His hand was still on Rimmon's neck. He felt the pulse there—too fast, too frantic. Like a bird beating itself against cage bars.
*But what about Rimmon?*
Pontodoros's eyes finally flicked to Rimmon, not with challenge, but with a dismissive, pitying assessment. "What do you give him, poet? Scenes? Wine-stained collars? A pretty chaos for him to clean up? Is that the purpose of a companion?"
Andrej felt Rimmon's pulse go frantic under his hand, his muscles tensed. Felt the breath catch in his partner's throat.
"I gave him a snake," Rimmon stated simply, clearly confusing the older man.

Pontodoros turned back to Andrej, his voice dropping to a near whisper. "The bond between *erastes* and *eromenos*—mentor and student, lover and beloved—is the highest form of love a city like this understands. It is not a chain. It is an investment. I would invest in you, Andrej. I would teach you to wield your strength like a general, not a laborer. I would introduce you to men who could trace that 'Ironborn' pulse in your veins to its source."
The source. Andrej's breath caught. His entire life had been a question with no answer: Where did I come from? Why am I built like this? What does it mean?
Last night, wrapped around Rimmon in the dark, he hadn't cared. The questions had seemed distant, unimportant compared to the heat of skin on skin, the sound of Rimmon's voice saying his name like a prayer.

Pontodoros paused, letting the offer hang like a blade. Then, as if an afterthought, he added, "Your partner would, of course, have a place. There are always roles for those with... artistic temperaments. Scribes need assistants. Playwrights need muses. He would be comfortable."
Andrej looked at Rimmon. Saw the blood drain from his face, leaving the wine-streak on his neck looking like a fresh, dark wound.

The wine-stain wasn't an accident. The sliding himation wasn't carelessness. The provocation, the deliberate scandal—Rimmon had orchestrated all of it. Just like he'd orchestrated their first night together by pushing and pushing until Andrej's restraint had finally shattered.

He forced a smile—not the wicked, cat-like grin from before, but something sharper. Wounded. "You offer him the source of his blood, and you offer me... a desk? You've misread the meter of this farce, Pontodoros."
Rimmon reached up and slowly, deliberately, wiped the wine from his neck with the back of his hand. He didn't look like a boy playing at ferocity anymore. He looked like a man realizing he was in a cage with a more elegant lock.
"He doesn't hold me up to keep me from falling," Rimmon said, turning to Andrej, his eyes searching the silver of his gaze. "He holds me because without me, he has nothing to fight for. Excellence is a lonely house, veteran. You polished your Thracian warrior, but where is he now? Is he still by your side? Or did he walk away the moment he didn't need your 'investment' anymore?"

Pontodoros didn't move to stop them. He remained seated, his hands folded peacefully over his stomach, the picture of a man who had already won a game they hadn't realized they were playing.
"The reason is wearing a wine-stained collar," Pontodoros repeated, a soft, dry chuckle escaping him. "How romantic. How very... young."
He looked up at Andrej, and for the first time, the warmth was entirely gone from his expression. There was only the cold clarity of a man who had seen this play out a thousand times.
"You think you are protecting him from the world, Silver-Eyes. But look at him."

Pontodoros leaned forward, his voice dropping to a whisper that seemed to slide under Andrej's skin like a needle.
"He doesn't want to be respectable because respectability has rules. He wants to be a mess because as long as he is a mess, you are necessary. You are the one who translates. The one who protects. The questions Pontodoros had planted were spreading like roots, cracking the foundation Andrej had built his entire world on.
Pontodoros picked up his cup and took a slow, leisurely sip.
"Enjoy the night. But remember: a beast in a cage is predictable. A beast that chooses its own cage? That is the one you should fear."
He waved a dismissive hand, already looking away toward the entrance of the tavern as if they had already ceased to exist. "Go. The barber did a fine job, but the hair is already a disaster. It's a shame to waste such good wool on a tragedy."
Andrej turned. Started walking. His hand dropped from Rimmon's elbow entirely now, falling to his side.
Rimmon followed, but for the first time since the archway, since the kiss that had promised *to hell with respectable*, Andrej didn't reach back to make sure he was there.
He just walked.
The words lodged deep, burrowing into the soft, unprotected places where loyalty lived.
*Am I protecting him? Or am I just keeping him dependent?
Am I his lover? Or am I just the only audience he has left?*
The tavern door swung shut behind them. The street outside was dark, the harbor breeze sharp with salt and distant smoke. Andrej could hear Rimmon's breathing beside him—quick, shallow, uncertain.
"Andrej," Rimmon said quietly.
Andrej didn't look at him. He kept walking, his stride long and purposeful, putting distance between them and the Prosperous Voyage. Between them and Pontodoros's cold, calculating offer.
But the distance wasn't just behind them. It was between them now, too.

Rimmon's panic-driven attempt to win Andrej back fails    The user asks: "how does Rimmon plan to win Andrej back?"This is a pivotal moment. Andrej has just been fundamentally shaken by Pontodoros's words. The veteran has planted the seeds of doubt: Is Andrej's protection actually a form of control? Is Rimmon's chaos just a way to keep Andrej tethered? Andrej is now questioning the very foundation of their relationship. He's walking away, not just from the tavern, but emotionally—his hand dropped, he's not looking back. Rimmon is left behind, uncertain, for the first time facing the possibility that his greatest weapon (provocation, chaos, making Andrej *choose* him publicly) might be the very thing that drives Andrej away.Rimmon's plan to win Andrej back cannot be more of the same. It can't be another provocation, another performance. Pontodoros called out his pattern: create chaos, force Andrej to choose, make himself indispensable. If Rimmon does that again, he proves Pontodoros right.His plan must be the opposite: **stillness, honesty, and surrender of control.** He needs to show Andrej that he can be *stable*, that he can be *content* without constant crisis, that he can be a partner not a project. He needs to show that his love isn't predicated on Andrej's need to save him.But this is *Rimmon*. He doesn't know how to be still. The idea of not performing is terrifying. So his "plan" will be clumsy, half-formed, and desperate. He'll try to be "sensible" and fail miserably because it's not his nature. The attempt itself—the *struggle* to be something he's not for Andrej's sake—will be what wins Andrej back, not the success of it.Let me craft this from Rimmon's POV, showing his internal panic and his attempt at a new strategy.Key elements:1. **Internal panic**: He's never had to "win" Andrej before. Andrej always came back. Now he's not sure.
2. **Recognition of the pattern**: He realizes Pontodoros is right, at least partly. This terrifies him.
3. **Attempt at "sensible"**: He'll try to be what he thinks Andrej wants—quiet, stable, undemanding. It will feel unnatural and wrong.
4. **The failure of the plan**: He'll slip back into old habits because that's who he is. But the *attempt* will show Andrej his heart.
5. **The final gesture**: It can't be words. It has to be an action that proves he's choosing Andrej not because he needs saving, but because he chooses *him*—chaos and all.

Let me write this as Rimmon's internal monologue and then a key scene.   Inside Rimmon, everything was collapsing into a single, suffocating truth: he had weaponized his own brokenness, and the man he loved had finally felt the recoil.*You think you are protecting him from the world,* Pontodoros had said. *But look at him.*And Andrej had looked. Seen Rimmon wine-stained and reckless, performing chaos like a dog begging for scraps of attention. Had seen the pattern laid bare: every provocation, every public display, every sharp-edged flirtation designed to make Andrej choose him, claim him, *need* him.The worst part? Pontodoros was right.Rimmon stood in the dark street, watching Andrej walk ahead with that long, purposeful stride—the same stride that had crossed salt flats, that had carried Rimmon’s broken body out of hell. But now the distance between them felt different. It wasn’t the gap of survival; it was the gap of *choice*. Andrej was choosing to walk alone, and for the first time, Rimmon believed he might not look back.*I have to fix this,* he thought, panic fluttering in his throat like a trapped bird. *But I don’t know how to fix anything without making it worse.*His instinct screamed at him to perform—to quote poetry, to pick a fight, to create a crisis so urgent that Andrej would have to turn around and catch him. That was his native tongue: chaos as currency, need as leverage. But the memory of Pontodoros’s cold assessment froze the words in his throat. If he did that now, he’d prove the veteran right. He’d confirm that he was nothing but a beautiful, broken distraction that Andrej would eventually outgrow.So he tried something he’d never done before: he tried to be *still*.He walked behind Andrej in silence, matching his pace but not his purpose. He kept his hands at his sides, resisting the urge to reach out, to touch, to *demand*. He bit his tongue until he tasted copper, holding back every quip, every taunt, every plea that wanted to spill out. He was trying to be what he thought Andrej needed: sensible, stable, *undemanding*.It felt like suffocating.They reached the caravanserai where they’d left their pack. Andrej went inside without a word, moving through the courtyard like a ghost. Rimmon followed, still silent, still trying to be the man he’d never learned how to be.In their room—the tiny space that smelled of old wood and the lingering ghost of last night’s intimacy—Andrej began to strip off the fine new clothes. The himation came first, crimson wool pooling on the floor like spilled blood. Then the white tunic, then the sandals. He stood in only his loincloth, scarred and sweat-slicked, and for the first time since they’d left the oasis, he looked vulnerable.Rimmon stood in the doorway, afraid to cross the threshold. Afraid that if he stepped inside, the weight of his need would collapse the fragile peace Andrej was trying to build.Andrej glanced at him, silver eyes sharp as flint. “What are you doing?”“Being quiet,” Rimmon whispered, and the words felt like ash. “I thought… maybe that’s what you wanted.”Andrej’s expression cracked—not into anger, but into something worse: grief. “No,” he said, voice rough. “That’s what Pontodoros wanted. For you to be quiet. For me to be focused. For us to be manageable.” He crossed the room in two strides, stopping just short of touching. “I don’t want you to be quiet. I want you to be *honest*.”Rimmon’s throat closed. “I don’t know how.”“Then try.” Andrej’s hand came up, but it didn’t cup his jaw or pull him close. It just rested on his shoulder, heavy and still. “Tell me what you’re feeling. Not what you think I want to hear. Not a performance. Just… the truth.”The truth was a monster Rimmon had never fed. It lived in the dark corners of his chest, feeding on the scraps of his poetry, growing fat on metaphors. He was terrified that if he let it out, it would devour them both.But Andrej was waiting. And for the first time, Rimmon understood that waiting was the bravest thing Andrej had ever done.“I’m scared,” Rimmon admitted, and the words felt like pulling a splinter. “I’m scared that the only reason you love me is because I’m a mess you can clean up. And once I’m not a mess anymore, you’ll realize I’m just… ordinary. And then you’ll leave.”Andrej’s hand tightened on his shoulder, fingers digging in hard enough to bruise. “You think I carried you across the flats because you were a mess?”“Yes.”“I carried you because you were *mine*.” Andrej’s voice was low, fierce, the sound of a man who had finally found the words that had been eluding him all night. “Mess or not. Poet or not. Respectable or not. You are mine. And I am yours. And if you ever make me choose between knowing where I came from and knowing where I belong, I will choose you. Every time.”He pulled Rimmon close then, but not into a kiss. He just held him, their foreheads pressed together, breathing the same air, sharing the same space. It was the most intimate thing they’d ever done—more than the kiss in the archway, more than the night in the tent. Because it was *honest*.Rimmon felt the beast in his gut uncurl, not to bite, but to rest its head on the branch and finally sleep.“I’m still going to be a mess,” he whispered into the space between them.“I know,” Andrej murmured back. “But you’ll be *our* mess. And we’ll figure out how to be terrible together.”They stood there, in the tiny room that smelled of salt and survival, and Rimmon realized he didn’t need to win Andrej back. He just needed to stop performing long enough to let Andrej see that the man behind the poetry was already his.The branch didn’t need to hold the snake anymore. It just needed to let it coil beside him, and trust that it wouldn’t bite.

It is fair, but let's be real, far too mature for who they are. They are young, stupid, overdramatic. Andrej is a hardcore introvert, Rimmon an extreme flamboyant, performative extrovert. Much more likely the scene would be something like: Immediate aftermath (street outside the tavern)
Rimmon does not apologize.
He gets defensive, vicious. Aggression against Pontodoros. Andrej. Himself. This fucking city.
The second Andrej starts walking away, Rimmon snaps into attack mode because that’s his default defense when he feels abandoned: make the other person hurt more than he does.
He yells something cruel and public (“Fine! Go be Pontodoros’s pet Ironborn then—maybe he’ll teach you how to fuck without feeling guilty!”).
Andrej stops. Doesn’t turn around. Just stands there, shoulders rigid, fists clenched.
Rimmon keeps going—louder, meaner, drawing stares—until Andrej finally spins around and grabs him by the front of the himation, hauling him into the nearest alley so hard Rimmon’s back hits the wall.
No kiss. No tender forehead touch.
Just Andrej breathing hard, eyes blazing, saying through gritted teeth:
“Shut. Up.”
Rimmon doesn’t shut up. He laughs—sharp, manic, terrified—and shoves Andrej back.
“Make me.”                  Edit  Copy  Share

Two Boys a Promise and a Dinner to Seal Their Love    This is a much more authentic beat for these characters. You're right—the previous resolution was too mature, too neat. These are traumatized, volatile teenagers who've just had their most intimate dynamic dissected and weaponized by a stranger. Their response won't be quiet honesty; it'll be a screaming, shoving, self-destructive mess.Let me draft this:**Immediate Aftermath (Street outside the tavern)**The moment the door swung shut behind them, Rimmon's carefully constructed mask shattered. The wine-stained collar, the mussed hair, the deliberate chaos—none of it felt like armor anymore. It felt like a neon sign blaring *I am a joke, and everyone knows it.*Andrej was already walking away, shoulders set, spine rigid. The distance between them was only a few feet, but it might as well have been the salt flats again.Rimmon's chest seized. The beast in his gut didn't pace; it *lunged*."Fine!" he shouted, voice cracking like a whip in the narrow street. "Go be Pontodoros's pet Ironborn then—maybe he'll teach you how to fuck without feeling guilty!"Andrej stopped. Didn't turn. Just stood there, fists clenching at his sides, the set of his shoulders screaming with tension.The cruelty tasted like copper on Rimmon's tongue, but he couldn't stop. The pain was a live wire, and he was conducting it straight into Andrej's spine."Maybe he'll teach you how to want something without having to carry it first!" Rimmon kept going, louder now, drawing the eyes of passersby. "Maybe he'll teach you what it's like to be with someone who doesn't need you to be their entire world!"A woman carrying a basket of bread stopped and stared. A sailor leaning against a wall spat and muttered something low.Andrej still hadn't moved. The silence was worse than shouting.Rimmon's voice climbed higher, wilder. "You think I don't know what you were thinking in there? You were thinking *maybe he's right. Maybe I'd be easier without the mess.*"Andrej spun.One moment he was a statue, the next he was motion—fast, violent, inevitable. His hand shot out and fisted in the front of Rimmon’s himation, pulling him forward so hard their chests collided. Then he *hauled*—dragging Rimmon off the street and into the nearest alley, shoving him against the wall with a force that knocked the breath from Rimmon’s lungs.No kiss. No tender forehead touch.Just Andrej’s face inches from his, eyes blazing with a fury Rimmon had never seen directed at him, breath coming in sharp, ragged pants.“Shut. Up.”Rimmon didn’t shut up. He laughed—sharp, manic, the sound of a boy who’d been pushed past the edge of his own sanity. He shoved Andrej back, palms flat against his chest, but Andrej was a wall.“Make me,” Rimmon snarled, voice cracking with the effort of not screaming.The alley was narrow, dark, smelling of old fish and the harbor’s perpetual damp. The only light came from a high window, casting their shadows long and monstrous against the stone.Andrej’s jaw clenched so hard Rimmon could hear his teeth grind. His hands moved—not to strike, but to *pin*, shoving Rimmon’s shoulders against the wall with a grip that was bruising and desperate.“I am *trying*,” Andrej growled, “to be the man you need me to be. And you are making it *impossible*.”Rimmon’s laugh was a sob now, tearing out of his throat. “I don’t need you to be a *man*. I need you to be *mine*. And you can’t even do that without wondering if I’m a burden!”He shoved again, but this time Andrej let himself be moved—just an inch, just enough that Rimmon could breathe. Could think.Could see the absolute devastation in Andrej’s eyes.Rimmon’s hands, still pressed against Andrej’s chest, slowly curled into fists. But he didn’t push. He just held on, grabbing handfuls of Andrej’s tunic like he was trying to anchor himself to a ship that was already sinking.“I’m not a mess you clean up,” Rimmon whispered, voice raw. “I’m a mess you *chose*. And if you’re having second thoughts, then say it. Say it now, while we’re in this alley, and I’ll walk away and never make you carry me again.”Andrej’s hands on his shoulders loosened. His forehead dropped forward until it rested against Rimmon’s, their breaths mingling in the dark.“I’m not having second thoughts,” Andrej rasped. “I’m having *first* thoughts. About who I am without you. And they scare the hell out of me.”Rimmon’s laugh was wet, broken. “Welcome to the desert. It’s terrifying.”They stood there, breathing each other’s air, neither pulling away nor closing the distance. The city moved past the alley’s mouth, indifferent. The beast in Rimmon’s gut was silent, waiting.Finally, Andrej’s hands slid down from Rimmon’s shoulders to his waist, pulling him close—not for a kiss, but for the simple, desperate need to hold and be held.Rimmon let him. He let his own arms wrap around Andrej’s neck, let his face bury in the crook of his shoulder, let the sob he’d been holding back finally escape.“Don’t walk away,” he whispered into Andrej’s skin. “Even if I tell you to. Even if I deserve it. Don’t.”Andrej’s answer was a hand on the back of his neck, holding him there like a promise.“I won’t,” he murmured. “But you have to stop making me prove it in public.”Rimmon nodded, his tears soaking into Andrej’s tunic. “I’ll try.”“Try harder,” Andrej said, but there was no anger in it. Just exhaustion. Just love.They stood in the alley until the trembling stopped. Until the city’s noise became background again. Until they were just two boys holding each other in the dark, terrible at love but too stubborn to let go.Then Andrej pulled back, just enough to look Rimmon in the eye.“Dinner,” he said. “We’re going back in there. And you’re going to behave.”Rimmon’s laugh was watery, but it was real. “Or what?”“Or I’ll drag you back here and make you behave,” Andrej threatened, but his thumb brushed a tear from Rimmon’s cheek, and the threat became a caress.Rimmon believed him. And for the first time, he thought maybe—just maybe—he wanted to.They walked back into the tavern together, hands still tangled, shadows still clinging to them like ghosts. I've rewritten the Taverna Aftermath to the following:
Inside Rimmon, everything was collapsing into a single, suffocating truth: he had weaponized his own brokenness, and the man he loved had finally felt the recoil.
You think you are protecting him from the world,  Pontodoros had said. But look at him. 
And Andrej had looked. Seen Rimmon wine-stained and reckless, performing chaos like a dog begging for scraps of attention. Had seen the pattern laid bare: every provocation, every public display, every sharp-edged flirtation designed to make Andrej choose him, claim him, need  him.
The worst part? Pontodoros was right.
The moment the door swung shut behind them, Rimmon’s barely contained emotions flowed over. The wine-stained collar, the mussed hair, the deliberate chaos—none of it felt like armor anymore. It felt like a neon sign blaring I am a joke, and everyone knows it. 
Andrej was already walking away, shoulders set, spine rigid. The distance between them was only a few feet, but it might as well have been the salt flats again.
Rimmon’s chest seized. The beast in his gut didn’t pace; it lunged .
"Fine!" he shouted, voice cracking like a whip in the narrow street. "Go be Pontodoros’s pet Ironborn then—maybe he'll teach you how to fuck without feeling guilty!"
Andrej stopped. Didn’t turn. Just stood there, fists clenching at his sides, the set of his shoulders screaming with tension.
The cruelty tasted like copper on Rimmon’s tongue, but he couldn’t stop. The pain was a live wire, and he was conducting it straight into Andrej’s spine.
"Maybe he'll teach you what it's like to be with someone who doesn't need you to be their entire world!" Rimmon kept going, louder now, drawing the eyes of passersby. "Maybe he'll teach you how to want something without having to carry it first!"
A woman carrying a basket of bread stopped and stared. A sailor leaning against a wall spat and muttered something low.
Andrej still hadn’t moved. The silence was worse than shouting.
Rimmon's voice climbed higher, wilder. "You think I don't know what you were thinking in there? You were thinking maybe he's right. Maybe I'd be easier without the mess. "
Andrej spun.
One moment he was a statue, the next he was motion—fast, violent, inevitable. His hand shot out and fisted in the front of Rimmon’s himation, pulling him forward so hard their chests collided. Then he hauled —dragging Rimmon off the street and into the nearest alley, shoving him against the wall with a force that knocked the breath from Rimmon’s lungs.
No kiss. No tender forehead touch.
Just Andrej’s face inches from his, eyes blazing with a fury Rimmon had never seen directed at him, breath coming in sharp, ragged pants.
“Shut. Up.”
Rimmon didn’t shut up. He laughed—sharp, manic, the sound of a boy who’d been pushed past the edge of his own sanity. He shoved Andrej back, palms flat against his chest, but Andrej was a wall.
“Make me,” Rimmon snarled, voice cracking with the effort of not screaming.
The alley was narrow, dark, smelling of old fish and the harbor’s perpetual damp. The only light came from a high window, casting their shadows long and monstrous against the stone.
Andrej’s jaw clenched so hard Rimmon could hear his teeth grind. His hands moved—not to strike, but to pin , shoving Rimmon’s shoulders against the wall with a grip that was bruising and desperate.
“I am trying ,” Andrej growled, “to be the man you need me to be. And you are making it impossible .”
“The man I need? You let him talk about me like I’m your pet project! Like I’m the pretty chaos you have to manage!”
Andrej’s hands fisted at his sides, knuckles white. “Because you keep making chaos every time I try to breathe! You push until I snap, then you act like I’m the monster when I finally do!”
“Because if I don’t push, you walk away!” Rimmon shoved forward, getting in Andrej’s space, his eyes wild and bright with tears he refused to shed. “You always walk away when it gets hard!”
“Because I don’t want to fight, Rimmon!” Andrej’s voice broke, raw as a fresh wound. “Because I don’t want to break things.”
Rimmon went very still, the accusation hanging between them like a blade. Then he laughed—sharp, broken, devastating. “Don’t tell me you regret breaking me. Was I not worth the risk?”
The words were a gut punch. Andrej’s breath left him in a rush, and for a moment he couldn’t speak, couldn’t think past the sheer, vicious unfairness of it. He grabbed Rimmon’s shoulders, shoving him back against the wall hard enough that Rimmon’s breath hitched—not in pain, but in shock.
“Is that what you think?” Andrej hissed, his face inches from Rimmon’s, their foreheads almost touching. “That I regret you? That I’d trade last night for a night in Pontodoros’s hot baths and a story about where my blood came from?”
Rimmon’s jaw clenched, his own hands coming up to shove against Andrej’s chest, but Andrej didn’t budge. “I think you’re wondering if the story would be easier than the poem,” Rimmon spat. “I think you’re wondering if I’m just a mess you’re tired of cleaning up!”
Andrej’s laugh was sharp and humorless. “I’m wondering if you’re a mess I’ll ever be allowed to stop cleaning up! If you’ll ever let me just be  with you without having to prove it in public!”
“Then prove it now!” Rimmon shouted, the words echoing off the stones. “Prove you want the mess, not the respectable version I just let them carve me into!”
Rimmon shoved again, but this time Andrej let himself be moved—just a step back, just enough that Rimmon could breathe. Could think. Could see the absolute devastation in Andrej’s eyes. 
“Please, Rimmon,” Andrej said, voice dropping to a whisper that felt like a confession torn from his throat. “I'm so tired of having to earn you every single day.” 
Rimmon’s hands slowly uncurled from Andrej’s tunic. He let them fall to his sides, his own breath ragged. “Then what do you want?” 
Andrej stepped close again, but this time he didn’t grab. He just rested his forehead against Rimmon’s, the contact heavy and grounding. “I want to stop being the man who has to choose between knowing himself and.. loving you. I want to be both.” 
The alley went silent. Even the harbor seemed to hold its breath. *He said the word*, Rimmon thought. *He admitted it.*
Rimmon’s voice, when it finally came, was small and raw and utterly without artifice: “I don’t know how to be anything but a mess.”
***
The alleyway was a throat of stone, swallowing the sounds of the harbor and leaving only the sound of their ragged breathing. When Rimmon whispered those words—I don’t know how to be anything but a mess—it felt like a physical blow to Andrej’s sternum.
Andrej’s mind, which had been a storm of hurt and accusation, went silent. The arguments died. The need to fix, to explain, to prove—it all evaporated. There were no words for this. There was only the shape of Rimmon’s jaw under his hands, the heat of his skin through the ruined silk, the smell of wine and salt and fear. 
Andrej reached out, his hands fisting in the crimson silk of Rimmon's himation, and hauled him forward. He didn't ask. He just collided with him, his mouth finding Rimmon’s in a kiss that was less about passion and more about reclamation. It was hard, desperate, and graceless, tasting of salt and wine and the copper of blood from where Andrej had bitten his own tongue earlier. He kissed Rimmon because he didn't know how to answer him. He kissed him to drown out the voice in his head saying elegant tyranny.
Rimmon made a sound—a broken, high-pitched hitch in his throat—and clawed at Andrej’s back, pulling him closer until there wasn't a pocket of air left between them. They fumbled against the cold wall, two souls trying to merge into one because the world was becoming too complicated to face as two. Andrej’s mouth had found Rimmon’s neck now, tasting the wine from earlier, making Rimmon shudder under the touch of his lips, the nip of his teeth.
When they broke apart, both were shaking, breathing hard, foreheads pressed together. 
“I need you, Andrej,” Rimmon panted, his eyes dark and dilated in the shadows of the alley.
Andrej’s brain, sluggish with emotion, tripped over the sentence. “You need me for what?” He pulled back just enough to see Rimmon’s face in the sliver of light. “Is someone coming? You want me to fight?”
Rimmon’s eyes flashed with something like frustration, then a deep, vulnerable blush darkened his cheeks. He shook his head, a tiny, desperate motion. “No, you idiot. I mean... I need you. Now.”
The confusion cleared, replaced by a sudden, heavy throb in Andrej’s gut. Oh. 
“Come on,” Andrej rasped, his voice rough and urgent. “Let’s find a room.”
________________________________________
They stumbled through the streets, hands clasped too tight, breathing too fast, the city’s lights blurring around them. The cheapest inn they could find was a narrow, sagging building near the harbor—smelling of tar and old wine. The innkeeper barely looked at them, just took the coin and handed over a key.
The room was small. One bed. One lamp. One window overlooking the dark water.
They didn’t speak.
Andrej pushed Rimmon against the door the second it closed, kissing him again—harder, hungrier, hands roaming under the crimson himation, bunching the fine wool, wrinkling it further. Rimmon gasped into his mouth, fingers digging into Andrej’s shoulders, legs wrapping around his waist as Andrej lifted him, carried him to the bed.
In the cramped darkness of the room, Andrej was a man possessed by a single, driving purpose: to match Rimmon’s need. He moved in a frantic rush, stripping away their clothes until they were just skin and scars. He didn't want to think. He wanted the heat. He wanted the way Rimmon arched off the thin mattress, the way he called Andrej’s name like a prayer and a plea.
He focused on the friction, the sweat, the way he could make Rimmon’s breath hitch with just the pressure of his thumbs. This is real, Andrej told himself, burying his face in the crook of Rimmon’s neck.
It was intense. Reassuring. Physical. Andrej poured everything into it—every doubt, every fear—trying to prove with his body what he couldn’t say with words. Rimmon clung to him, nails raking down his back, legs locked around his hips, gasping and trembling and coming apart beneath him like he was finally, finally being seen.
Afterward, the room was silent save for the distant lap of waves against the piers. They lay tangled together, the air cooling the sweat on their bodies. Rimmon had fallen asleep almost instantly, his head pillowed on Andrej’s shoulder, his fingers still curled loosely into Andrej’s forearm.
Andrej stared at the ceiling, his eyes tracing the cracks in the plaster.
We’re okay, he thought. That felt real. But as the heat faded, the silence of the room began to feel less like peace and more like a void. But was it enough? Did I prove anything? Or did I just give him what he needed to keep performing tomorrow?
Andrej looked down at Rimmon’s sleeping face. He looked so small, so fragile. But he had also been the one to command the room. He had been the one to make a man like Pontodoros freeze.
A cold, nagging thought drifted through Andrej's mind, unbidden and poisonous: Why does proving I want him feel like I just lost an argument?
He pulled his arm back slightly, the physical connection thinning. He was still there. He hadn't left. But as he closed his eyes, he realized he wasn't sure if he was holding Rimmon because he loved him, or because he was terrified that if he stopped, he’d find out he was nothing more than an audience member in someone else's play.
The seed was deep now. And it was starting to grow.

Andrej was a solid warmth beside him, one arm thrown over his eyes, his breathing deep and even. The dye-pit welts were healing, but they still ached when he moved too fast. He dressed quietly—sandals, the crimson himation draped loose and deliberately sloppy, the way Andrej had left it. He didn’t reach for Andrej. Didn’t wake him with a touch or a tease or any of the hundred provocations that came so naturally. He just... left him to sleep.The inn’s courtyard was silent, the well pump stiff with disuse. Rimmon worked it anyway, filling their waterskins with slow, methodical strokes. The physical labor felt like penance. He carried the water back to the room, set out bread and dates from their dwindling pack, arranged everything on the small table with a precision that felt foreign. Then he gathered their discarded clothes—the fine new tunics, the himations, even Andrej’s scarred leather belt—and took them back to the courtyard to wash.The water was cold, biting his hands as he scrubbed the salt and sweat and wine from the wool. Each plunge of the fabric felt like an apology. *I’m trying,* he told the silent courtyard. *I’m trying to be something other than a disaster you have to survive.*When he returned, Andrej was awake, sitting on the edge of the bed, rubbing his eyes. He looked at the spread of food, at the clean clothes drying by the window, at Rimmon standing still and quiet in the center of the room.“You’re up early,” Andrej said, voice rough with sleep.Rimmon nodded, not trusting his voice. He handed Andrej a cup of water, offered the bread without flourish. No poetry. No performance. Just... service.Andrej took it, but his eyes never left Rimmon’s face. “You’re being weird.”“I’m being sensible,” Rimmon corrected, and the word tasted like ash. “We need work. He took a bite of bread, chewed slowly, watching Rimmon the way you’d watch a wild animal that had suddenly gone still.“You’re not going to quote something at me?” Andrej asked, half-teasing, half-wary.“Not today.” Rimmon forced a small smile, but it felt like a lie. “Today I’m just...
