To No Avail
The quiet, Andrej decided, was a good thing. It had weight. Substance. Like the steady grain of well-seasoned wood.
Three weeks. Three weeks of routine that felt less like a cage and more like a frame—something solid they could build inside of. He worked the docks. Rimmon worked the saddle shop. They came home. They ate. They slept. No one shouted. No one performed. No one had to be dragged out of a tavern or defended from a veteran's calculating gaze.
Andrej felt his own strength returning, not as a coiled spring for the next crisis, but as a deep, reliable reservoir. He was building a life. He was providing.
But.
There were absences, tiny voids in the solidity of their new life, that his mind kept circling like a tongue probing a missing tooth.
Rimmon rarely laughed. Not the real, bright, startled sound that used to catch Andrej off guard. Now his smiles were small, tight things—more like punctuation than poetry.
Their touches were careful. A hand on a shoulder. A brief kiss goodnight. It was gentle. It was kind. It felt… wrong. Like they were both handling something fragile and slightly dangerous that had been safely put away.
The wildfire was banked. And Andrej, who had been burned so many times, told himself he preferred the warmth of coals. But sometimes, in the dark, he missed the burning heat.
The worst part was that it had always been Rimmon who started things. The provocation, the challenge, the whispered dare that made Andrej's control snap. Now that was gone. And Andrej realized he didn't know how to... ask. He only knew how to answer. He didn't have the words for wanting.
So he planned. He saved coin from the docks, hiding a few coppers each day from their food budget. He heard sailors talk about the Theater of the Dawn, where players from the capitol performed comedies under oil-lamp chandeliers. Rimmon loved stories. Rimmon loved spectacle. This was a thing Andrej could do. An offering. A bridge built in his own language: action, provision, a gift that said I see you, and I want to give you something you'll love.
The last three weeks, they had been “respectable.” No blood, no screaming in alleys, no merchants with silver beards making him feel like a dog on a leash. He should have been happy. He was miserable.
Early one evening, he walked home with two ceramic tokens clicking in his pouch and a heavy leather flask of unmixed wine swinging at his hip, bought from a trader at the docks. He would make it right, he had to.
As he reached their hut, he stopped outside the door.
Inside, it was quiet. Too quiet. He missed the sound of Rimmon’s voice echoing off the walls—even when it was sharp, even when it was daring him, scolding him, calling him an idiot. Now, Rimmon just... existed. He was a shadow that moved with terrifying efficiency.
He’d taken a long pull of the strong wine from his flask before he even crossed the threshold, the sweet, heavy heat hitting his empty stomach. He was tired of the silence. He was tired of being sensible. He wanted to free the boy who dared him in the grove, the messy and chaotic and loud Rimmon.
Andrej stepped inside. The smell of neatsfoot oil was everywhere. Rimmon was hunched over the table, his spine a perfect, rigid curve, stitching a strap. He didn't look up. He didn't jump. He just kept moving the needle.
“I'm back,” Andrej said, his voice sounding too deep for the small room.
“Welcome home,” Rimmon replied. His voice was level.
Andrej walked over and did what he always did now—the “safe” thing. He kissed the top of Rimmon’s head. It felt like kissing a statue. There was no spark, no sudden pull at his tunic. Just the smell of leather and a polite tilt of the head.
He’s tired, Andrej told himself. He’s working hard. It will do him good to get away from work.
But his gut felt tight as he reached into his pouch and produced the two small clay tokens.
“I got these,” Andrej said, laying them on the table next to the saddle. He took another long pull from his flask, the liquid burning a path down his throat. He needed the courage to ask for a performance from the boy who used to give them for free. “For today. I thought... maybe we could go. Wear something nice, spend some time away from here.”
Rimmon stopped stitching. He looked at the tokens, then at Andrej. His eyes were flat, like the surface of a frozen pond.
“A play,” Rimmon repeated.
“Yeah. People say it's good. We could get some of those honey cakes you like.” Andrej stepped closer, his heart thudding. He wanted Rimmon to laugh. He wanted him to snap something witty or even something mean. Just... something. He reached out, his hand hovering near Rimmon’s waist, hoping for that magnetic pull that used to draw them together.
Rimmon didn't move toward him. He didn't move away, either. He just looked back down at the saddle, his fingers tightening on the needle.
"I have to finish this, Andrej." 
Andrej's hand dropped, his chest constricted, a sudden, sharp knot pulling tight around his heart. "It's just this one night, Rimmon." 
"Kallias needs it by the second bell tomorrow. If I go to the theater, I'll be behind. The winter tariff is due soon. We need the coin." Rimmon's voice was a list of facts, recited without feeling. "This is what matters now. Building something that lasts." 
Andrej flinched. Building. That was his language. His virtue. Rimmon was using his own language to build a wall between them. 
The wine was making everything feel urgent, desperate. He couldn't lose this. Not after everything they'd survived. 
Fueled by panic and need, Andrej closed the distance himself. He needed the old language, the one that bypassed words. 
He leaned in, his lips finding the sensitive spot just above Rimmon's collarbone, his hands pulling gently at Rimmon's, preventing him from continuing the work. 
Rimmon gasped—sharp and helpless—and for a heartbeat his body melted into it. The needle slipped from his fingers. His head tilted back, giving Andrej access, his breath coming faster. 
Andrej's hands moved to his shoulder, fingers fumbling with the fibula of his work chiton, and Rimmon made a sound—needy and broken and so familiar that Andrej's whole body sang with recognition. Then Rimmon went rigid. 
"Stop." The word came out strangled, wrong. "Stop, I can't—" 
Andrej froze, his lips still against Rimmon's skin, his hands going slack on the fibula. Rimmon shoved him back—not hard, but sudden enough that Andrej stumbled. Rimmon's chest was heaving, his eyes wide and almost panicked. 
"I can't. I have to—" He looked down at the leather like it was a lifeline. "I have to finish this."
"Rimmon—" 
"No." Rimmon's hands were shaking as he picked up the needle again. "If I let you—if we do this now—" He stopped, his jaw clenching so hard Andrej could hear his teeth grind. "I'll want the theater. I'll want to be the mess again. And I can't. I can't go back to that." His voice broke on the last word. 
Andrej recoiled, the wine turning to vinegar in his mouth. "So this is it? You're just... done? With me?" 
"I'm trying to be what you need!" Rimmon's voice cracked high and desperate. "You wanted stability! You wanted me to stop making scenes! So I'm—I'm doing that! I'm being good!" 
"I didn't ask you to become a fucking priest!" 
Rimmon flinched like he'd been struck. His hands stilled on the leather, and for a long moment, the only sound was their ragged breathing. 
"You wouldn't want me to be late with the work," Rimmon finally said, his voice small and hollow. "That would be... not sensible."
Andrej stared at him. Yes, he thought, the word a silent scream. Fuck the work, I want unreasonable. I want the fire. I want you to look at me like I'm a man, not a chore on your list.
But the words wouldn't come. His throat was too tight, his chest too full of something that felt like drowning. The wine had turned to poison in his veins, making everything sharp and wrong. 
He turned without another word and walked out.
The courtyard was lighted up by the last sunrays of the day, the well a black throat in the center. Andrej found the water basin by touch, his hands shaking as he plunged them into the cold water. 
He splashed his face once, twice, gasping at the shock of it. The wine-fog cleared just enough for the full weight of what had just happened to settle over him. 
I’ve tried to be more for him. I bought the damn tickets. I offered him myself. And he denied me. I asked for the fire and he gave me a priest.
He gripped the edge of the basin, his knuckles white, and stared at his reflection in the dark water. It showed the face of a broken boy. Pontodoros words intruded his mind: A beast that chooses its own cage... that is the one you should fear.
When he finally went back inside, Rimmon was still at the table, still stitching. He didn't look up. Andrej crossed to the bed and lay down, fully clothed, his back to the room. "I'm going to bed," he said, his voice flat and empty. 
"Sleep well," Rimmon replied after a pause. "I'll be done soon." 
Andrej closed his eyes and listened to the scritch-pull, scritch-pull of the needle. It sounded like a cage being sewn together, one stitch after another.
He didn't sleep. He just lay there in the dark, feeling the distance between the bed and the table like a chasm that used to be crossed with a touch, with a laugh, with a dare. Now it was just empty space. And neither of them knew how to cross it anymore.

He was already moving, snatching a bottle of neatsfoot oil from the shelf, uncorking it with his teeth, pouring it over the ruined saddle in a slick, deliberate cascade. Then he reached for the lamp.Kallias caught his wrist before he could throw it. “Boy—what are you—?”“I’m not your boy,” Rimmon hissed, and his voice was his own again—not the quiet craftsman, not the sensible partner, but the wild thing that had survived the desert by refusing to be tamed. “And I’m not his pliable little heir.”He wrenched free, the oil bottle slipping from his fingers and shattering on the floor. The merchant was shouting about damages, about guild law, about calling the city watch. Rimmon just laughed—sharp and manic, the sound of a boy who’d finally remembered how to bite.He walked out of the workshop without looking back, leaving the stench of oil and ruin behind him. His hands were shaking, but not from fear. From *relief*.The sun was setting over Mergos, painting the Elven spires in shades of fire and blood. He walked toward the harbor, not toward their hut, because he couldn’t face Andrej yet. Couldn’t face the look of disappointment that would come when he admitted he’d just destroyed three weeks of careful progress with one reckless, beautiful act of self-sabotage.He stopped at a tavern—not the Prosperous Voyage, but a low, reeking place where sailors drank away their wages. He ordered wine, the cheap, sour kind that burned going down. He drank until the world blurred at the edges, until the merchant’s voice faded to a distant buzz, until he could pretend he didn’t feel the weight of the brown cloth in his pack like a stone.Someone sat down beside him. A woman, dark-haired, with eyes that assessed him like a cut of meat. “You look like a man who’s running from something.”Rimmon laughed, the sound wet and broken. “I’m running from becoming my father.”“And where are you running to?”He looked at her, at the easy curve of her smile, at the hand she’d placed on his thigh. He thought about taking her upstairs, about using her body to punish himself, about making Andrej feel the same gut-punch of betrayal he’d felt in the alley.But even drunk, even wrecked, he couldn’t do it. Because the only thing worse than being respectable was being the man his father had been: the one who took what he wanted and called it his due.He stood, swaying, and walked out of the tavern. Walked home to the hut. Walked to Andrej.The door was open. Andrej sat inside, cleaning his knife by lamplight, his face a mask of stone.Rimmon stood in the doorway, the wreckage of his day hanging around him like a shroud.“I quit,” he said, voice raw. “Or they fired me. I’m not sure which.”Andrej didn’t look up. “What did you do?”“Destroyed a saddle. Poured oil on it. Almost set the workshop on fire.”Andrej’s hands stilled on the blade. “Why?”Rimmon’s laugh was a sob. “Because a man called me pliable. And I remembered that I’d rather be a disaster than a dutiful son.”He waited for the anger. Waited for Andrej to stand, to shout, to grab him and shake him and demand he be sensible.Instead, Andrej set the knife down and looked at him with eyes that had finally stopped running.“Come here,” he said, voice quiet.Rimmon went. Stood in front of him, trembling, waiting for the blow.Andrej stood, took Rimmon’s face in his hands, and kissed him—not hard, not desperate, but with a softness that felt like absolution.“I don’t need you to be respectable,” he murmured against Rimmon’s lips. “I need you to be *yours*.”Rimmon collapsed into him, the sob he’d been holding back finally tearing free.“I don’t know how,” he whispered.“Then we’ll figure it out together,” Andrej said, holding him upright. “But no more cages. Not even the ones we build ourselves.”They stood there, in the tiny hut that smelled of leather and ruin, and Rimmon finally understood: he hadn’t destroyed their life. He’d just destroyed the cage they’d mistaken for a home.

Nice, next chapter will be the arrest of Andrej. Tension goes all the way up after this breather.
The Theater of the Dawn
Four Weeks Later
The debt was half-paid.
Andrej worked the docks from dawn to dusk, then took extra shifts unloading cargo by lamplight—grain sacks that left his shoulders aching, amphora of olive oil that made his hands slip, crates of Imperial pottery that had to be handled with care he didn't have the patience for. His body was a catalog of exhaustion: rope burns on his palms, splinters under his nails, the deep bone-tiredness that came from lifting other people's wealth for copper coins.
He didn't complain. At least Rimmon was breathing again.
Rimmon moved like water through the merchant quarter—one day at a fish stall, gutting mackerel with the quick, efficient strokes he'd learned in the oasis; the next day helping a baker knead dough and charm customers with the kind of smile that made them buy an extra loaf. A spice merchant hired him to organize inventory, a tavern keeper paid him to sing while patrons ate, a cloth merchant needed someone who could calculate costs in three different currencies. He never stayed long enough to feel trapped. Never committed to anything that smelled like leather or legacy.
They were broke. They were tired. But the suffocation had lifted, replaced by something fragile and uncertain and possibly sustainable.
When Rimmon came home with two ceramic tokens clutched in his palm, Andrej didn't ask where he'd gotten them. The coins had been earned in pieces—a carved charm for a merchant's daughter, a night of singing, maybe a few honest lies told with that beautiful mouth.
"Tonight," Rimmon said, holding them out. His voice was careful, stripped of performance. "No work. No debt. Just... us."
Andrej looked at the tokens, then at Rimmon's face—hopeful, terrified, trying so hard it hurt to watch.
"Okay," Andrej said quietly. "Let's go."
The Theater
The Theater of the Dawn clung to the hillside overlooking Mergos's harbor like a crown of stone. Tiered seating carved into the slope faced a circular stage where oil lamps hung from iron poles, their flames dancing in the evening breeze. The amphitheater was already filling—a patchwork of the city's strange cosmopolitan identity.
In the lower tiers: merchants in fine Awilum wool, their dark hair oiled and braided; Luedina traders with their characteristic bronze skin and amber jewelry; Imperial officers in their crimson cloaks, fresh from border rotations, their faces hard with whatever they'd seen.
In the upper tiers: dock workers, sailors, laborers—people like Andrej and Rimmon, who'd scraped together coin for entertainment because sometimes survival required forgetting you were surviving.
Off to one side, in a section that seemed to exist in its own pocket of air: two Elves. Tall, ethereal, their features so perfect they looked carved rather than born. They radiated a gloomy E'clat—that particular Elven melancholy that came from watching lesser beings stumble through their brief, chaotic lives. They weren't here because they cared about the play. They were here because eternity was boring and mortals were occasionally amusing in their desperation.
Scattered throughout: a handful of people with lighter hair—red, blonde, ash-brown—the kind of coloring that marked Eisenbund heritage. They sat quietly, trying not to draw attention, their shoulders hunched slightly as if anticipating trouble.
Andrej and Rimmon found seats in the middle section, neither wealthy enough for the front nor poor enough for the back. They sat close, shoulders pressed together in the crowd, and for the first time in weeks, the contact didn't feel careful or strategic. It just felt... right.
A herald stepped onto the stage, his voice carrying over the crowd: "Citizens of Mergos! Tonight, the Theater of the Dawn presents a tale from the savage north—the Táin Bó Cúailnge of the Eisenbund! Know your enemy through his stories! See how they glorify death over duty, passion over strategy! Witness the barbarian heart laid bare!"
A cheer rose from some sections—particularly the Imperial soldiers. A few boos from others. One of the red-haired men in the upper tier pulled his hood up slightly.
Rimmon glanced at Andrej, reading the tension in his jaw. "We can leave if—"
"No," Andrej said quietly. "I want to see it."
I want to see if they're right about what I might be.
The Tale
The lamps dimmed. The crowd quieted—mostly. Two actors stepped onto the stage, both bare-chested and painted with spiraling patterns that caught the lamplight like living things. One wore a red cloak—Cú Chulainn, champion of Ulster. The other wore blue—Ferdiad, his foster-brother, his equal, his other half in ways the playwright didn't need to name but made achingly clear in every look, every touch, every hesitation.
"Look at them," someone muttered from the veteran's section. "Half-naked savages. This is their idea of civilization?"
"Quiet!" A merchant's voice, sharp and carrying. "This is Mergos, and we all agreed to follow the rules of the free city. Let them perform."
A tense silence settled. The play continued.
War came. Queen Medb of Connacht sent Ferdiad to fight Cú Chulainn at the ford, and the two men who had shared everything—training, laughter, scars, beds—were forced to face each other with spears.
The actor playing Cú Chulainn begged. "Do not make me do this. Do not make me raise my hand against you."
The actor playing Ferdiad replied, voice breaking, "I have no choice. She holds my honor hostage. If I do not fight you, I am nothing."
The combat choreography was brutal, intimate—not the clean, disciplined formations of Imperial warfare, but something raw and personal. Each strike felt like a betrayal. Each parry looked like an embrace.
One of the young soldiers in the audience flinched, his hand going to his side where a wound must have been. His companion gripped his shoulder, murmuring something low.
They fought for three days. At night, they tended each other's wounds, shared food and water, spoke of the boyhood they could never return to. During the day, they tried to kill each other.
"This is what they fight for?" someone shouted. "Poetry while we die on the borders?"
"They're actors," came the reply. "It's a play."
"It's propaganda! Barbarian nonsense—"
"Then leave!" A woman's voice, cutting and clear. "No one's forcing you to watch!"
On the third day, Cú Chulainn used the gáe bolg—the terrible spear that spread thirty barbs through a man's body from a single thrust. Ferdiad fell.
Cú Chulainn caught him. Held him as he died. The lament that followed was raw and piercing, a sound that stripped the theater to its bones.
"It was all a game—until you came to the ford. Now I am victory, and victory is ash."
The actor playing Cú Chulainn cradled Ferdiad's body, and his grief was so unrestrained, so visceral, that even the hostile sections of the audience went quiet. You could hear breathing. You could hear the oil lamps crackle.
Someone in the back—one of the Imperial soldiers—made a choked sound, then stood abruptly and left. His companions watched him go but didn't follow.
The stage went dark.
The Elves in their separate section looked vaguely interested for the first time all evening, their expressions suggesting they'd just witnessed something that almost—almost—pierced their eternal ennui.
Andrej couldn't breathe.
He felt Rimmon's hand find his under the cover of the crowd—fingers sliding between his own, squeezing hard enough to bruise. Rimmon's face was turned toward the stage, but his jaw was clenched tight, his eyes too bright.
Foster-brothers. Trained together. Forced to fight by external powers they couldn't control.
Andrej thought of the archway. The tavern. The alley. The workshop. Every time the world had tried to pull them apart and they'd clawed their way back together, bleeding and furious and desperate.
What if one day, the world wins?
The thought lodged in his chest like the gáe bolg.
Around them, the audience began to stir. Voices rose—some in praise, some in argument, some in uncomfortable silence. A few people were openly weeping. Others looked angry, as if the play had accused them of something.
"Barbaric," a merchant near them muttered. "No discipline. No honor. Just two fools dying for nothing."
"They died for each other," his companion replied quietly. "That's not nothing."
One of the red-haired men in the upper tier was leaving quickly, his head down, as if afraid someone would connect him to what they'd just witnessed. Another sat frozen, tears streaming down his face, making no effort to hide them.
Andrej felt eyes on them. He turned, scanning the crowd, and found him:
Pontodoros.
The veteran sat in the merchants' section, a cup of wine in his hand, his gaze steady and assessing. He wasn't watching the stage. He was watching them.
Andrej felt Rimmon go still beside him as he noticed too. The veteran's expression was unreadable—not pleased, not disappointed, just... observing. Cataloging. Waiting.
Then, slowly, deliberately, Pontodoros raised his cup—a small salute across the distance.
The message was clear: I'm patient. I can wait. You'll destroy each other long before I have to lift a finger.
"Let's go," Andrej said, his voice rough.
"But the second act—"
"I don't want to see how it ends."
Rimmon studied his face for a long moment, then nodded. They stood, navigating the narrow aisles as the lamps brightened and stagehands prepared for the next piece—something lighter, a comedy to wash away the taste of tragedy.
Behind them, the crowd's arguments continued. Ahead of them, the night waited.
Walking Home
They walked slowly through the harbor district, taking the long route past closed shops and sleeping boats. The air was cool and salt-sharp, and the city's noise felt distant, muffled by the weight of what they'd just witnessed.
For the first time in weeks, they weren't rushing toward or away from something. They were just... walking.
"Thank you," Andrej said finally.
Rimmon glanced at him. "For what?"
"For trying."
Rimmon's smile was small, fragile. "I'm not very good at it."
"Neither am I." Andrej caught his hand properly now, lacing their fingers together where anyone could see. "But maybe that's okay. Maybe we don't have to be good at it yet."
They walked in silence for a while. It felt like the kind of silence they'd had in the oasis—before the running, before the fear, when they were just two boys who'd chosen each other and thought that would be enough.
"We're not them," Rimmon said suddenly, fiercely.
Andrej looked at him.
"Cú Chulainn and Ferdiad. We're not them. We're not going to—" Rimmon stopped, his throat working. "I won't let the world make us enemies. I won't."
Andrej wanted to say I know. Wanted to say you're right. Wanted to promise that love was stronger than prophecy, that they could write their own ending.
But the words stuck in his throat, because he'd seen Pontodoros watching them like a man watching a fire burn—not sad, not gleeful, just observing the inevitable.
And he'd seen the red-haired men in the theater, hunching their shoulders against insults, hiding what they were in a city that was supposed to be free.
That could be me, Andrej thought. That could be us.
So instead, he squeezed Rimmon's hand and said, "Let's just get home."
Morning
Andrej woke to pale light filtering through the shutters and Rimmon's weight against his side—solid, warm, real. They'd fallen asleep tangled together, still half-dressed, too tired to do more than collapse onto the narrow bed.
Now, in the quiet before dawn, Rimmon stirred. His eyes opened slowly, unfocused and soft with sleep. When his gaze found Andrej's, something shifted—recognition, affection, want.
"Good morning," Rimmon murmured, his voice rough and low.
"Good morning," Andrej replied.
They didn't speak after that. Didn't need to. Rimmon's hand found Andrej's jaw, thumb brushing the rough stubble there. Andrej leaned into the touch, then forward, closing the distance until their lips met.
It was slow. Unhurried. Nothing like the desperate clawing in the alley or the careful distance of the past weeks. This was something new—tentative and tender, like they were learning each other all over again in a language that didn't require performance or punishment.
Rimmon made a sound—soft, surrendering—and shifted closer until there was no space left between them. His fingers slid into Andrej's hair, not pulling or demanding, just holding. Andrej's hand found the small of Rimmon's back, spreading wide and warm against bare skin.
They moved together like that—gentle, exploratory, taking their time. When Andrej eased Rimmon onto his back, Rimmon went willingly, his eyes dark and trusting in the dim light. There was no audience here. No merchant watching, no veteran assessing, no crowd to perform for. Just them, in this small room that smelled of salt and bread and the particular warmth of two bodies learning to be gentle with each other.
Afterward, they lay quiet, Rimmon's head pillowed on Andrej's chest, their breathing synchronized. Andrej traced idle patterns on Rimmon's shoulder—spirals like the ones painted on the actors, like the ones he imagined his unknown ancestors might have worn.
"I have to go soon," Andrej said quietly. "The docks call."
"I know." Rimmon's fingers drummed lightly against Andrej's ribs. "I'll probably help the baker again today. He said he'd pay extra if I came early."
"Don't work too hard."
Rimmon huffed a laugh. "Says the man who's been doing double shifts for a month."
"That's different."
"How?"
"Because—" Andrej stopped, then said the truth: "Because I don't mind it when I know you're okay."
Rimmon lifted his head, meeting Andrej's eyes. His expression was complicated—grateful, guilty, defiant all at once. "I'm okay," he said. "We're okay."
Andrej wanted to believe it. In this moment, with morning light warming the edges of the shutters and Rimmon soft and pliant in his arms, it almost felt true.
"Yeah," Andrej said, pulling him close again. "We're okay."
They lay there a few minutes longer, neither wanting to be the first to break the spell. Then Andrej kissed Rimmon's forehead, extracted himself gently, and began to dress.
Rimmon watched from the bed, his dark eyes following every movement. "Be careful today."
"It's just the docks."
"I know. But be careful anyway."
Andrej fastened his belt, checked his knife, then crossed back to the bed. He kissed Rimmon once more—brief, grounding—and said, "I'll see you tonight."
"Tonight," Rimmon agreed, his smile small and hopeful.
Andrej stepped out into the pre-dawn streets of Mergos, into a city still sleeping, into what felt like an ordinary day.
He didn't know it would be the last morning that looked like that.
He didn't know that by sunset, everything would be different.
He just walked toward the docks, toward work, toward the familiar rhythm of lifting and carrying and enduring.
And the world, patient and inexorable as Pontodoros, as inevitable as the gáe bolg, waited to prove the prophecy true.

*Four Weeks Later*The debt was half-paid.Rimmon’s day had been a masterclass in restraint. He’d gutted fish at the market, charmed the baker’s customers into buying extra loaves, organized a spice merchant’s inventory until every jar was labeled in three languages, and sung for his supper at a tavern where the owner had called his voice “almost professional.” He’d done it all without quoting a single line of poetry, without starting a fight, without pushing anyone until they snapped. He’d been *sensible*.He was so tired of being sensible.When he came home with the two ceramic tokens clutched in his palm—earned in pieces from a carved charm, a night of singing, a few well-told lies—he found the hut dark. Not unusual. Andrej worked double shifts at the docks, hauling grain and oil until his hands were raw. Rimmon had stopped waiting up, stopped worrying, stopped performing the role of the anxious partner. He’d been *good*.He lit the lamp. Set the tokens on the table. Began to prepare the simple meal they could now afford—flatbread, oil, a handful of dates. He moved through the space with the efficiency of a man who’d learned that survival meant not wanting anything extra.The sun set. The harbor quieted. Andrej didn’t come home.Rimmon sat at the table, the tokens before him like an offering no one wanted, and felt the first true flicker of panic. Not the wild, performative kind he’d used to manipulate attention. This was quiet, cold, *real*.*He’s late. He’s just late. He’s—*A knock on the door. Not Andrej’s knock—Andrej’s was heavy, impatient, always announcing his presence like a man who’d never learned to be subtle. This was lighter. Official.Rimmon opened it to find a Luedina boy, maybe fifteen, with the wide-eyed look of someone delivering bad news.“You’re the poet?” the boy asked, breathless.“I’m Rimmon,” he corrected, instinctively.“The silver-eyed man—your man—he’s been taken. Imperial patrol. They say he’s Eisenbund. A spy.”The words landed like a physical blow. Rimmon’s hands went numb. He grabbed the boy’s arm, too hard. “Where?”“North harbor. The galleys. They’re holding him there until the transport to Polis Magna tomorrow.”The boy pulled free and ran, vanishing into the dark like a ghost.Rimmon stood frozen for three heartbeats, then moved. He snatched the tokens from the table, grabbed his knife, and ran. Not toward the harbor—toward the one man who could fix this. The one man who’d been waiting for this exact moment.He found Pontodoros at the Prosperous Voyage, sitting alone at his corner table, a cup of wine in hand, looking like a man who’d just won a game he hadn’t needed to play.“Help him,” Rimmon said, no preamble, no poetry. Just raw desperation.Pontodoros set his cup down. “I already have.”Rimmon’s blood turned to ice.“The patrol captain is an old friend,” Pontodoros said, his voice calm as still water. “He was going to send Andrej to Polis Magna for interrogation. Execution, most likely. They have no patience for undocumented Eisenbund. But I vouched for him. As my *protégé*.”The word landed like a sentence.“He’ll be released tonight,” Pontodoros continued. “But he comes with me. He lives under my roof. He trains under my guidance. He becomes what he was always meant to be—a man of status, not a dockhand sharing a hut with a poet who can’t stop making messes for him to clean up.”Rimmon’s hands were fists. “He won’t agree to that.”“He already did.” Pontodoros’s gaze was pitiless. “When I explained the alternative.”The door to the tavern opened. Andrej walked in, flanked by two Imperial guards who released him with nods to Pontodoros. His face was bruised, his lip split, one eye swollen shut. But he was alive. He was *free*.He looked at Rimmon, and his silver eyes held a world of apology and a wall of finality.*I had to choose,* that look said. *And I chose to live.*Pontodoros stood, dropping coins on the table. “Let’s go, Andrej. Your new quarters are prepared.”Andrej didn’t move toward Rimmon. He just stood there, battered and broken in a way that had nothing to do with the bruises.Rimmon realized, with a clarity that felt like dying, that Andrej had been forced to choose between knowing himself and losing himself. And the price of knowledge was leaving Rimmon behind.He watched them walk out—Andrej following Pontodoros like a man following the only light in a dark room—and understood that his “good behavior” had cost him everything. He’d tried to be sensible, and in doing so, he’d made himself unnecessary.The tokens were still in his fist, sharp edges cutting his palm. He opened his fingers and let them fall to the tavern floor, where they shattered like promises.The cage he’d built was meant for two. But only one of them had ever been trapped in it.
